### [1] [Intuition with Evidence: Adaptive Dual Memory for Multimodal Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33019)
*Qiuhe Hong,Jingya Wang,Cuzhen Geng,Shenyao Peng,Munan Ning,Yuyang Liu,Yonghong Tian*

Abstract: As multimodal agents engage in sustained interactions, long-term memory serves two distinct but complementary functions: fine-grained evidence recall and internalized interpretation for global understanding. Although episodic and latent memory address these demands through explicit historical records and internalized experience, neither paradigm alone can accommodate the diverse memory affinities across tasks, owing to limitations in episodic evidence coverage and integration, and in latent factual grounding. To bridge this divide, we propose a dual-memory framework, unifying a model-native latent memory as an intuitive prior with a multi-indexed episodic memory for exact evidence reconstruction via retrieval-conditioned joint alignment. At inference, the framework adaptively composes these memories through a training-free routing mechanism driven by Jensen-Shannon Divergence (JSD) sensitivity and confidence proxies for query-dependent memory affinity. Extensive evaluations across multimodal benchmarks demonstrate state-of-the-art performance, outperforming single-paradigm baselines overall (+4.67\% over vanilla Episodic), with further analysis highlighting episodic strengths in evidence-localized tasks and latent contributions to integrating dispersed information and compensating for imperfect retrieval.

</details>

Abstract: We introduce Nemotron 3.5 Bolt 74B-A9B 24GB, a hybrid Mamba-Transformer vision-language model that optimizes speed and intelligence per GB of memory used for local agentic workloads. Derived from the Nemotron 3.5 Super VL 120B-A12B model family, Nemotron 3.5 Bolt 74B-A9B 24GB combines Star Elastic compression with 2-bit Quantization-Aware Distillation for memory-constrained deployment. The model is optimized for reasoning, agentic tool use, and web & terminal interactions. We also trained a LiLiCorr speculative drafter that is released alongside the model to reach our target inference speed. The resulting model achieves a higher accuracy than leading models with similar memory footprints and speed across many benchmarks for general agentic capabilities (e.g., GDPVal v2), web browsing (e.g., BrowseComp), and terminal use (e.g., Terminal-Bench 2.1), with a relatively high TPS of 84 on RTX Spark that is comparable to models with much lower active parameter counts and more than sufficient for long-horizon agentic applications.

*Weilin Ruan,Yingjie Xu,Yangkai Wei,Bohan Zhou,Shuaibo Li,Harold Haodong Chen,Kaiwen Zhou,Yinchuan Li,Ying-Cong Chen,James Cheng*

Abstract: Embodied vision-language models must answer questions from partial, evolving observations. Their success depends on both the reasoning capability selected and the evidence available to it. Expert routers adapt internal computation, while tool-augmented systems acquire external evidence; choosing these independently can leave a suitable expert without the evidence it needs. We introduce EKT-Router, a bounded framework that jointly selects a capability expert, optional knowledge, and perceptual tools. Outcome-derived signatures represent the overlapping strengths of General, Spatial Reasoning, and Spatiotemporal experts. A joint utility scores complete expert-evidence routes under a call budget. After acquisition, a packet verifier admits valid evidence with positive predicted expert-conditioned gain. The selected expert then answers once, reverting to the unchanged observation if all packets are rejected. Across 6 embodied QA benchmarks, EKT-Router achieves a mean score of 61.4, the highest among the compared systems, and averages 74.7 on 10 general vision-language benchmarks. With all external evidence disabled, question-conditioned expert selection alone improves over the best fixed expert by 1.75 points. Ablations show that learned routing is the dominant component and that knowledge and perception are complementary.

</details>

Abstract: Integration of artificial intelligence (AI) technology into identity workflows, such as automated face recognition (FR), has enhanced operational capabilities, with FR algorithms achieving super-human scale and accuracy. However, these gains can make human oversight of FR more difficult, raising questions about the effectiveness of these human-AI teams. We measured human reviewers' ability to detect false positive errors made by commercial FR algorithms across varying security configurations. Using a novel dataset, the Difficult Facial Image Comparison Test (DFICT), derived from false positives made by four comparable commercial face recognition algorithms, we measured the ability of 801human reviewers to detect algorithmic false positives across three security configurations, representing operationally relevant biometric thresholds. Our findings show that human reviewers can reduce algorithmic false positives, but performance declines at stricter security thresholds. Further, reviewer performance varied by algorithm indicating that some algorithms are more compatible with human oversight. We suggest that biometric systems should be designed to account for human reviewers' variable effectiveness, particularly in high-security applications. Our findings should be considered during algorithm selection and inform human oversight strategies to minimize AI-related risks in operational FR deployments.

</details>

Abstract: Simulating an individual's disease course requires connecting patient history to future events, clinical timing and disease morphology. Longitudinal prediction and multimodal representations exploit this information, but organizing future readouts around a common patient state and advancing trajectories without real intermediate observations remain central challenges. Here we introduce Saga, a multimodal patient world model that learns a shared state from patient histories to predict clinical events and generate multimodal images. Generated events and times are appended to the history and re-encoded to advance prediction. We studied 461,370 patients with 12 cancer types across 112 centres. Modality-specific paired evaluations linked future images to observed follow-up. With historical images held fixed, removing patient conditioning reduced event F1 from 0.716 to 0.591 and increased lesion-burden error from 11.8% to 21.3%. In free rollouts, eight-step event F1 was 0.672, compared with 0.553 for a longitudinal Transformer evaluated from the same origins, and timing error was lower by 8.7 days. In the internal test set, one-year progression AUROC was 0.882, with values of 0.871 and 0.856 in temporal and geographical external evaluations. These results show that shared patient states connect future phenotypes across observational scales with event-driven trajectory progression, sustaining comparative predictive advantages during rollout and discrimination in external clinical evaluation. Saga makes patient-level disease simulation a multimodal prediction task that can be tested against observed follow-up.

</details>

Abstract: Face recognition systems have been ubiquitously deployed across daily scenarios. To protect user privacy, mainstream systems store facial templates rather than raw face images for identity verification. However, facial template inversion (FTI) technologies can reconstruct faces from templates, which endangers biometric privacy. Existing FTI methods only fit single type of templates, failing to reconstruct identity-consistent faces from unseen types. To tackle this limitation, we survey mainstream template extractors which can be classified into five categories according the template distribution: unit-sphere, angle-linear, strict unit-sphere, cone-shaped, anuniform hypersphere. Inspired by this observation, this paper proposes GMoE-FTI, a universal FTI framework powered by a genuine MoE mechanism that differs from load-balanced multi-branch alternatives. It follows a three-stage training strategy: (1) independently train five mapping experts (MEs) and a matching module (MM); (2) conduct collaborative inference by weighting each ME's output with MM's output, so as to generate unified latent codes compatible with pre-trained StyleGAN3; (3) perform end-to-end joint fine-tuning over all modules to identity consistency and boost cross-expert coordination. Extensive evaluations on the LFW benchmark validate the superiority of GMoE-FTI against SOTA methods. Evaluated on seven prevailing unseen template types including AdaFace, CosFace, CurricularFace, ElasticFace, SphereFace2, SphereFace-R, and TransFace, GMoE-FTI achieves an average 40% TAR improvement, with average FAPD and FAPC each reduced by 2%, verifying its universal generalization across heterogeneous templates.

</details>

Abstract: While Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have revolutionized general visual perception, existing methods suffer from severe hallucination in the aerial domain, where extreme scale variations and visual ambiguity abound. Existing Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) paradigms force models to mimic ground truth without modeling uncertainty, often resulting in blind guessing on tiny or unrecognizable objects in aerial scenes. In this paper, we present Aerial-R1, the first aerial MLLM framework to reinforce visual reasoning capabilities for aerial reasoning and segmentation. Drawing inspiration from the reasoning-incentivizing mechanisms of DeepSeek-R1, we find that trustworthy perception stems from consistency across views, establishing a visual parallel to the Chain-of-Thought in logic. To effectively adapt policy optimization for non-autoregressive mask generation, we introduce Hybrid-View Group Advantage-Weighted Regression (H-GAWR). This strategy constructs dynamic groups of high-resolution and low-resolution views, seamlessly bridging group-relative advantage estimation with advantage-weighted policy extraction. By enforcing consensus on clear inputs and strictly penalizing hallucinations on ambiguous ones, Aerial-R1 develops an explicitly incentivized refusal mechanism. Extensive experiments on VSAI-Ref, the first aerial MLLM benchmark dedicated to jointly evaluating dense reasoning and hallucination robustness, demonstrate that Aerial-R1 significantly outperforms SFT baselines in segmentation precision while substantially mitigating fatal hallucinations in low-information scenarios, paving the way for more reliable and trustworthy aerial agents. Codes are available on https://github.com/phy709/Aerial-R1.

</details>

Abstract: Referring segmentation in low-altitude drone imagery requires distinguishing small, visually similar instances under complex linguistic and spatial constraints. Global inference may yield multiple plausible masks or leave the intended referent insufficiently represented, motivating selective local re-inference. We propose DRA-SAM, a disagreement-driven framework that allocates an additional inference pass according to competing referential hypotheses and an unresolved state. A lightweight EvidenceScorer estimates beliefs over existing candidates and the unresolved state. A training-free Referential Disagreement LOOK policy balances belief-weighted candidate competition and unresolved-region coverage to select a crop for local re-inference. The same benchmark-adapted SAM3-I backbone processes the selected region, and the resulting evidence updates existing hypotheses and introduces new candidates. Among the compared methods, DRA-SAM achieves state-of-the-art performance on six of seven test metrics on both RIS-LAD and UAVid-RIS, including mIoU and P@0.5-P@0.9, and on all seven RIS-LAD validation metrics. On RIS-LAD, DRA-SAM improves the matched global SAM3-I baseline from 49.55 to 52.34 mIoU and from 46.80 to 58.05 P@0.5. On UAVid-RIS, the independently adapted DRA-SAM achieves 73.26 mIoU and 86.86 P@0.5. Controlled crop-policy comparisons demonstrate the effectiveness of disagreement-guided crop selection, while target-size analysis reveals particularly pronounced gains for aerial targets occupying less than 1% of an image. These results establish referential disagreement as an effective criterion for allocating local inference. Code is available at https://github.com/phy709/DRA-SAM.

</details>

Abstract: Steady-state visual evoked potential (SSVEP)-based brain-computer interfaces (BCIs) achieve high information transfer rates but often require sufficient subject-specific calibration data for reliable decoding. Analytical spatial filters are effective with limited data, but their linear formulations constrain representation capacity. Deep models learn more flexible representations but are prone to overfitting when calibration data are scarce. We propose KDHNet, which combines knowledge-driven analytical processing with data-driven representation learning. After filter-bank preprocessing, cascaded task-discriminant component analysis (TDCA) and task-related component analysis (TRCA) extract discriminative and trial-reproducible components. Learnable sub-band weighting then fuses spectral information, and an SSVEPformer decoder learns temporal and spectral representations for classification. For inter-subject decoding, a target-guided least-squares transformation aligns source trials using one labeled target trial per class. Extensive experiments were performed on three public datasets, covering intra- and inter-subject protocols, five analysis windows, and multiple calibration ratios. In intra-subject evaluation, KDHNet achieved the highest mean accuracy and information transfer rate in 14 of 15 window settings and all ten calibration-ratio settings. In inter-subject evaluation, KDHNet obtained the best or second-best performance, and the gains were generally larger with less calibration data. Ablation studies indicated that the estimated TDCA projection performed better when fixed than when optimized from analytical or random initialization. Combining fixed knowledge priors with trainable neural modules is effective for low-calibration SSVEP decoding. This framework supports more practical, robust, and plug-and-play BCIs. Code is available at https://github.com/2665023991/KDHNet.

</details>

Abstract: Pathology foundation models (PFMs) differ substantially in architecture, pretraining objective, training data and representation dimensionality, yet it remains unclear whether they learn fundamentally different feature spaces or alternative coordinate systems of a shared underlying representation. We present MOSAIC, a systematic study of latent-space similarity, alignment and interoperability across $12$ patch-level and $6$ slide-level PFMs. We first quantify representational agreement using complementary similarity measures and analyze how architecture, pretraining objective, network depth and magnification influence latent geometry. We then evaluate multiple alignment strategies for mapping independently learned representations into a common latent space. The resulting representations are evaluated through cross-model agreement, retrieval, geometry preservation, and downstream multiple-instance learning across molecular and morphological prediction tasks. MOSAIC provides a unified framework for studying whether heterogeneous PFMs can move beyond representational similarity toward a shared and functionally transferable pathology representation space. Our code, benchmarks and additional results are available at https://researchsubmissions66.github.io/MOSAIC/.

</details>

Abstract: Generating an articulated object from images and text requires more than plausible parts and movable joints: the assembled mechanism must execute the intended motion. This is particularly challenging for closed-chain and coupled mechanisms, where an error in one connection can block motion or separate connected parts. We introduce MARC, Mechanism-Aware Reflective Correction, which shares an executable kinematic graph across program construction, motion solving, and correction. The graph binds physical connections to local attachment frames and represents loop closures and joint-coordinate couplings. Given prescribed driver inputs, a numerical solver computes follower motion and associates errors with specific connections and configurations. The agent uses these diagnostics to probe possible causes, revise connections or geometry, and retest the mechanism along the original motion paths. On a 20-object test set, MARC with Kimi-K3 achieves 67.8% valid motion coverage and 60.0% overall success, outperforming six baselines. Starting from the same initial candidates, correction improves these scores by 13.7 and 20.0 percentage points, respectively. Ablations further show the benefits of graph-based mechanism support and graph-guided repair. Project homepage: https://demianmusk.github.io/MARC-Home/.

</details>

Abstract: Unified large multimodal models (ULMMs) are able to perform visual reasoning through interleaved text-image generation, but generating explicit intermediate images incurs significant computational overhead. While visual latent reasoning addresses this, most existing methods rely on vision-language models (VLMs) incapable of visual generation. We propose Oddin, a novel framework enabling latent visual reasoning in ULMMs. Oddin replaces intermediate images with compact latent tokens processed entirely by the understanding branch, reserving the generation branch for the final output image. We train the model via offline self-distillation with three objectives, final image generation losses, text distillation losses, and cross-entropy losses for special tokens. Oddin surpasses interleaved generation baselines and closed-source VLMs on average across seven VBVR-Pro visual reasoning tasks. Qualitative analysis reveals that latent tokens attend to relevant regions in an input image, demonstrating that ULMMs can perform complex visual reasoning without generating intermediate images.

</details>

Abstract: Deployed vision models auto-accept confident predictions, so high-confidence errors propagate silently. Maximum Softmax Probability (MSP), the standard failure predictor, has a structural blind spot: as a scalar read from the classifier head, it is first-order blind to feature variation orthogonal to its confidence gradient. Through a factorial analysis of feature-space detectors, we identify a conditional-redundancy principle explaining why they fail to complement MSP: the class-separability geometry they measure is already encoded in MSP and collapses under fusion. The signal that survives is class-conditional typicality on the off-manifold residual---and because this residual's distribution varies by backbone, from near-Gaussian to heavy-tailed, it must be estimated non-parametrically. We instantiate these findings as CANARY, a training-free detector that computes a per-class kNN distance on the PCA residual of the penultimate feature, percentile-calibrates it on validation data, and fuses it with MSP through a single validation-tuned weight. CANARY's benefit follows a headroom law: gains grow as baseline MSP quality falls, so a single clean-validation number forecasts the improvement. With every parameter selected on in-distribution data alone, CANARY matches or improves 382 of 385 shift cells (e-AURC; 379 on AUROC) across five backbones, with worst-case regression within seed noise ($-0.0002$ AUROC) and the largest gains on natural distribution shifts (ImageNet-A/R, ObjectNet)---where deployed models are most exposed.

</details>

Abstract: EEG visual decoding remains difficult because scalp recordings are noisy, spatially coarse, and variable across trials and subjects. Most systems align each trial with a dense image embedding, although that embedding mixes semantic content with instance-specific texture, pose, and background. This target can demand details absent from EEG and provides no explicit account of the visual factors supporting a prediction. We propose \textbf{ProtoEEG}, a prototype-mediated framework that reformulates EEG--image alignment as soft composition over a shared learnable visual codebook. A trainable EEG encoder and a frozen visual teacher produce assignment distributions over the same $K=64$ prototypes. Their weighted reconstructions form a structured representation for retrieval and image generation. Prototype contrast supplies the main cross-modal training signal, distribution alignment coordinates codebook use across modalities, an instance anchor preserves fine-grained discrimination, and diversity regularization prevents redundant prototypes. The shared assignment profile also makes the learned bottleneck directly testable through prototype retention, random selection, and identity shuffling. Across five seeds, ProtoEEG reaches 64.0\% Top-1 and 91.5\% Top-5 on THINGS-EEG2 200-way retrieval. After retraining on EEG-ImageNet, it obtains 44.8\% Top-1 and 73.8\% Top-5 in 80-way retrieval. ProtoEEG also ranks first on all seven reconstruction metrics. Removing prototype contrast lowers Top-1 by 56.9 percentage points, while shuffling prototype identities reduces it to 0.5\%. Prototype analyses further show distributed assignments and broad codebook use rather than one-prototype routing. These results support structured alignment targets as a practical alternative to increasing encoder or teacher capacity alone.

</details>

Abstract: We introduce OvisOCR3, a 0.8B model for a broad range of OCR-related tasks. OvisOCR3 unifies document parsing, general OCR, key information extraction (KIE), document figure captioning, and cross-page table reconstruction in a single model. We train the model on real and synthetic data through continued pretraining and use domain-balanced mid-training to balance supervision across tasks with different data volumes and response lengths. Multi-teacher on-policy distillation transfers expertise from five domain-specific 4B teachers into the shared model, followed by mixed-task reinforcement learning with domain-specific rewards. We compare OvisOCR3 with general-purpose vision-language models and specialized OCR models on public and in-house benchmarks covering all five task families. In these comparisons, it achieves an overall score of 96.74 on OmniDocBench v1.6 and the highest overall scores on PureDocBench, Real5-OmniDocBench, and MDPBench. On OCRBench v2, it ranks first, scoring 65.9 and 61.3 on the English and Chinese splits, respectively. It also outperforms the baselines on OCRBench and all four KIE benchmarks. On DocFigCapBench, it achieves the highest localization F1 and a caption score close to that of Qwen3.8-Max. For cross-page tables, it leads in Relation F1 on CrossTabBench and truncated-table TEDS on MPDocBench-Parse. These results demonstrate that a compact 0.8B model can achieve strong performance across diverse OCR-related tasks. OvisOCR3 is available at https://huggingface.co/ATH-MaaS/OvisOCR3.

</details>

Abstract: We present a decentralized multi-view tracking system for scalable camera networks. Single-view detections are projected to bird's-eye-view (BEV) coordinates and fused by independent multi-view trackers, each managing a dynamic spatial zone. To support scalability across spatially partitioned camera networks, we introduce a decentralized layer for trajectory unification, utilizing a locking protocol and periodic track-merging operations termed global collate. This process is supported by a delayed-output buffering mechanism that retroactively applies history corrections. The configurable delay window absorbs asynchronous coordination latency and helps stabilize cross-zone identity consistency. We detail the design of dynamic zone polygons, consensus-based coordination, and the validation framework, which together maintain global ID consistency with minimal accuracy loss compared to a single-instance baseline in the evaluated configurations.

</details>

Abstract: Image-to-Video (I2V) diffusion models can animate a single portrait or photograph, enabling shared images to be repurposed for motion synthesis without the subject's authorization. We study proactive visual protection against such use: before an image is released, its owner applies a bounded perturbation that preserves the sharing workflow but disrupts downstream motion generation. Existing spectral disruption objectives globally attenuate temporal representations and therefore provide limited control over subtle or spatially localized motion. Our key idea is that generated dynamics depend not only on temporal feature strength, but also on the order in which feature events occur. We propose PhaseBreak, a white-box spectral retiming protection method that matches intermediate denoiser features to a constrained temporal rearrangement of their clean counterparts. PhaseBreak operates along the true temporal axis and uses a subject-aware mask to confine the protective perturbation to the depicted subject. Evaluations on Stable Video Diffusion and DynamiCrafter show that protected images induce greater short-term motion variation and lower temporal regularity, impeding stable motion customization from the released image.

Abstract: Understanding how vision-language models use visual information requires connecting internal representations to model behavior. We present vlmprobe, an open-source library that integrates lens readouts, attention attribution, causal interventions, and sparse autoencoder (SAE) analysis within a common model interface. Shared adapters, calibration and checkpoint utilities, evaluation pipelines, and visualizations support systematic analysis across architectures. Using this framework, we compare methods across vision-language, language, and vision models through matched case studies and controlled evaluations of pretrained sparse dictionaries. The comparisons show that representation readouts and spatial explanations depend on the analysis objective and observation site, and that local attribution estimates can disagree with measured intervention effects. SAE evaluations further expose distinct trade-offs in reconstruction fidelity, concept readout, and spatial localization. Together, these results support combining multiple forms of evidence to evaluate an interpretation. vlmprobe provides a reusable basis for selecting methods, comparing their conclusions, and testing hypotheses about model behavior. Abstract: Vision-language-action (VLA) models enable robots to perform tasks specified through natural-language instructions, but their large weight footprint challenges deployment on memory-constrained onboard platforms. Ultra-low-bit quantization reduces this footprint, yet preserving task performance and controlling weight recovery costs remain challenging. We propose Action-Guided Quantization, which combines action sensitivity with a measured proxy for weight recovery cost to allocate matrix bitwidths under storage budgets. Per-channel calibration compensates for quantization error, while a uniform bitwidth within each matrix preserves regular layouts. Bit-dependent grouped lossless coding reduces resident weight storage, and block-wise recovery limits temporary buffers. On LIBERO, AGQ achieves 92.7% task success at W3, exceeding AWQ and GPTQ by 1.2 and 3.7 percentage points, respectively, and retains 70.5% success at W2.5. An asymmetric configuration, denoted W1.5$^{*}$, uses W4 vision weights and a parameter-weighted average of 1.5 bits for language weights. With lossless coding, it achieves $6.10\times$ complete-model compression relative to BF16 while retaining 25.5% task success. Abstract: Large-scale AI training increasingly relies on massive video datasets with random temporal sampling patterns. However, existing video storage paradigms are optimized for sequential playback and employ sophisticated inter-frame reference structures to maximize compression efficiency. When applied to the random-sampling workloads of AI training, these paradigms face substantial decoding-efficiency bottlenecks. Workarounds such as independent JPEG frames and short groups of pictures (GOPs) reduce decoding work at the cost of larger storage and transfer volumes. In this paper, we identify two sources of decode amplification: sequential reconstruction of unrelated frames and reconstruction of the reference chains required by sampled targets. We address both sources by co-designing the decoding schedule and video reference structure. Dependency-aware sparse decoding reconstructs only requested frames and their required references, skipping unrelated reconstruction without transcoding the source videos. Optional offline Dependency-Decoupled Random Access (DDRA) encoding further reduces reference reconstruction by restricting each interior frame to two bounding intra-coded anchors. Together, sparse decoding and DDRA retain inter-frame prediction while bounding reconstruction to at most three frames per requested target, independent of the intra period. Experiments on a large-scale robotics corpus show that, at comparable reconstruction quality, our method achieves $1.45\times$ the loading throughput of LeRobot v3 with 58.7% less media payload.

</details>

Abstract: The calibrated thermal channel in infrared person detection comprises two structurally orthogonal axes. True calibrated $ΔT$ outperforms scale-matched grayscale pseudo-$ΔT$ by $+0.0221$ mAP (paired Wilcoxon $p=0.0098$, Cohen's $d=+1.08$), confirming that the calibrated channel carries information the grayscale channel lacks. The calibration axis is driven by the joint pairing-scale condition. Preserving both paired correspondence and absolute calibrated scale raises expected calibration error (ECE) from $\approx 0.05$ to $0.142$. The $2\times 2$ interaction ($p=0.0059$) localizes the rise to this condition. Breaking either factor alone returns ECE to the broken-factor level (true-vs-const $p=0.002$; true-vs-shuffled $p=0.002$). Under post-hoc temperature scaling, the pairing cost survives recalibration ($p=0.002$), while the scale cost is largely absorbed ($p=0.16$). As regards the detection axis, the gain stems from structural de-structuring. Phase randomization, which destroys spatial structure while preserving per-sample variance, yields $+0.0185$ mAP over true $ΔT$ on FLIR ADAS ($p=0.014$, $d=+0.96$). The FLAME~3 gain is $+0.0064$ mAP ($p=0.078$, n=8). On the calibration axis, the same intervention is insignificant on FLIR ADAS ($p=0.47$) but significant on FLAME~3 ($p=0.0078$). These findings rest on a five-arm controlled attribution with temperature-evidential learning (TEL) as the testbed. TEL obtains a single evidence-derived routing scalar together with a complementary diagnostic entropy, and establishes a finite-evidence degeneracy theorem proving that the acquisition product degenerates to this scalar with a closed-form bound. A physical $0.5^\circ$C threshold admits cross-dataset physical readings, whereas a gray threshold of $20$ inverts without physical interpretation.

Abstract: Human activity recognition benchmarks underrepresent culturally situated forms of movement and interaction. We introduce Ampe-DB, a multimodal dataset based on Ampe, a traditional Ghanaian game involving coordinated jumping, leg movements, and two-person interaction. It contains 637 canonical clips from 15 participants, with RGB video, two-person pose representations, participant and pair metadata, four-class movement labels, and derived binary movement-relation labels.
  We evaluate four-class movement recognition and binary movement-relation classification using three protocols: a duplicate-safe fixed benchmark, four-fold pair-held-out evaluation, and participant-held-out diagnostics for participants with complete class coverage. Baselines comprise a frozen Kinetics-400-pretrained R(2+1)D-18 RGB encoder, a two-layer bidirectional GRU for pose sequences, and validation-selected RGB-pose late fusion.
  For four-class recognition, RGB macro-F1 falls from 0.955 on the fixed benchmark to 0.418 under pair-held-out evaluation and 0.384 under participant-held-out evaluation. Pose is more robust, achieving 0.957, 0.811, and 0.839 across the same protocols. Fusion reaches 0.976 on the fixed benchmark and 0.824 under pair-held-out evaluation, but does not consistently improve on pose under participant shift. Binary movement-relation recognition also becomes substantially harder under held-out evaluation, especially for different-leg configurations.
  These findings show that strong fixed-benchmark scores can mask failures to generalize across people and pairings. Ampe-DB supports research on fine-grained movement recognition, human-human interaction, multimodal learning, and identity-aware generalization.

</details>

Abstract: Precise camera control is essential for cinematic character video generation, yet existing methods struggle to follow large-angle trajectories with combined rotation, translation, and radius variation from arbitrary initial azimuths. Such viewpoint changes also reveal character regions unseen in the initial frame, causing geometric distortions, appearance degradation, and identity drift. To systematically study these coupled challenges, we introduce CLACT, a large-scale Character-centric dataset and benchmark covering diverse Large-Angle Camera Trajectories, with accurate camera geometry and multi-view character observations for training and evaluation. We further propose WorldCast, a framework for world-consistent character video generation under diverse large-angle camera motions. Specifically, we back-project the initial frame into a 3D point cloud using estimated depth, align the point-cloud scale with the target camera trajectory, and render it along the trajectory to obtain explicit geometric guidance and coverage masks. To complement the incomplete character information, we introduce Geometry-Aligned Memory Attention (GAMA). Using relative camera poses estimated jointly from the initial frame and multi-view character anchors, we express anchor and target-view geometry in a common coordinate frame. GAMA uses this geometric alignment to guide the retrieval and injection of view-relevant character features during generation. Evaluations across diverse large-angle camera trajectories demonstrate accurate trajectory following while preserving character geometry, appearance, and identity.

</details>

Abstract: Deep learning approaches for retinal optical coherence tomography (OCT) predominantly rely on predefined disease categories, limiting their ability to detect rare or previously unseen abnormalities. While visual foundation models provide transferable representations, their effectiveness in identifying pathological deviations from normal retinal anatomy remains insufficiently understood. Abstract: Real-world environments undergo continual semi-static changes, requiring a memory representation that captures both the spatial and temporal evolution of a scene. Existing spatio-temporal memories for robots typically rely either on structured representations, which are limited to the entities and attributes captured during construction, or on image-based memories, which are constrained to previously observed viewpoints. We introduce GEMS, a framework that combines a persistent spatio-temporal 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) map with an agentic reasoning pipeline. The 3DGS map is reconstructed once and subsequently updated only in regions where the scene changes, storing only these modifications while preserving visual detail across time. The agentic pipeline leverages this map to render novel views on demand, iteratively accumulating evidence to answer spatio-temporal questions and ground objects from natural-language queries. To advance future research, we introduce GEMS-Bench, a spatiotemporal question-answering benchmark focused on semi-static environments. It comprises 3 real-world and 12 synthetic environments, spanning 473 question-answer pairs. We evaluate GEMS on novel-view synthesis quality across scene updates, 3D object grounding, spatiotemporal question answering, and storage efficiency. GEMS outperforms recent baselines in 3D object grounding and spatiotemporal question answering, while achieving rendering quality on par with dedicated scene-update methods. Abstract: Interactive video world models require coordinated control over viewing perspectives, character actions, and camera motion. Abstract: Many real-world signals are corrupted by localized, structured perturbations whose distribution is unknown: an intermittent background noise in a music recording, a watermark stamped on a photograph, a cast shadow. We study the task of recovering a clean signal $x$ from an observation $y$ containing such a localized out-of-distribution (OOD) corruption. We propose Support-guided OOD Removal with Diffusion, SORD, an iterative algorithm to recover $x$ using only two ingredients: a binary map of the corruption support and a single diffusion model pretrained on clean signals. SORD does not require any paired data or any statistical model of the corruption. We evaluate SORD on natural images and human faces with spatially localized corruptions including handwritten digits, other images, watermarks, and shadows, as well as audio recordings of different musical instruments with temporally localized corruptions including other instruments and human speech. SORD is competitive with, and often outperforms, posterior-sampling inpainting given the same diffusion prior and the same mask, source-separation baselines that require a separate diffusion model for each source, and, on several benchmarks, supervised networks trained on paired clean and corrupted signals. Abstract: Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) has improved the performance of large Vision-Language Models (VLMs) on image quality assessment (IQA). Existing approaches often attribute these gains to improved reasoning before scoring, supported by increasingly plausible Chain-of-Thought (CoT). However, whether these gains exceed sampling effects and how they depend on explicit reasoning remain unclear. We rigorously investigate these questions through sampling-aware evaluation, controlled reasoning interventions, and shortcut experiments. We find that RL-trained models outperform base models under large-budget $\operatorname{max}_g@k$ evaluation, while improvements beyond averaging are concentrated in-distribution. RLVR produces chains that raise base-model performance when transplanted and improves scoring with the chain held fixed. Yet scoring becomes less dependent on the chain during inference, more clearly at 7B than at 32B. Steering the probed quality direction in thinking tokens also has little effect on scoring. Finally, RL can improve scoring accuracy by exploiting textual cues correlated with mean opinion scores (MOSs), which could inflate benchmark performance. Together, these findings show that RLVR raises quality scoring and produces more useful chains, but that better scoring does not imply a stronger reliance on explicit reasoning, while score-based rewards can also encourage reliance on quality-irrelevant shortcuts. We hope this preliminary study can enlighten more and more concrete reasoning schemes in IQA.

Abstract: Masked patch self-distillation is a key component of modern vision foundation models, including DINOv2 and DINOv3, yet its implications for local visual information remain unclear. We show that its semantic benefits can coexist with a scale-dependent locality cost. Using two annotation-free diagnostics, we distinguish recoverable local appearance from spatial correspondence. In smaller models, adding iBOT substantially weakens patch correspondence while largely preserving appearance decodability. In larger models, correspondence improves, but pixel and texture evidence becomes less linearly accessible in deep features. Thus, scaling changes the form of the locality cost rather than uniformly eliminating it. Our analysis of Gram anchoring further shows that preserving patch relations does not guarantee simultaneous improvements in both properties. These findings motivate evaluating and designing visual representations around the complementary demands of semantic abstraction, appearance preservation, and spatial correspondence. Abstract: Despite a ban on the international trading of recently poached ivory, elephants are still killed for their tusks. Ivory and its products are mainly sold in online marketplaces. However, due to the huge volume and variety of adverts and the similarity of ivory with other materials that can be legally sold, it is hard for enforcement officials to identify illicit trading and prosecute sellers. Existing techniques for identifying elephant ivory, such as Fourier-transform infrared spectroscopy and microscopy, can reliably distinguish ivory from other materials, but require physical access to the item and cannot be applied to photographs alone. As a result, these methods are unsuitable for screening the large volume of images posted in online marketplaces, leaving a gap for automated, image-based identification tools. In this proof of concept work, we investigate the potential of machine learning techniques for image analysis to automatically distinguish ivory objects from bone. We employ a semantic segmentation framework using a U-Net architecture with a ResNet34 backbone, leveraging transfer learning on an augmented dataset of 541 original images. Using a dataset of images of ivory and items resembling carved ivory, several models were trialled. On a distinct test set, the classifier achieved an Area Under the Curve (AUC) of 0.81 and an overall classification accuracy of 81% on confident samples. The paper presents the best-performing model as a proof of concept for developing a system that can effectively identify ivory and its products when indicative features of elephant ivory are present in an image. This work provides initial steps towards a machine learning-based framework designed to advise law-enforcement in detecting illegal elephant ivory trade.

Abstract: Visual effects are often easier to demonstrate via reference than to describe in words. We study effect-reference editing, where a reference image shows a desired visual effect and language identifies which properties should guide the edit. Existing single-pass editing techniques often fail to make fine-grained edits, resulting in unintended changes to source content or deviations from the reference effect. We introduce KINESYS, a test-time adaptation framework for effect-reference editing that combines reference-aware and source-aware feedback to transfer the desired effect while preserving the composition and content of the original shot. To ensure KINESYS can produce a diverse set of edits that preserve the source, we develop a robust optimization strategy in which the generative model is fine-tuned across multiple noise samples and optimization runs. Evaluation with VLM judges, source preservation metrics (LPIPS, TPIPS), and a user study shows that KINESYS outperforms state-of-the-art image editors in source preservation; on effect transfer, it remains competitive with Nano Banana 2 and outperforms open-weight editors.

Abstract: Vision foundation models for retinal imaging are large and computationally demanding, limiting point-of-care deployment. It combines gradient-free codebook learning via Growing Neural Gas, which yields an adjacency graph over the codes, with a graph-distance soft cross-entropy loss that replaces one-hot MIM targets with topologically graded supervision. With student pretraining on 383 public Duke iAMD optical coherence tomography (OCT) volumes, the pipeline distills an 86M-parameter teacher into a 22M-parameter student with at least 3.6x fewer FLOPs. The student performs close to, and in some settings above, its teacher across three classification and two segmentation benchmarks, with the magnitude and direction of the difference depending on the teacher, task, metric, and student capacity. Abstract: Motion perception is fundamental to computer vision and physical AI, and plays a central role in robotics, autonomous vehicles, human-computer interaction, and video compression. Traditional approaches such as background subtraction, frame-differencing and optical flow algorithms often struggle in complex or dynamic scenes, typically sacrificing spatial resolution for computational tractability. Here we introduce MoViD (Motion estimation via Virtual dispersion and phase Detection), a physics-inspired algorithm grounded in the principles that govern photonic time stretch - a highly successful ultrafast realtime data acquisition platform. Drawing inspiration from the optical physics therein, MoViD treats video as a collection of optical time series waveforms associated with pixels. Motion of an object through a pixel broadens the time series spectrum and, after group delay dispersion, induces a temporal phase that exists only if there is motion. Spatiotemporal group delay dispersion templates, corresponding to dielectric functions, classify specific motion trajectories. Unlike conventional empirical phase-based motion-analysis pipelines that require narrow-band spatial channelization and pyramid reconstruction, the physics-based MoViD operates directly on the broadband video signal, vastly increasing its computational speed. The resulting algorithm achieves unprecedented performance in both motion characterization and motion magnification, providing full-resolution motion mapping in real time at nearly 200 frames per second at 4K resolution and 70 frames per second for 8K video - orders of magnitude faster than classical optical flow approaches or learning-based methods. Notably, such extreme performance enables real time motion analysis at ultrahigh resolution for motion detection and compensation in robotics and other autonomous devices.

</details>

Abstract: Video models have the architecture to support encoding temporal aspects of the input, i.e. concepts that require more than one frame to define, like object movement. However, because motion and appearance change are entangled, a feature that appears to encode temporal concepts like object movement may instead encode non-temporal concepts like spatial presence at different positions whose activations merely correlate with motion. To resolve this, we introduce the No-False-Positives (NFP) test, a statistical test with a formal guarantee of statistical specificity for identifying features encoding temporal concepts. Using specially designed stimuli, we apply this test to representations produced by VideoMAE, V-JEPA2, and TimeSformer, and observe that temporal features exist in each model. We validate the test first by showing it yields no temporal features for the DINOv2 image-encoder. Then, using a synthetic representation space, we show that detection rate increases with a direction's projection onto the temporal subspace, and that Sparse Autoencoder features test-flagged as temporal project much more of their norm onto the temporal subspace than unflagged features. Interventions on temporal SAE features yield mixed results across models, suggesting that intervening on temporal features may not outperform a non-temporal feature baseline.

</details>

Abstract: Event cameras provide microsecond temporal resolution, high dynamic range, and sparse asynchronous measurements, making them compelling for odometry under fast motion and challenging illumination. Yet, accurate event-based state estimation remains difficult: existing systems often use events as a complement to frames or rely on feature tracking, correspondence search, and optimization-heavy backends, limiting the efficiency promised by event sensing. We introduce Tight Learned Event-Inertial Odometry (TLEIO), a compact filter-based pipeline that learns short-window relative motion directly from voxelized event streams and tightly fuses the resulting constraints with IMU propagation. Its learned front-end, EventsFormer, predicts local displacements and per-axis uncertainty from event voxels in a single forward pass, while a stochastic-cloning EKF fuses these overlapping predictions with IMU propagation, producing metric trajectories without explicit feature tracks and pose optimization at inference time. The network is trained directly from pose displacements, without requiring event-flow labels. We evaluate TLEIO on the TartanAir/TartanEvent benchmark and on real DAVIS240c sequences. On TartanAir, TLEIO reduces average ATE from 2.26 m to 1.10 m over the IMU-only TLIO baseline, while achieving up to 48.9% lower runtime than optimization-based event odometry methods.

</details>

Abstract: Multi-object tracking in satellite video underpins persistent monitoring, yet offline-tuned trackers lack the onboard intelligence and efficiency to adapt within an observation of tens of seconds. The root is that adaptation still means relabelling and retraining the detector, which cannot run on board in time. Tuning the tracker configuration strongly controls accuracy without touching the network, yet search needs labelled or proxy evaluations, and a language agent costs seconds and tokens per decision. We propose a dual-speed agentic framework pairing a fast instinct compiled offline with a slow reason serving the mission. Its algorithm, Arbitration of Unlabelled Shortlists from Prior Experience, or AUSPEX, learns a covering shortlist and a label-free ranker from labelled source tasks and commits one configuration per sequence. Its system, a Cascaded Onboard Runtime with Verified Uplinked Skills, or CORVUS, cascades reflex, skill, and agent layers, so that a verified skill commits 2.5 s into each observation while a language agent serves natural- language missions. On SatMTB and SatVideoDT with three recent detectors, AUSPEX raises IDF1 by 2.84 to 2.94 points and MOTA by 3.21 to 3.29 points on average without target labels, balances accuracy and labels best among sixteen methods, and loses at most 0.28 IDF1 points at 1.25 tracking passes. CORVUS executes four missions with execution accuracy up to 0.99 and target recall at the perception ceiling, spends no token on the routine decision, runs on Jetson Xavier NX, and hosts fifteen language models from six families without altering any commitment, demonstrating its extensibility and generalization.

</details>

Abstract: Generative models for microscopy can already produce realistic whole-image fields of view, but offer no principled way to control how each individual cell looks. We address this gap by recasting microscopy generation at the single-cell level and designing the input controls and the model's internal representation jointly, so that both are organized along the same biologically meaningful directions. On the input side, we aggregate a long list of standard cell measurements into a small set of semantic axes (size, elongation, brightness, texture, etc.) that are each named, measurable, and traversable. On the model side, we introduce BioVAE, an encoder-decoder whose latent space is explicitly aligned with the same set of measurements. A key finding is that the benefit of biologically structuring the latent emerges only when conditioning is also moved to the semantic axes, showing that the two ends must be designed jointly. Experiments on LIVECell show that semantic-axis conditioning substantially improves generative fidelity over high-dimensional conditioning, a blind study with biologists confirms the realism of generated cells, and per-axis traversals produce stable and interpretable phenotypic changes.

</details>

Abstract: Existing Ultra-High-Definition (UHD) low-light image enhancement (LLIE) methods typically rely on either spatial- or frequency-domain modeling, or independently process different frequency components for efficient restoration. However, they often fail to jointly exploit complementary spatial--frequency information and the inherent relationship between illumination and structural information across frequencies, leading to inconsistent illumination, residual noise, and detail artifacts. In this paper, we propose a novel Bidirectional Cross-frequency Domain Interaction Network (BiFreqNet) for UHD LLIE. Specifically, BiFreqNet incorporates a Dual-domain Feature Interaction (DFI) module into the multi-scale representations of a pretrained variational autoencoder (VAE) to perform reciprocal enhancement between low- and high-frequency features. DFI consists of a Spatial and Spectral-Aware Low-Frequency Enhancement (S$^2$LFE) block and a Cross-Frequency Enhancement (CFE) block. S$^2$LFE exploits high-frequency structural cues to guide low-frequency restoration and jointly models spatial and spectral information to recover global exposure and spatially varying illumination while suppressing sensor noise. CFE further leverages the enhanced low-frequency contextual information to guide adaptive high-frequency refinement, enabling faithful reconstruction of structures and fine details. Extensive experiments on four general and UHD LLIE benchmarks demonstrate that BiFreqNet achieves competitive or superior performance compared with state-of-the-art methods.

</details>

Abstract: Localized AI manipulations in long-form videos often affect brief events within otherwise authentic content, making video-level classification insufficient for forensic analysis. Existing audio-visual deepfake benchmarks largely focus on short talking-head clips, while long-form temporal forensics remains predominantly visual. We introduce AV-FABLE, a dual-timeline benchmark for sparse manipulations in open-scene long-form audio-visual videos. AV-FABLE contains 16,650 pristine--manipulated twin pairs (998 hours) created with 27 editing configurations, with separate audio and visual timelines that enable modality-specific temporal localization and expose errors hidden by joint-timeline evaluation. On AV-FABLE, zero-shot prompting of audio-visual large language models (AV-LLMs) yields near-chance detection and poor temporal localization. In contrast, supervised readouts of their frozen perception features recover strong forensic signals, suggesting manipulation evidence is encoded in these representations but not effectively exposed through language outputs. Motivated by this observation, we propose NFSR(Native Forensics via Separated Readouts), which reads frozen AV-LLM features with separate audio and visual temporal decoders. NFSR achieves the best audio-visual localization performance in-domain and across all three out-of-distribution settings, including transfer to held-out videos from an external long-video forensic benchmark. AV-FABLE, code, and model weights will be publicly released.

</details>

Abstract: Accurate assessment of Alzheimer's disease stages from MRI is important for understanding disease progression and supporting the development of computer-aided analysis tools. Deep learning models have shown promising results for Alzheimer's disease classification from MRI. However, when evaluated on single datasets, it is difficult to determine whether the observed performance reflects consistent architectural behavior or dataset-specific effects. In this work, we present a multi-dataset benchmarking framework with standardized preprocessing and evaluation. We compare five architectures: ResNet-50, ReXNet-150, Swin Transformer, ViT-Base, and ViT-Large. The models are evaluated across three MRI datasets representing severe class imbalance, train-test distribution mismatch, and large-scale augmentation. The results show broadly consistent performance patterns across datasets, with ViT-Large achieving the best performance on all three datasets and reaching up to 99.84% accuracy and 99.86% macro F1. Performance differences between architectures are most pronounced under distribution mismatch and considerably smaller in the augmented-data setting. Across datasets, lower class-level performance is observed primarily among adjacent early-stage categories, particularly Very Mild and Mild impairment. These findings highlight the importance of evaluating deep learning architectures across multiple data conditions rather than relying on single-dataset performance.

Abstract: Recent DETR-based tiny object detectors adopt dynamic query mechanisms to handle severe density imbalance. However, existing designs entangle two effects: how many and which queries are decoded. This entanglement obscures what drives the gains and complicates dynamic-query design; additional learned predictors and heuristic filtering also introduce overhead and potential selection errors. We disentangle these effects and show that dynamic query primarily acts as \emph{budgeting}---allocating decoder query capacity according to object density---rather than intricate query selection.Motivated by this view, we formulate a monotonic and conservative budgeting principle and propose a budgeting-based DETR (BUTR), a streamlined framework that uses a lightweight, non-learned budgeter to estimate an input-dependent query budget $K(x)$ from encoder confidences. BUTR retains standard Top-$K(x)$ query initialization without additional learned modules or heuristic filtering in the budgeter.At $800\times800$ resolution, BUTR-L achieves 35.7 AP at 47.8 FPS on AI-TOD-v2 and 29.8 AP at 42.1 FPS on VisDrone on an NVIDIA T4 GPU. Inference-time integration on COCO further demonstrates its applicability to general scenario detection. Code is available at https://github.com/zhangyi-magic/BUTR.

Abstract: Controllable image synthesis should modify visual affect without replacing the objects, layout, or relations specified by a prompt. Existing methods address this tension through conditioning architectures or auxiliary losses, while mixed ordinary differential equation (ODE) and stochastic differential equation (SDE) sampling has mainly been used to accelerate group relative policy optimization (GRPO). We instead study stochastic-window position as an attribute-control variable for flow-based image generation. Using FLUX.1-dev with EmotiCrafter-style valence-arousal conditioning, we compare fixed early, middle, and late four-transition SDE windows, progressive MixGRPO, and full-path Flow-GRPO under matched settings. Because step 0 has the highest noise, our semantic-first schedule uses deterministic ODE sampling at steps 0-19 to establish content, then confines stochastic exploration and policy updates to steps 20-23. Across three seeds, it achieves a CLIPScore of $29.662\pm0.276$, cross-affect DINO consistency of $80.1\pm0.9$, and semantic drift of 2.9%, while reducing training cost by 33.5% relative to full-path Flow-GRPO. Full-path stochastic training remains more accurate on valence-arousal targets, revealing a genuine trade-off. Semantic-first nevertheless improves both affect and semantic metrics over progressive scheduling, preserves the principal subject in 95.98% of images at five extreme or neutral targets, and receives the highest semantic-preservation and overall-preference ratings in a 30-participant study. These results identify probability-flow time as a practical graphics control dimension for reliable affective appearance manipulation.

</details>

We find that PE scales along three axes: training data, model size, and information density, with sufficient information density as a prerequisite for effective enhancement. Abstract: Lung tumor segmentation plays a vital role in early detection and treatment planning of lung cancer. We propose a lightweight, energy-efficient model that leverages the ENet architecture for its real-time inference capability and low computational cost, enhanced with UNet-style skip connections between encoder and decoder stages for feature reuse and the lightweight Convolutional Block Attention Module (CBAM) for improved feature representation. The model is trained on publicly available data from The Cancer Imaging Archive (TCIA) via the Medical Segmentation Decathlon Challenge (MSD) and evaluated on the Lung Image Database Consortium and Image Database Resource Initiative (LIDC-IDRI) dataset. Our model achieves a DSC of 0.90812 (threshold of 0.5) with its compact architecture of 430,052 parameters and 0.549 Giga Multiply-Accumulate operations (GMAC), making it up to 10 times faster, requires 79 times less GMAC than UNet, and has 22 times fewer parameters than SegNet. These characteristics make it well-suited for deployment in clinical settings where computational resources are limited and decrease the cost of utilizing such a system. Additionally, the explainable AI technique of Grad-CAM is used to provide visual interpretability of the model's predictions and to identify regions where segmentation performance requires improvement.

Abstract: Text-to-3D solid retrieval requires aligning natural language with precise geometry despite variations in pose, scale, and recording conventions. Existing methods discretize Boundary Representation (B-rep) models into point clouds or meshes, or learn features from faces, edges, and topology, risking information loss or lacking explicit invariance guarantees. We propose a text-to-B-rep retrieval framework based on group actions and spectral geometric invariants, making invariance an explicit constraint on descriptor construction. Specifically, we use group actions to characterize equivalence transformations induced by translation, rotation, reflection, uniform scaling, and contour recording conventions. For sketch-extrusion solids, we construct a fixed 280-dimensional spectral geometric descriptor by integrating geometric information from three complementary levels, including cross-sectional regions, contour boundaries, and extrusion structures. Furthermore, we use descriptor-consistent multi-positive sets and geometric attribute supervision to promote structural alignment between text and solids. On Text-to-3D solid retrieval, our method achieves 88.6% Recall@5, exceeding the previous state of the art by 13.6 percentage points and demonstrating the effectiveness of explicit geometric invariants for precise 3D solid retrieval.

Optimization-based solvers decide each contact's stick-slip state from the reaction that governs it, but only by re-optimizing the garment state at every step; a friction term in a one-pass training objective acts as a fixed-weight penalty on sliding, and the trained weights apply no per-contact Coulomb condition at inference. We therefore resolve friction explicitly after prediction: a Modular Contact Operator, attached to a frozen predictor without retraining or an iterative solve, estimates the normal load, applies a closed-form Coulomb correction along a path across body triangles, and propagates the induced motion through the cloth as a linearized membrane wave. Abstract: Automotive millimeter-wave (mmWave) radar has attracted increasing attention for semantic segmentation in autonomous driving, owing to its robustness under adverse weather and lighting conditions. Recent works have introduced radar-specific neural operators such as PeakConv (PKC), inspired by constant false alarm rate (CFAR) detection, to better model the peak-dominated characteristics of radar images. However, existing methods typically treat the guard bandwidth as learnable parameters, without explicitly characterizing the frequency response of the PKC operator. In this work, we revisit PKC from a frequency-domain perspective and propose Frequency-Aware PeakConv (FreqPKC), which explicitly models and exploits its underlying spectral characteristics. By analyzing the frequency response of PKC, FreqPKC enables the network to capture a broader range of frequency components and adaptively adjust its effective bandwidth. With the proposed Frequency-Aware (FA) module, the network can infer appropriate guard bandwidths to balance high- and low-frequency information: it enhances high-frequency components in target regions for more precise segmentation while suppressing noise through low-frequency filtering in background areas. Building on FreqPKC, we further develop FreqPKC-Net, a radar semantic segmentation (RSS) framework that integrates frequency-guided adaptive modulation with cross-view fusion for multi-view inputs, and can also be adapted to single-view data. Experiments on the public datasets CARRADA, CARRADA-RAC, and a self-built dataset named RS-SAR demonstrate that FreqPKC-Net consistently outperforms existing SoTA methods on both range-Doppler and range-angle views.

Abstract: Few-shot remote sensing scene classification (FSRSSC) aims to recognize novel scene categories from only a few labeled images. Most FSRSSC methods summarize the feature map of an image by global average pooling, which treats the few objects that define an aerial scene and the large background around them alike, and they rarely consider that aerial images have no canonical orientation. To address these issues, we propose RoCoP, a parameter-free component pooling network trained across orientations. RoCoP consists of a feature extractor, a non-negative decomposition module (NDM) and a component scoring module (CSM). NDM factorizes the local descriptors of each image into a few additive components with a deterministic initialization and a single multiplicative update, so that the positions of one object are grouped together. CSM weights each component by its activation magnitude and converts the weights into a soft mask for pooling. Because both modules are invariant to permutations of feature positions, the only rotation-sensitive part of the pipeline is the backbone, which we train to classify randomly rotated views of every image. Experiments on NWPU-RESISC45, AID and UC Merced show that component pooling and rotated-view training are complementary: together they improve a strong transfer-learning baseline by about 2.5 points in 5-way 1-shot accuracy on NWPU-RESISC45 and AID, without adding parameters at inference. Under an identical protocol, RoCoP outperforms re-implemented metric-based and task-adaptive methods, and NDM and CSM also improve existing methods when plugged into them. We further identify the regime in which the method does not apply, namely very small base sets.

</details>

Abstract: Running deep models for medical image analysis at the point of care is challenging with standard architectures: convolutional neural networks (CNNs) are often too heavy for edge devices, while Vision Transformers (ViTs) require massive training datasets to compensate for missing inductive biases. This study investigates how far the dual-token distillation used to train data-efficient ViTs can be pushed when applied across architectures, from a CNN-based teacher into a lightweight ViT student. LiteMed-ViT is introduced as a dual-token ViT featuring a convolutional stem and a class-balanced distillation objective; at 0.234M parameters, LiteMed-ViT carries 47.8x fewer parameters than its ResNet18 teacher at a memory footprint of just 0.89 MB. Evaluated against three compact CNN baselines on three MedMNIST subsets of varying modality and class balance, LiteMed-ViT attains higher accuracy than all three baselines on BloodMNIST (95.05%) and two on PathMNIST (89.15%). Under MedMNIST-C corruptions, it is at least as robust as every compact CNN across all subsets, outperforming them on BloodMNIST and PathMNIST without declining in relative standing as severity increases. Particular attention is devoted to identifying the exact conditions where performance plateaus. On DermaMNIST, LiteMed-ViT's balanced accuracy remains up to 1.9 points below the strongest compact CNNs and does not benefit from higher input resolutions up to 224 pixels, where ResNet18 gains 12.6 points. Stronger teachers do not yield a more accurate student, and overconfident ones lower its DermaMNIST balanced accuracy unless calibrated.

Abstract: Image tokenizers convert pixels into compact latent sequences for efficient generative modeling. Most approaches arrange latents on a downsampled two-dimensional grid, tying sequence length to image resolution. We compare two variable-length strategies: computationally efficient patch-derived tokens, which inherit explicit spatial organization, and more demanding register tokens, which have no predefined spatial layout. Our reconstruction results reveal that patch-derived tokens perform substantially better under extreme compression, whereas register tokens achieve slightly better reconstruction quality at intermediate budgets. At high token budgets, both approaches achieve similar reconstruction quality. For downstream generative tasks, register tokens outperform patch-derived tokens. Adding representation alignment with external pretrained visual encoders substantially improves the generative performance of patch-derived tokens, allowing them to match or outperform register tokens depending on the token budget. We further find that both approaches learn spatially localized representations. Patch-derived tokens largely retain their explicitly imposed spatial organization, while register tokens develop an implicit two-dimensional spatial structure despite having no predefined layout. Representation alignment reduces this spatial organizational constraint, enabling individual tokens to exert a stronger non-local influence.

</details>

Abstract: High-resolution remote sensing (RS) imagery provides fine spatial detail over large areas, making it essential for fine-grained scene understanding. Referring Remote Sensing Image Segmentation (RRSIS) aims to segment regions described by natural language, yet many existing methods rely on sequential alignment--fusion pipelines with limited joint modeling of cross-scale visual hierarchies and linguistic dependencies. Moreover, dense attention mechanisms struggle with the sparsity and wide spatial dispersion of RS targets, while current datasets offer limited structured evaluation of multi-target references, multi-hop relational reasoning, and multi-granularity descriptions, limiting both model development and evaluation. To address these challenges, we propose a Cross-Scale Sparse Attention Model (CSSAM), which aligns multiscale visual features with complex referring expressions through two key modules. The Cross-scale Modality-aligned Fusion Module (CMFM) performs unified cross-scale cross-modal fusion, while the Dynamic Multimodal Sparse Hybrid Attention (DMSHA) selectively attends to informative visual--textual tokens, reducing dense visual interactions. Furthermore, we introduce M$^{3}$RRSIS-D, a RRSIS dataset explicitly designed around three core reasoning dimensions: multi-target (M$^{1}$), reference-dependent multi-hop (M$^{2}$), and multi-granularity (M$^{3}$) expressions. Extensive experiments on M$^{3}$RRSIS-D and two public RRSIS benchmarks demonstrate that CSSAM achieves competitive performance across accuracy, fine-grained reasoning, and high-IoU localization metrics, validating its effectiveness for complex semantic reasoning in realistic RS scenarios. Code, dataset, and model weights will be released upon acceptance.

Abstract: Automated prostate lesion detection from diffusion-weighted MR imaging (DWI) conventionally relies on reconstructed images and does not fully use all acquisition information. The proposed network, VarNet-Seg, consists of a k-space variational network (VarNet) with six variational cascades and an image-space 3D segmentation U-Net. Comparator models used the same segmentation architecture with fully coil-combined images (CC-Seg), fixed root-sum-of-squares reconstruction (RSS-Seg), or fixed SENSE reconstruction (SENSE-Seg). We performed ablations with end-to-end training and data consistency (DC) enabled or disabled. We evaluated lesion segmentation using Dice and patient-level detection using AUROC in five-fold cross-validation with full sampling, simulated undersampling, added noise, or both.
  VarNet-Seg achieved the highest Dice at every undersampling level. At eight-fold acceleration, Dice was 0.22 for VarNet-Seg, 0.14 for SENSE-Seg and 0.16 for VarNet-Seg (no DC). The paired difference from SENSE-Seg was 0.08 (95% CI, 0.04-0.11). With full sampling, VarNet-Seg and RSS-Seg performed similarly (0.40 vs 0.39). Patient-level AUROC decreased from 0.75 with full sampling to 0.63 at eight-fold acceleration; corresponding RSS-Seg AUROCs were 0.76 and 0.64.
  The proposed multicoil network improved prostate lesion segmentation under simulated undersampling compared to conventional models. Matched ablations supported contributions from both end-to-end training and data consistency. Prospective evaluation is needed to establish whether this approach can be used clinically in accelerated prostate DWI.

Abstract: Video spatial reasoning requires multimodal large language models (MLLMs) to understand how objects are arranged and related across viewpoints. While these models can recognize objects in individual frames, integrating these observations to solve complex spatial tasks remains challenging. We examine route planning as a representative task and observe that existing models become substantially less accurate as the number of required turns increases, with several models achieving accuracy close to random guessing on routes requiring three or more turns. This finding suggests that recognizing landmarks alone is insufficient: models must also connect observations across viewpoints and track how landmarks are arranged in a shared space. Motivated by this observation, we introduce GeoLift, a training-free framework that uses feed-forward 3D reconstruction to map objects detected in individual frames into a shared metric 3D scene. GeoLift combines a frozen MLLM for 2D grounding with pretrained geometry models for scene reconstruction. It then uses shared geometric regions, called superpoints, to associate observations of the same object across views without a learned matcher. The reconstructed 3D map enables explicit computation of spatial relations and multistep routes, and provides geometric evidence for estimating distances and sizes. With all model weights frozen, GeoLift improves Qwen3-VL-4B's average score on VSI-Bench from 55.4% to 64.1%, including an increase in route-planning accuracy from 30.9% to 53.6%, and achieves 59.8% accuracy on MindCube. These results show that connecting frame-level object grounding through a shared 3D map can improve complex spatial reasoning without task-specific training.

</details>

Abstract: In the era of large-scale pretrained models, zero-shot generalization has increasingly blurred the boundary between known and unknown concepts. However, current evaluations do not directly reveal which open-world concepts different vision-language models can recognize. To study this problem, we introduce OODConceptBench, a hierarchical benchmark for open-world concept recognition on image-concept pairs. The full benchmark set (L1) contains 30,752 images, and the harder subset (L2) contains 14,285 images. The benchmark covers 560 concept phrases across seven open-world categories. We evaluate 37 vision-language models across release year, model family, and variant. We obtain three main findings. First, target-concept recognition is lower on L2 than on L1 across all 37 evaluated models. Second, later-released models do not necessarily achieve stronger open-world concept recognition. Third, an increase in overall recognition does not imply consistent improvement across all benchmark categories. Together, these results show that OOD remains a challenge even in the era of large-scale vision-language pretraining.

</details>

Abstract: Video world models are expected to generate videos that accurately follow specified camera trajectories while maintaining consistent 3D structures. However, existing models are predominantly trained on densely sampled videos with small inter-frame camera motions. When applied to sparse, large step trajectories, they often produce frozen frames, inaccurate camera motion, and severe geometric drift. We attribute this degradation to a mismatch between the gradual viewpoint transitions seen during training and the large viewpoint changes required at inference time. To address this problem, we propose Dense Trajectory Teacher Distillation (DTTD), a post-training framework that transfers a model's generation capability from dense trajectories to large camera motions. Given a sparse trajectory, DTTD interpolates additional camera poses to construct a dense trajectory covering the same path. The frozen base model then generates reliable teacher videos along this familiar dense trajectory, and pose aligned teacher observations are used to evaluate candidate videos generated under the original sparse trajectory. In this way, the model serves as its own generation teacher, requiring neither additional video data nor a stronger external video generator. We further combine dense teacher supervision with geometry aware rewards that assess cross frame consistency and camera trajectory following, and use the resulting preferences for offline preference optimization. To stably expand the model's motion range, we introduce a cumulative curriculum that progressively incorporates larger motion steps while retaining simpler trajectories to reduce capability forgetting.

</details>

Abstract: Existing methods for CAD reconstruction from 3D scans achieve strong performance on standard benchmarks, yet their effectiveness often degrades in real-world scenarios where objects consist of multiple parts and scan artifacts such as noise. A key reason is that current CAD reconstruction datasets predominantly contain single-part models and clean geometric inputs, limiting the ability of learned models to generalize to realistic settings. In this work, we introduce CADonatello, a part-aware framework for reconstructing parametric CAD models from real-world 3D scans. Our approach first decomposes the input geometry into structurally meaningful components using a foundation-scale 3D part segmentation model, and then reconstructs each component independently through a cross-section-driven pipeline that performs sketch parameterization followed by an extrusion. To improve the robustness of sketch parameterization to scan-derived cross-sections, we further introduce CC3D Cross-Sections, a dataset of paired rasterized cross-section images from realistic scans and their corresponding parametric sketches derived from aligned CAD models. This dataset enables effective domain adaptation from clean CAD sketch images to scan-derived cross-section images. Extensive experiments demonstrate that the proposed approach improves generalization to noisy scans and enables reliable reconstruction of complex multi-part objects across multiple real-world CAD reverse engineering benchmarks.
