Abstract: Megapixel visual synthesis with latent diffusion models requires operating far beyond the resolutions at which these models are typically trained. Cascade refinement is a leading strategy for this setting: it starts from a base image at a native resolution and progressively upsamples it to the target resolution through multiple refinement stages. Each stage typically performs a pixel-space resolution transition followed by latent-space denoising. While prior acceleration efforts mainly focus on reducing denoising cost, we identify a complementary and underexplored bottleneck: the repeated movement of representations through the VAE. We call these operations pixel-space transitions. In cascade pipelines, intermediate latents are often decoded to RGB, resized, and re-encoded before refinement, while final latents are decoded patch by patch with dense overlap to suppress boundary artifacts. We propose UltraFlash, a transition-efficient approach that accelerates these VAE-heavy operations. UltraFlash has two components. Latent Hyperloop replaces intermediate RGB round trips with a learned latent-space shortcut that approximates the decode-resize-encode transformation without repeated VAE decoding and encoding. Sparse Patch-based Decoding accelerates final reconstruction by addressing patch seams caused by inconsistent GroupNorm statistics and reusing cached statistics across patches, enabling sparse VAE decoding with much smaller overlap. Integrated into a state-of-the-art cascade baseline, UltraFlash reduces 4K generation latency by approximately 2x while improving image quality. These results show that optimizing pixel-space transitions can improve both efficiency and fidelity, offering an effective route toward fast, high-quality megapixel visual synthesis.

</details>

Abstract: While interactive video world models require autoregressive generation, directly adapting pretrained bidirectional video diffusion models into autoregressive ones with teacher forcing can disrupt their visual priors and degrade autoregressive generation quality. In this paper, we introduce Mix Forcing, a causal pre-training paradigm for interactive video world models, implemented with the Mixture-of-Bidirectional-and-Autoregressive (MoBA) attention. Mix Forcing jointly trains a video diffusion model with both teacher forcing and bidirectional training, yielding a strong causal initialization for few-step post-training. In self-attention, Mix Forcing leverages teacher forcing to learn causal next-chunk prediction for autoregressive rollout, while using bidirectional attention to preserve pretrained visual priors. To support online text interaction, Mix Forcing further adopts a chunk-wise cross-attention design that conditions each video chunk on a shared environment prompt and its own local prompt, without exposing future instructions to the current chunk. Following causal pre-training on a 14B image-to-video model, we apply consistency and distribution matching distillation to obtain a four-step autoregressive generator. Experiments on our internal benchmark and public benchmarks, including WBench and SANA-WM-Bench, show that our model achieves state-of-the-art visual quality and timely event following with precise camera control. Notably, our model supports high-fidelity long-horizon generation without quality degradation even over one-hour rollouts. Project page is here: https://zelingao98.github.io/mixforcing

Abstract: Before every neutral objective in professional League of Legends, each team decides which of its five players walk to the pit, which hold a lane, and when to commit. The leading analysis of such setups, TAGNet for Counter-Strike 2, clusters graph-network embeddings of player positions and reads cluster win rates as tactical quality. We treat a setup as an allocation on a walled map: formations are set partitions on the lattice $Π_5$, control is a geodesic Voronoi split of a navigation graph, and commitment is an allocation game solved as a quantal response equilibrium priced in win probability. On all 164 games of MSI and Worlds 2025 (2,994 team-setups, minimap tracks validated against Riot positions), trailing teams leave the pit empty far more often than leading teams, by +34.8 percentage points at the first baron and +30.4 percentage points at Atakhan. Our model, HASSE-QRE, finds six setup patterns that whole-model series-bootstrap refits reproduce (ARI 0.819, comparable to 0.813 for TAGNet's $K$-Means step alone) and indicates that teams price fights, travel and committed players, not the objective itself (weight 0.018; fixing it to one costs 59.9 log-likelihood points). The equilibrium reproduces a quarter of the commitment gap, almost all of it through state variables rather than its strategic channels. Weighting a team-level contrast by exposure moves it across $p=0.05$ (0.046 against 0.052). We release the tracks, setups and code.

</details>

Abstract: Pavement crack segmentation under low light is degraded by reduced contrast, shadows, headlight glare, joints, stains, and wet seams. Existing crack segmentation methods mostly assume good illumination. Low-light enhancement methods improve visual quality, but they do not explicitly preserve crack topology. To address these limitations, we formulate low-light crack segmentation as a degradation-aware, prompt-grounded structural defect segmentation problem and propose DROA-CLIPSeg, a parameter-efficient framework that adapts a frozen CLIPSeg vision language backbone using two lightweight modules. QKV Low-Rank Adaptation is injected into the Q/K/V projections of the vision and text encoders to improve crack-specific image text alignment without backbone fine-tuning. The proposed Domain Residual Orthogonal Adapter (DROA) decomposes intermediate features into a dominant crack-domain branch, which captures recurring low-light crack patterns, and an orthogonal residual branch, which preserves faint, thin, and fragmented evidence usually suppressed by noise, shadow, or over-smoothing. We also introduce CrackLingo-9K and CrackLingo-12K, text augmented versions of Crack9k and Crack12k with crack-aware prompts. Training only approximately 1.93 M parameters, corresponding to 1.3% of the full model, DROA-CLIPSeg outperforms 9 zero-shot and 4 fine-tuned vision language baselines on both splits, generalises zero-shot to MVTec AD across textures and defect morphologies, and is supported by extensive ablations. Our source code is publicly available at https://github.com/VShuklacode/DROA-CLIPSeg

Abstract: Pedestrian Attribute Recognition (PAR) predicts semantic attributes of individuals, such as gender, age, clothing and accessories, and its descriptions support attribute-based person retrieval and person re-identification. However, accurately recognizing fine-grained attributes across domains remains challenging due to occlusion, appearance variations, and shifts in image distributions. Ensembles of independently trained foundation encoders combine their logits with fixed fusion weights, so every attribute uses the same mix and the encoders are never trained together. We propose UniQuery, which instead jointly fine-tunes DINOv3 and CLIP for attribute-specific fusion. It combines two query heads, one for each encoder, with a joint query head whose attribute queries attend to tokens from both encoders, and the prediction is the average of the logits of the three query heads. We evaluate UniQuery on the benchmark of the UPAR Challenge 2027 at the WACV 2027 Real-World Surveillance workshop, where models are trained on PA100k, PETA and Market-1501 with a shared set of attributes. In leave-one-dataset-out experiments, UniQuery outperforms the independent ensemble in Challenge_Avg on both held-out datasets. Ablations show that learning fusion over independently trained encoders does not improve upon logit averaging in the mean over the two held-out datasets, while freezing the encoders lowers the Challenge_Avg of UniQuery. On the hidden test set of the benchmark, UniQuery achieves a Challenge_Avg of 0.8001.

Abstract: Attribute-based person retrieval ranks surveillance images from a binary query of semantic attributes, but mean Average Attribute Degree of Match (mADM) drops on unseen cameras as predicted probabilities shift. Abstract: GUI grounding requires locating small controls in cluttered screenshots and identifying which control a natural-language instruction refers to. Abstract: Continuous physiological monitoring is essential for linking behavior, internal state, and neural activity in neuroscience and translational research. In common marmosets, a key nonhuman primate model for social neuroscience, respiratory rate provides a basic readout of arousal, stress, and physiological regulation. Contact sensors can provide accurate measurements, but they add preparation burden and may interfere with head-free behavioral protocols. Thermal video offers a noncontact alternative by capturing breathing-related temperature changes around the nose and mouth. However, in head-free marmosets, these cues are weak, localized, and easily corrupted by head motion, pose changes, and fur. Here we show that this setting exposes a broader challenge in video-based physiological sensing: visually reliable tracking does not necessarily imply physiologically valid measurement. Using synchronized thermal video and contact respiration recordings, we show that marmoset respiration is recoverable from thermal video but strongly ROI-sensitive. Under head-free motion, visually plausible nasal tracking can still produce traces inconsistent with contact respiration, demonstrating that tracking is not sensing. We therefore estimate respiratory rate from learned temporal signals extracted from thermal clips and introduce Selective Temporal Attenuation Regularization (STAR). STAR selectively attenuates high-motion thermal discontinuities through local temporal imputation during training, regularizing models toward respiration-consistent dynamics while leaving inference unchanged on raw video. Across multiple deep-learning backbones, STAR improves head-free estimation, reaching 2.23 bpm MAE compared with 20.76 bpm for tracked ROI analysis.

Abstract: Visual token pruning substantially reduces the inference cost of multimodal large language models (MLLMs), yet existing methods are mostly training-free and plug-and-play. They treat the resulting performance drop as an inevitable cost of compression and pursue increasingly complex pruning rules. We rethink this view through two questions: how much of the loss can post-training rescue, and, building on this recovery, how can joint compression push pruning to its limit? We curate a 282K-sample post-training mixture covering general VQA, reasoning, OCR, and grounding, and post-train 36 configurations that drop or merge tokens under a unified protocol. Post-training rescues most of the loss, even for OCR and grounding, and its adaptation gain in closing the gap between the pruned and the dense model grows as the budget tightens. For joint compression, we find that the vision encoder, not the LLM, dominates prefill time in native-resolution MLLMs, so where tokens are removed matters more than how many: keeping 40% of tokens at the encoder runs faster than keeping 10% at the projector. Our best configuration prunes the encoder lightly and the projector aggressively, leaves the LLM intact, and keeps general understanding and reasoning at the dense level. It accelerates the LLM stage by 3.49x but the end-to-end time-to-first-token by only 1.54x, showing that LLM-stage speedups overstate end-to-end gains. We hope these findings offer insights for future research on joint visual token compression. Our data, protocol, and code will be released.

Abstract: Unrolled variational networks achieve state-of-the-art performance for accelerated MRI reconstruction, but standard end-to-end training becomes prohibitively memory intensive as the number and width of cascades increase. We propose a memory-efficient training framework that updates a randomly sampled cascade while retaining supervision from the final reconstruction loss. The error signal is propagated from the end of the chain to the sampled cascade using an activation-light backward pass that avoids storing full chain activations, thereby decoupling training memory from chain length. A time-conditioned network shared across cascades further keeps the parameter count independent of chain length. The backward pass is approximate only through frozen normalization statistics, and a trained exact gradient control shows the approximation leaves reconstruction quality unchanged. The framework remains trainable at chain lengths and cascade widths where standard end-to-end backpropagation exhausts GPU memory. On fastMRI single-coil knee and multi-coil brain reconstruction, our method consistently outperforms other local training approaches. On high-SNR knee slices, our method surpasses E2E-VarNet at $8\times$ and matches it at $4\times$, while on the complete brain validation set it surpasses E2E-VarNet at $8\times$ and achieves comparable performance at $4\times$, with fewer parameters, lower peak memory, and faster per-iteration training. Abstract: Depth boundaries remain a major source of error in stereo matching. For IGEV on Scene Flow, geometric edge pixels account for only 6% of evaluated pixels but about half of the total absolute disparity error. Photometric edges can respond to texture changes without corresponding depth discontinuities, making them unreliable cues for boundary refinement. We propose E3Stereo, which learns geometric edges from disparity supervision and combines these boundary cues with the backbone disparity and image details to predict full-resolution disparity corrections. Abstract: Driver emotion provides an important indication of how drivers perceive and evaluate ongoing traffic situations, making reliable emotion recognition an important capability for effective human-vehicle interaction. Yet, most existing driver emotion recognition approaches rely primarily on in-cabin images and overlook how surrounding traffic interactions shape driver emotional responses. To address this limitation, we propose a traffic-context-aware driver emotion recognition (TADER) framework that integrates in-cabin signals with out-of-cabin traffic scenarios, modeling driver emotions as arising from expectation deviation represented by the discrepancy between predicted and observed vehicle trajectories. The proposed framework comprises two key components: (1) a vision-based base recognizer that extracts spatiotemporal representations from in-cabin facial images to infer driver emotions, and (2) a traffic-context-aware fusion module that quantifies expectation deviation from external traffic scenarios and integrates it into the base recognizer via attention-based embedding fusion. To overcome the scarcity of paired in-cabin and out-of-cabin data, we introduce a transfer learning strategy that transfers a trajectory prediction model trained on large-scale naturalistic datasets to smaller paired datasets. Extensive experiments demonstrate that TADER consistently outperforms state-of-the-art methods across both naturalistic and simulated driving environments by incorporating traffic context information into emotion recognition, resulting in improvements of 5% in accuracy on the AIDE and manD datasets.

The approach addresses five gaps not jointly covered by any existing model: external obstruction encoding including balconies, own building height, variable material reflectances, non-orthogonal room geometries, and regulatory validation. Both are trained on about 20,000 synthetic Radiance-simulated rooms and validated on a held-out synthetic dataset and 102 rooms from 18 real building models. The default-materials model achieves $δ_1$ = 83.4% and MAE = 0.11% DF on synthetic data, and on real buildings predicts BBR (Swedish) compliance area within 4.85 pp MAE (bias +2.31 pp) and TEK17 (Norwegian) mean room DF within 0.304% DF MAE (bias +0.287% DF). The default-materials model is recommended as a replacement for preliminary daylight simulations; the custom-materials variant requires further development before use in final compliance verification.

This project offers a framework for other developers to test different approaches and algorithms. Abstract: Road infrastructure perception is a fundamental component of autonomous driving, as it recovers the structured road elements that define roadway geometry, delimit operational space, and encode traffic regulations. Although individual tasks such as lane detection, drivable-space estimation, traffic-sign recognition, and online map construction have been extensively studied, the field remains fragmented across task definitions, sensing assumptions, output representations, datasets, and evaluation protocols. This survey provides a function-oriented synthesis of road infrastructure perception. We first clarify its scope and organize existing studies into three complementary categories: geometric-structural, functional-spatial, and regulatory-semantic infrastructure. Based on this taxonomy, we review sensing modalities, scene representations, representative methods, benchmarks, and evaluation protocols, emphasizing the field's evolution from image-space recognition toward metric, vectorized, topology-aware, occupancy-based, and rule-grounded scene modeling. We further examine how representation choices affect evaluation and downstream usability, revealing limitations of current benchmarks in relation reasoning, temporal consistency, robustness, and planning-oriented assessment. Finally, we discuss open challenges and future directions toward unified infrastructure representation, open-environment generalization, relation- and rule-aware reasoning, and downstream-oriented evaluation. By connecting tasks, representations, and evaluation under a common functional framework, this survey aims to establish road infrastructure perception as a coherent foundation for map-compatible and behavior-relevant autonomous-driving scene understanding.

Abstract: Simulation is widely used in autonomous-driving validation, but matching task outputs between real and synthetic images does not guarantee that a system relies on the same visual evidence. When reported alongside task-specific output-value context metrics, C-DFF shows that output agreement does not by itself establish mechanism parity. Abstract: We present a principled, analytical framework for segmenting multiple independently moving rigid objects during single camera motion, built directly upon the OWL perception function. OWL encodes two fundamental visual motion cues, perceived looming $L$ and perceived rotation $ω$, into a single closed-form representation of relative 3D structure. A key consequence of this structure is that the unit direction of relative translation $\hat{t}$ is analytically recoverable per image point from $L$ and $ω$ alone. All points belonging to a non-rotating rigid body share the same $\hat{t}$, whereas points on different rigid bodies generally yield distinct values. This furnishes a natural segmentation criterion: clustering per-point $\hat{t}$ estimates partitions the visible scene into independently moving rigid bodies. Building on the 2D complex form of OWL, we derive the framework in 3D using quaternions, for a single moving camera. The approach is parallel, camera rotation-invariant, and requires no training data, no depth information, and no semantic priors.

</details>

Abstract: An image is rarely described only once. Retrieval benchmarks and many collections attach several captions to each picture, yet dual encoders still judge every image-caption pair in isolation. We ask what these neighboring captions contribute when a frozen encoder consults them at inference time. Fusing image similarity with similarity to an image's own captions improves text-to-image R@1 by 3.4 to 16.8 points across nine frozen encoders when the captions come from the same annotators as the queries. The gain falls to 1.9 to 7.2 points when an off-the-shelf captioner writes them, and to at most one point when they come from a different annotation source; a paraphrase-trained text encoder strengthens the text side further. A natural refinement, scoring the part of the image that the captions leave unexplained, appears to add much more under a leave-one-out evaluation. We show that this advantage arises because the correct candidate's supports are built differently from those of the negative candidates, with set size and query removal each contributing about half, and that under a fixed support index the residual reduces to a reweighting of what the captions already provide. On compositional benchmarks, the captions help by matching candidates to human descriptions of the image, without improving image-text grounding. Abstract: When an image and its caption disagree, can a user reliably control which one a vision-language model (VLM) follows? Accuracy alone cannot answer this question, since a model might give the correct answer from parametric knowledge without following the requested evidence. We therefore evaluate modality grounding control through conflict-preserving counterfactual interventions. Specifically, Flip measures whether an initially correct answer follows an edit to the requested modality, while Stay measures whether it remains stable when the competing modality is edited. To learn this control, we build on neologism learning by introducing new words for visual and textual grounding, training only their embeddings while keeping the pretrained model fixed. We also introduce Counterfactual Twin-Margin (CTM) supervision, which uses paired interventions to teach how answer probabilities should change when the requested evidence changes. Across three VLMs on three existing benchmarks and our multi-attribute Ground-CF benchmark, we find that learned modality words improve control over natural-language instructions. Surprisingly, a single learned embedding per modality provides strong lightweight visual control and shows little average degradation on held-out values. LoRA remains strongest overall, although its visual advantage narrows under held-out evaluation. Moreover, counterfactual supervision improves control in several settings where standard supervision is weaker. Finally, we test our method on real-world news video and find that visual control improves when the relevant evidence is made easier to access. Overall, our results show that reliable modality control requires testing not only whether an answer is correct, but whether it continues to follow edits to the modality of choice.

</details>

Abstract: We introduce TrackTok, an efficient video tokenization framework that produces object-centric tokens, i.e., tokens that consistently encode the same semantic objects across time, even as it moves, deforms, or undergoes partial occlusion. Conventional patch-based or existing holistic tokenizers produce token identities often tied to fixed image locations. In contrast, our approach learns temporally persistent object-centric representations that bind visual features across frames into stable semantic units. This enables substantially more compact, continuous latent video representations, reducing the number of tokens required for downstream generative modeling with state-of-the-art flow models, while preserving the semantic structure needed for high-quality synthesis. Our tokenizer offers a favorable quality-efficiency trade-off. TrackTok achieves comparable class-conditional generative FVD on UCF-101 dataset at 8 times fewer latent tokens and better latency, and outperforms existing 1D tokenization baselines on frame prediction task on Kinetics-600 across low token budgets. These results suggest that object-aligned and temporally persistent tokenization is a promising direction for scalable video generation, where reducing latent token counts is critical for efficient modeling. Abstract: Catastrophic forgetting in continual learning (CL) manifests not only as accuracy degradation but also as \emph{explanation drift}: models maintain predictive accuracy while silently shifting attention from diagnostic features to spurious correlations. This work identifies and resolves a previously overlooked structural flaw in explanation-aware CL: pixel-level saliency regularization derives supervision targets from the model's own frozen saliency maps, creating a \emph{self-referential drift loop} that actively reinforces spurious attention across tasks. We introduce \textbf{C$^3$L} (Concept-Consistent Continual Learning), which breaks this loop by anchoring regularization to class-consistent semantic concepts (e.g., ``curved beak'', ``striped pattern'') discovered offline, independent of model state, providing (i) target independence from model drift, and (ii) cross-instance semantic consistency across every instance of a class. We identify that our concept-based objective, combined with approximate LRP relevance conservation, induces a \emph{crowding-out effect}: since concept and background regions compete for the relevance of the same prediction, enforcing high relevance within concept regions implicitly suppresses spurious background correlations without explicit penalization. \method does not require human-annotated concept sets, as it employs a fully automated concept discovery and spatial grounding. Extensive evaluation across five benchmarks and over 20 baselines demonstrates state-of-the-art performance in accuracy, forgetting, explanation quality, spurious correlation mitigation, and out-of-distribution robustness.

</details>

Abstract: World action models have demonstrated promising performance in robot policy learning, yet their high inference latency remains a critical bottleneck for real-world deployment. Recent approaches have sought to improve inference efficiency by reducing test-time future-frame imagination or accelerating action prediction. However, these methods often leverage representations from all video world model layers without explicitly considering their varying relevance to action prediction, leading to redundant computation and suboptimal representation utilization. In this work, we propose TurboWAM, a fast world action model that efficiently aggregates structured world representations conditioned on action predictions. Specifically, TurboWAM introduces a structured world representation aggregation module that adaptively fuses representations from different world model layers according to their relevance to the evolving action denoising state. Furthermore, TurboWAM employs a structured action supervision mechanism that adaptively modulates gradients from action supervision across different world model layers during backward propagation, facilitating world representation learning. During inference, by reducing the number of world model layers executed and caching relevant representations across action denoising steps, TurboWAM substantially reduces computational overhead. Extensive experiments on multiple robot manipulation benchmarks demonstrate that TurboWAM achieves low inference latency while maintaining competitive task success rates compared with state-of-the-art methods.

Abstract: Deep learning facilitates rapid single shot three dimensional (3D) reconstruction using fringe projection profilometry (FPP), but recovering accurate surface height from a single high frequency fringe image remains challenging because both long-range spatial consistency and fine geometric details must be preserved. Convolutional networks are limited in modeling long range dependencies, whereas window-based Transformers establish global interactions only progressively with increasing depth. To address these limitations, a hybrid Mamba Transformer network is proposed for fringe to height regression. The network adopts a Swin based U shaped architecture and introduces visual state space modeling at the intermediate resolution encoder stage. An axial and dilated vision state space (ADVSS) block is further developed to enhance prescan feature encoding through local, dilated, and axial depthwise convolutions while retaining four directional selective scanning. This design combines linear complexity long range modeling with direction aware preservation of local fringe structures. Experiments on a publicly available FPP dataset show that the proposed method outperforms hNet, SwinConvUNet, UHRNet, and HiDNet in mean absolute error(MAE), root mean square error (RMSE), absolute relative error, and structural similarity index measure (SSIM). Using only one deformed fringe image, the proposed method achieves an RMSE of 0.548 mm and an SSIM of 0.9985, reducing RMSE by 65.2% and 59.6% relative to UHRNet and HiDNet, respectively. These results demonstrate the effectiveness of combining selective state space modeling with hierarchical window based attention for accurate single-shot FPP reconstruction.

Abstract: Camera motion recognition requires separating viewpoint changes caused by camera translation, rotation, or zoom from object motion and scene dynamics. Video-language models often rely on semantic cues and struggle with the geometric evidence needed for this task. Joint-Embedding Predictive Architectures such as V-JEPA offer strong spatio-temporal representations, but our experiments suggest that they underrepresent geometric cues in some regions. Under temporal-reversal supervision, V-JEPA improves overall but degrades on several motion families, whereas a weaker geometry-oriented VGGT predictor improves across all families. We refer to these geometrically underrepresented regions as geometric blind spots and propose Fovea-JEPA to identify and address them. Using V-JEPA as the base model and VGGT as a geometry expert, we construct intervention pseudo-labels from their prediction errors to weakly supervise a blind-spot predictor. The predictor produces token-wise response maps, which are aggregated into a clip-level intervention score to guide adaptive fusion of visual and geometric features. This selective intervention introduces complementary geometric information while preserving reliable visual predictions. Experiments on CameraMotionDataset, CameraBench, and ShotBench demonstrate the effectiveness of Fovea-JEPA, with gains of 9.67, 8.67, and 24.38 percentage points over previously reported state-of-the-art baselines, respectively. Abstract: LVLM benchmarks tell us whether a model answers correctly, but not which visual conventions it has retained, nor where those conventions live. Sketches expose this hidden structure. A sketch removes texture, lighting, and photographic context while preserving the strokes humans judged sufficient for recognition. If an LVLM can operate on that abstraction, the surviving signal is structural rather than merely photometric. We introduce a training-free sketch probe with three readouts: temporal part-construction order, spatial attention-based grounding, and the mechanistic layer/head locus of grounding. We evaluate nine LVLMs on part order and five on the attention readouts, over two part-annotated sketch datasets. Three findings emerge. First, LVLM part order tracks human convention: models reproduce canonical orderings where human drawings converge, and become correspondingly variable where human orderings vary. Controls that blank, withhold, and mismatch the sketch locate this convention in the language model. Second, part-level grounding survives the abstraction: attention heads selected from the frozen model with an existing recipe recover part and object regions on object and scene sketches, with no grounding supervision. Third, the grounding signal concentrates in a narrow mid-network band whose location follows the base language model rather than parameter count in the two families we compare, and remains stable across object and scene sketches. The result is a practical diagnostic for sketch-driven LVLMs: no fine-tuning, no auxiliary segmenter, and a layer band that doubles as a head-selection prior, matching or improving grounding mAcc@0.1 in every model and dataset we tested.

</details>

Abstract: Few-step distillation for autoregressive (AR) video generation commonly first initializes an AR student and then refines it with distribution matching distillation (DMD). This refinement process faces two challenges. First, although initialization already yields a reasonably capable student, DMD obtains the refinement direction indirectly by estimating the full student score before forming the teacher-student correction. Second, detached-history training prevents the generator from assigning credit to a current chunk for its influence on subsequent chunks. We introduce Leta, a few-step distillation framework that addresses these challenges with ScoreDelta and FutureCredit. ScoreDelta directly estimates the teacher-student correction from denoising residuals, while FutureCredit learns an auxiliary cross-chunk update signal from vector-Jacobian products (VJPs) of next-chunk teacher residuals. Leta combines these signals into a joint teacher-guided update field in a single forward pass during generator updates. Teacher evaluations and credit VJPs are used only for estimator training. For ScoreDelta, we establish population equivalence to score-difference estimation and a conditional approximation advantage under limited capacity in a fixed-feature linear model. In our controlled experiments, Leta improves over DMD by 0.9 points on VBench and 0.66 on VideoAlign. This advantage persists at a higher generator update frequency (a generator-to-estimator update ratio of G:E=1:1), where Leta improves over DMD by 1.6 on VBench and 0.35 on VideoAlign. Abstract: Indoor IoT cameras can support assisted living and activity-aware spaces, but exporting raw RGB frames raises privacy concerns by exposing facial, clothing, and household details that body-motion tasks often do not require. We fully implement StickFigure and evaluate it on public benchmarks and real-world deployment. Compared with state-of-the-art MCU pose models, StickFigure achieves the highest pose accuracy and downstream HAR utility on both NTU RGB+D and Penn Action, while using 96.5% fewer parameters and 99.3% fewer MACs per frame than RTMPose-S-based baselines, achieving 0.107 s p95 latency on a representative STM32H753 MCU. We believe StickFigure establishes a new operating point for privacy-aware edge vision on massive IoT cameras.

Abstract: Fine-tuning adapts pretrained models to new tasks, but identifying which weight changes support the resulting behavior remains difficult. Weight-space interpretability assigns semantic descriptions to these changes, called fine-tuning deltas. Such descriptions leave open how much task performance depends on the identified updates, particularly when other updates can support the same predictions. We address this gap by pairing reversion, which restores selected parameters to their pretrained values, with retention, which keeps only selected fine-tuning updates. We evaluate Contrastive Language-Image Pretraining (CLIP) models on synthetic financial charts, Flowers102, EuroSAT, and FGVCAircraft with ViT-L/14, and on Flowers102 with ViT-B/16, using a shared recipe that tunes the final six visual blocks. Our contributions are threefold. First, attention value-output (VO) updates alone recover 43-87% of the accuracy improvement, although reverting them leaves chart accuracy unchanged. Cross-entropy loss preserves this contrast on Charts and EuroSAT, while correct-class score margins reveal additional contributions. Second, VO weights recover more loss improvement than all ten equally sized, layer-matched random subsets in each setting. VO and multilayer perceptron (MLP) retentions jointly correct 25-81% of pretrained errors, demonstrating overlapping recovered behavior, which we call operational redundancy. Third, removing semantically selected directions reduces VO-retained recovery more than matched-random removal in all five settings. Together, these results distinguish semantic alignment, removal cost, and retained recovery as complementary evidence about the functional role of fine-tuning updates within the evaluated CLIP recipe.

</details>

Abstract: Optical character recognition (OCR) systems depend strongly on the scale and diversity of their training data. One approach to document OCR first analyzes the page layout and extracts text-line images, which are then transcribed by a recognition model. Multilingual line-level recognition involving Persian, Arabic, and English presents additional challenges because different writing directions, fonts, and styles may coexist within a single line. This paper introduces MuSTEL, a large-scale synthetic multilingual OCR dataset containing more than 14.5 million annotated text-line images, organized into a training partition and predefined small and large test sets. Samples are generated from natural-language corpora, including multilingual content already present in the source texts. The generation framework incorporates capability-aware font selection, within-line font and style changes, mixed-direction text rendering, highlighting, and underlining. A validated repository of 2,009 font files from 711 families supports broad typographic coverage, while varied backgrounds, blur, geometric distortion, skew, stroke width, and image save quality provide diverse document-like appearances. Statistical analysis characterizes the dataset's language composition, transcription lengths, lexical and character-sequence diversity, image dimensions, typography, and rendering conditions. MuSTEL provides a resource for training and evaluating multilingual line-level OCR systems and investigating recognition robustness across languages, writing directions, and visual conditions.

Abstract: While Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) excel at zero-shot reasoning, their spatial capabilities are primarily evaluated through static, passive observation. Moreover, fine-tuning with Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) shows that rewarding view-seeking increases active exploration but lowers overall accuracy, as models over-explore and fail to fuse expanding multi-view contexts across turns. These findings indicate that scaling passive multi-view perception does not intrinsically yield active spatial reasoning, highlighting the need for models that know where to look, how to integrate what they see, and when to stop. Abstract: Crowd barriers are widely used to regulate pedestrian movement during large public events such as pilgrimages. However, fixed barrier layouts often create unnecessary stopping and local congestion even when they successfully control the overall crowd flow. Finding better barrier configurations is difficult because there is no dataset that provides the best barrier placement for a given crowd scene. We address this challenge by introducing a simulation-driven reinforcement learning framework that learns barrier configurations without requiring labelled training data. Starting from a segmented crowd scene, we construct a realistic simulation environment that reproduces the observed pedestrian distribution. This environment is then used to train a Soft Actor-Critic (SAC) agent that continuously adjusts barrier positions to improve pedestrian movement. Unlike supervised approaches the proposed framework allows the agent to explore barrier configurations that have never been observed in real data. We evaluate the learned policy under unseen numbers of pedestrians. The learned policy consistently outperforms the existing manually designed barrier configuration by reducing pedestrian stops while maintaining effective flow regulation. These results demonstrate that combining computer vision crowd simulation and reinforcement learning provides an effective framework for optimizing crowd control infrastructure.

Abstract: Instruction-based image editors built on diffusion transformers run tens of denoising steps. We ask how a high speed-up target should be split between them. On Qwen-Image-2.1, freezing does more than remove tokens: it lowers the cost of every step, so the step cache can reach the same overall speed-up with a conservative threshold that keeps its error small. A per-step cost model fitted on development runs predicts held-out latency with a median error of 4.0% and reproduces the speed ceilings of the region methods we re-implemented (2.73$\times$ predicted vs. 2.70$\times$ measured for RegionE). Driving frozen tokens to the model's one-step $x_0$ estimate beats returning them to the reference image on every development sample, and in 90% of measured cases the composed error stays below the sum of the two individual errors. The resulting method, FreezePlan, keeps five full steps and gives the rest of the budget to TaylorSeer. On a pre-registered test split it runs 5.05$\times$ faster end to end at 29.8 dB PSNR to the original output, with no significant drop in a VLM judge score; TaylorSeer at 5.02$\times$ reaches 23.5 dB. The curve extends to 5.99$\times$ at 28.0 dB. Swapping in the region selectors of prior methods under our schedule lowers median PSNR by only 0.5--0.6 dB, which places the gain in the schedule and budget split rather than in the selector.

Abstract: Open-vocabulary instance segmentation (OVIS) provides a promising solution for marine perception by recognizing and segmenting categories beyond predefined label spaces. Existing OVIS methods usually rely on vision-language alignment, where visual region features are matched with textual category embeddings to recognize both seen and unseen classes. However, this alignment assumption becomes fragile in underwater environments. Color attenuation, scattering, turbidity, and low contrast distort visual appearance, causing region features to deviate from their corresponding semantic concepts. As a result, the similarity between degraded visual features and textual classifiers becomes unreliable, which particularly weakens the recognition of unseen underwater categories that cannot be corrected by category-specific supervision. To address these issues, we propose GeoPrompt, a geometry-conditioned prompt learning framework for underwater open-vocabulary instance segmentation. Our key insight is that geometric structure is more stable than visual appearance in degraded underwater scenes. Specifically, GeoPrompt introduces a geometry-guided visual adaptation module (GVAM) to extract depth-derived structural priors and inject them into multi-scale visual features. It further develops a geometry-conditioned prompt learning (GCPL) that generates geometry-aware textual classifiers from structural representations. These geometry-aware classifiers are integrated with fixed and image-conditioned textual classifiers to improve open-vocabulary semantic alignment. Experiments on the MARIS-OpenIS benchmark show that GeoPrompt achieves state-of-the-art performance and brings consistent improvements on underwater open-vocabulary instance segmentation.

</details>

Abstract: On-policy distillation (OPD) enables flow-matching models to acquire complementary capabilities from specialized teachers through supervision on student-generated trajectories. However, different evaluation dimensions, such as aesthetics and OCR accuracy, and different tasks, such as image generation and editing, emphasize different aspects of the denoising process, suggesting that their effective supervision may occur at different stages. Existing methods typically select supervision states from predefined timestep distributions, which may overlook these phase-dependent differences and incur unnecessary computational overhead. This motivates two critical timing decisions: when to adaptively query teachers for effective supervision based on the evolving generative process, and when to skip the remaining rollout to avoid redundant computation.
  In this paper, we introduce PhaseOPD, a phase-aware query-and-skip framework that identifies generative phases online from revisions of predicted-clean latents, using existing student velocity predictions without additional model evaluations. For each task-assigned teacher, PhaseOPD selects a supervision state based on its phase preference and skips the unused rollout suffix once all query decisions are finalized, preserving the single-query velocity-distillation objective. On SD3.5-M, PhaseOPD executes only 5.56 of 16 rollout positions per local batch, reducing training time by 41.3% relative to DanceOPD while improving Aesthetic, GenEval, and OCR scores.

Abstract: Aesthetic image cropping (AIC) aims to improve image composition and aesthetic quality by removing extraneous or distracting content. Over the past decade, AIC methods have evolved from specialized vision models to multimodal large language models(MLLMs). Reliable evaluation that reflects human preferences is essential for benchmarking AIC methods and guiding model optimization. However, the current AIC evaluation relies largely on geometry-based metrics such as intersection over union (IoU), as well as image aesthetic assessment(IAA) models. Although inconsistencies with human preferences have long been observed, the robustness and reliability of these evaluation criteria remain underexplored. To bridge this gap, we first construct CropBench, a large-scale benchmark comprising 106,995 human preference pairs for 1,019 images, with each candidate set containing the original image and candidate crops produced by 14 AIC methods. A systematic study on CropBench reveals that the existing criteria, ranging from IoU to off-the-shelf IAA assessment models, only exhibit limited alignment with human preferences. Building upon CropBench, we further propose CropReward, an MLLM-based reward model that evaluates a candidate crop by comparing with the original uncropped image and outputs a score indicating explicit aesthetic improvement or degradation relative to the original. In addition, we employ CropReward to construct CropReward-300k, the largest AIC dataset to date, and further develop CropAlign, a preference-aligned AIC model trained solely with CropReward supervision, without human-annotated crop boxes. Experimental results demonstrate that CropReward aligns with human preferences significantly better than existing criteria. CropAlign also surpasses the state-of-the-art AIC methods, demonstrating the advantage of CropReward in guiding AIC model optimization.

Abstract: High-resolution 3D Gaussian Splatting training increases pixel and Gaussian-tile intersection counts, expanding rasterization caches that coexist with projection and gradient state at peak memory. A complete-view update aggregates gradients across the view; each region's forward and backward caches can be released after its contribution is collected. BoundedGS separates rasterization and projection backward passes and bounds the active local rasterization working set according to its measured load. Candidate-indexed projection and compact gradient storage address the view-level peaks exposed by this change, while CPU-assisted structure rebuilding and bounded image supply manage resources outside rendering. In a resolution sweep at a fixed count of about 6M Gaussians, the measured rendering working set stays within 1.073-1.077 GiB from 1K to 16K; at 11K, a warm pass takes 19.79 seconds versus 21.76 for full-frame execution. Across five native 5K outdoor scenes, full training reduces the mean rendering working set from 9.97 to 1.76 GiB (82.3%), retains similar reconstruction quality, and shortens training time from 36.7 to 33.9 minutes.

Abstract: Scalable 3D Gaussian Splatting allows one scene representation to serve different Gaussian-count budgets, yet its quality-budget curve may lag behind models optimized separately for each budget. Reducing the number of Gaussians changes both the selected members and the reconstruction tasks carried by the survivors; parameters inherited from a high-budget model may fit a low-budget representation poorly. We propose ParetoGS, which describes member changes and parameter evolution with a small set of adapted budget anchors and decodes intermediate budgets without separate optimization at every requested count. ParetoGS alternates pruning and fixed-member adaptation to build nested anchors with persistent identities, and encodes their parameter changes relative to the full model in a compact residual atlas. At decoding, it interpolates the states of shared members and gradually introduces the rendering contributions of new members, connecting sparse anchors into a continuous-budget representation. Across 13 scenes, ParetoGS attains better average PSNR, SSIM, and LPIPS with fewer Gaussians than the compared prefix methods at 10% of each method's own full model. From a shared full model, it also outperforms FlexGS-imp at its trained 1%, 5%, and 10% budgets and NanoGS-imp at the 1% and 0.1% budgets. On Garden, its non-anchor quality approaches an empirical reference from independently optimized GNS models at higher retention ratios and exceeds it at some lower-budget points. Across 13 scenes, encoding reduces mean additional state from 390.0 to 85.7 MB with an average PSNR change of 0.01 dB.

Abstract: 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) has emerged as a promising approach for fast, high-quality novel-view rendering. However, its large parameter size remains a bottleneck for storage and transmission. We propose RacerGS, a compact 3DGS compression framework that achieves scene reconstruction at significantly reduced storage sizes with preserved fidelity. To obtain compact and spatially coherent features, we design a hash projection model that projects the anchor hash encodings into compact context features. Additionally, we introduce a FiLM-modulated autoregressive GRU context model that utilizes causal dependencies among anchor feature groups and uses recurrent hidden states to refine entropy-model parameters, improving bitrate efficiency. Furthermore, we adaptively entropy-code quantized anchor-attribute residuals relative to their context-predicted means, leveraging spatial consistency to reduce symbol entropy. Overall, RacerGS achieves more than 150x compression over vanilla 3DGS and 28x over Scaffold-GS, while providing comparable rendering quality. Extensive experiments across five datasets demonstrate that RacerGS outperforms prior 3DGS compression methods in both storage reduction and rate-distortion performance.

Abstract: We propose a novel method for robust point cloud registration with only one tunable parameter. Our method, dubbed TLP, uses truncated $L_p$ functions with different values of $p$ to assign priority scores to correspondences, select the hypothesis, and refine the final model. By leveraging the robustness of truncated functions, TLP effectively handles the high outlier rates commonly encountered in real-world 3D feature matching. We also exploit GPU parallelization to evaluate hypotheses efficiently, enabling registration from thousands of correspondences in under a second on average. With only one tunable parameter, our method offers greater practicality than multi-parameter methods that require dataset-specific tuning of several parameters. Evaluations on public benchmark datasets demonstrate that TLP achieves higher accuracy than state-of-the-art single-parameter methods. Furthermore, it outperforms two widely used multi-parameter methods on three of the four evaluated datasets while maintaining competitive runtime.

</details>

When post-training emphasizes one readout, improvements can therefore concentrate on that interface while transfer to others remains weak or even degrades; we call this observed specialization interface lock-in. ChronoReplay addresses it by replaying heterogeneous spatial supervision as chronologically ordered episodes, retaining source-native targets, and balancing supervision across families. Under the same observation access, a single ChronoReplay checkpoint improves OVO-S-Bench, OST-Bench, and UCS-Bench together, including a 13.41-point gain on OST-Bench, and achieves the highest three-benchmark mean among the supervision settings compared here. Same-data controls further separate the ingredients of this transfer: answer serialization redistributes gains across OST and UCS, family-balanced learning improves over ordinary joint SFT and natural turn weighting, and chronological replay exceeds a shifted-order control by about 2.41 OST points across two paired seeds. Adaptive frame selection then changes evidence access while keeping the trained model fixed, providing an additional inference-time gain at lower visual cost. Streaming spatial performance depends on what distinctions supervision preserves, how heterogeneous histories are organized during learning, and what causal evidence inference makes available at test time.

Abstract: Conveyor belts are vital arteries in continuous bulk material handling, particularly in heavy process industries such as cement manufacturing. Undetected structural anomalies (longitudinal tears, holes, degraded patch repairs, and impact punctures) cause catastrophic belt snapping, costly downtime, and severe safety hazards. While deep learning offers high accuracy, existing models rely on power-hungry workstation GPUs, hindering practical deployment at remote industrial edge points. In this paper, we propose, implement, and validate an end-to-end, low-power edge AI vision system for real-time conveyor belt defect detection deployed on a Raspberry Pi 5 accelerated by a Google Coral Edge TPU coprocessor. The system leverages an optimized YOLOv11 nano (YOLOv11n) architecture trained on a multi-source industrial corpus exceeding 3,000 augmented samples from an operational cement plant in West Java, open-source datasets, and automated captures. The model undergoes post-training full integer quantization (INT8) compiled for systolic tensor execution. Experimental results demonstrate robust detection capability, achieving precision of 0.742, recall of 0.730, and mAP@0.5 of 0.764 across four defect classes. Deployed on-site in an IP-rated enclosure interfaced with an RTSP PoE industrial camera, the system achieves stable throughput of 30-40 FPS at 320x320 resolution with an end-to-end latency of 70-90 ms. Operational profiling under harsh clinker dust confirms thermal equilibrium at 55 +/- 5 deg C on the host CPU and ~65 deg C on the Edge TPU with zero frame loss. This demonstrates that edge AI bridges the gap between high-accuracy computer vision and cost-effective predictive maintenance in continuous manufacturing.

</details>

Abstract: Object re-identification requires embeddings that are robust to intra-class variation in viewpoint, illumination, and occlusion, yet preserve sharp separation between visually similar identities. The hardest errors arise precisely where confusable identities meet, a region that pairwise losses shape only implicitly. We present PBR-ReID, a prototype-based framework that makes this inter-class geometry an explicit learning target. Each identity is represented by a learnable prototype jointly optimized with a DiNOv3 backbone and the resulting prototype similarity matrix reveals, at every training step, which identities are most confusable. On this signal we build Boundary Repulsion MixUp (BRM), a geometry-aware regularizer that converts standard MixUp's weakness into a discriminative signal. Rather than interpolating samples across confusable identities, which blurs the boundaries that matter most, BRM constructs an explicit boundary proxy between each confusable pair and repels their features away from it, converting boundary ambiguity into a discriminative signal. We adopt a two-stage training protocol consisting of a prototype warmup phase followed by EMA-stabilized joint fine-tuning. Extensive experiments on person and vehicle ReID benchmarks demonstrate strong performance, with thorough ablations confirming each component's contribution. Code is available at https://github.com/psaltaath/pbr-reid.

Attention is factorised over the joints and the frames of a motion and carries a spatial--temporal encoding designed for skeleton graphs of any topology: queries and keys are rotated across joints by a heat-kernel coordinate of the skeleton graph, which is defined for every tree and does not change with the order of its joints or the eigenbasis of its Laplacian, and across frames by a rotary encoding of frame order. Abstract: 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) has become a standard representation for real-time novel-view synthesis. Yet, its quality-efficiency trade-off still relies heavily on adaptive density control. Existing cloning, splitting, and pruning rules use proxy signals, such as position gradients, opacity, and image-space error. Although effective in practice, these signals do not directly answer the structural question behind density control: where should new Gaussians be allocated to reduce the multi-view reconstruction loss, and which existing primitives can be pruned with minimal penalty. To address this, we propose first-variation birth-death control for 3DGS, a principled approach that replaces heuristic structural decisions with a variational score derived from the reconstruction loss. At each structural update, we freeze the current Gaussian population, attach auxiliary mass coordinates to each primitive, and differentiate the multiview loss with respect to these coordinates. We demonstrate that this score is a coordinate of the first variation of the Splat Regression Model, giving a rigorous local descent surrogate for birth-death moves: high-score Gaussians are selected as birth sites, while low-score, low-opacity Gaussians are selected for removal. Across standard 3DGS benchmarks, integrating this score into 3DGS-MCMC yields state-of-the-art reconstruction quality and outperforms existing methods, delivering the most significant gains under tight Gaussian budgets. Ultimately, our work provides compelling evidence that gradient-based variational metrics offer a principled alternative to proxy heuristics in 3DGS.

Abstract: Traditional autoencoders rely on static network architectures to learn latent representations. Recently we suggested a dynamic architecture inspired by Hebbian plasticity, enabling topological self-organization among convolutional feature maps (R. Koç et al., Proceedings of the SIU, 2026). Early implementations of this paradigm infer relationships among feature maps using global average pooling and model their interactions through scalar-valued connections. This approach falls short to fully exploit the underlying spatial information, embedded in input images. Furthermore, abrupt transitions between different loss functions and training dynamics can introduce representation shocks, leading to unstable feature learning. In this paper, we introduce a Spatially-Aware Self-Organizing Graph Autoencoder (S-LooM) to address these structural bottlenecks. By using spatial cross-correlation, and spatial kernels, S-LooM establishes dynamic connections among feature maps. We replace static scalar message passing of autoencoders with learnable spatial kernels and eliminate high-frequency deconvolutional artifacts observed in decoders using nearest-neighbor upsampling. Via a soft-blending training curriculum, the latent topology aligns smoothly during training. Our experiments show that S-LooM yields a more stable topology, compared to the state of the art dynamic architectures. Meaning, the graph structure that emerge has more resistance to feature entanglement and noise. It forms functional communities responsible for visual attributes, such as illumination, shape and texture even under high connection densities.

</details>

Abstract: Non-rigid structure from motion (NRSfM) aims to recover camera motion and time-varying 3D shape from tracked 2D image measurements. Recent high-performing methods show that shape-space factorization remains highly effective for this task. These methods often use costly solvers for two key steps. First, a semidefinite programming (SDP) cone solver to perform Euclidean correction. Next, an alternating direction method of multipliers (ADMM) solver to recover the low-rank shape. These steps often scale poorly to long sequences and dense point tracks. We propose a fast, scalable NRSfM solver that avoids these costly steps. For rotation estimation, we introduce a differentiable Euclidean correction that directly optimizes small factors for each sequence. A row-polar projection then maps each estimated camera block to the closest row-orthonormal matrix. For shape estimation, we introduce an unrolled spectral-proximal solver. It combines reprojection-gradient steps with weighted singular-value shrinkage in the reshuffled shape domain. This solver further uses FISTA-style restart and continuation. The spectral shrinkage operator itself is classical, but we embed it in a fixed differentiable solver, while preserving NRSfM's geometric constraints. The proposed approach is category-agnostic, requires no external training data, and maps naturally to modern accelerator-friendly linear algebra primitives. Across sparse, dense, noisy, missing-data, and long-sequence benchmarks, our solver achieves substantial speedups with competitive reconstruction accuracy, including 7x-1000x runtime improvements on dense sequences with 28,887 tracked points.

</details>

Abstract: Federated continual learning (FCL) has attracted considerable interest by enabling local clients to continually acquire knowledge from new tasks. However, most FCL methods assume that the global server has prior knowledge of the arrival times and number of categories for new tasks, which is unrealistic in the real world. Furthermore, they neglect the importance of positive inter-task semantic relationships when learning new tasks and are significantly affected by irrelevant relationships from old tasks, thereby exacerbating catastrophic forgetting. To resolve the above challenges, we develop a novel Task Relevance-driven Prompt Expansion (TRPE) model. Specifically, to eliminate the reliance on prior knowledge of new tasks, we propose an out-of-distribution detector to identify the arrival of new tasks within local clients, along with a radius-adaptive DBSCAN to infer the quantity of categories in new tasks on the proxy server. Moreover, to explore positive inter-task relationships, we calculate the affinity between the original gradient for learning new task and its gradient orthogonal to the feature space of old tasks to evaluate semantic relevance between old and new tasks from a global perspective. Then, we use this global task relevance to determine the key-prompt pair for new tasks and identify highly related old tasks. After concatenating their prompts for training, we can leverage such positive inter-task relations to improve the continual learning of newly arriving tasks. To balance the learning of new tasks and the forgetting of old ones, a soft orthogonality constraint is applied to the local key and prompt gradients. Experiments spanning diverse datasets and backbones confirm the efficacy of our TRPE model.

</details>

Abstract: Vision--language models applied to medical question answering can reach correct diagnoses without using the diagnostic image, inferring instead from the clinical vignette. We ask whether this is visible in how \emph{firmly} a model holds its answer: does a model defend a diagnosis derived from a blank placeholder as tenaciously as one derived from a real medical image? Crossing an image-substitution protocol with two levels of adversarial pressure on 60 Italian State Examination items, we find no evidence that ungrounded verdicts are more fragile. In a paired item-level analysis, fabricated consensus (L4) overturns 94.4\% of verdicts with the real image and 98.1\% without ($Δ= +0.037$; one-sided 95\% upper bound $+0.105$); contentless doubt (L1) overturns 62.5\% and 51.8\% ($Δ= -0.107$, 95\% CI $[-0.209, -0.001]$), the point estimate running opposite to the direction a grounding detector requires. The two levels nonetheless produce opposite \emph{dynamics}: under L4 the model flips once, always to the exact option argued for (110/110 first-turn flips), and then freezes, averaging 1.9 verdict changes over ten turns; under L1 it never settles, averaging 7.4 changes. Instability under contentless doubt tracks intrinsic item ambiguity rather than evidence availability, and so does not serve as a grounding signal. Flips are almost entirely corrupting (99/100 across both levels). The model is not unaware of the missing evidence---it flags the blank image at baseline on 35 of 60 items and abstains on 4--6---yet those abstentions do not survive contact with pressure. None of the epistemic signals a user could plausibly consult distinguishes a grounded diagnosis from a confabulated one.

Abstract: Visual-token compression is usually evaluated by clean accuracy and efficiency, but these averages do not reveal failures that compression introduces under an image shift where the uncompressed model stays correct. We introduce VTC-AUDIT, which estimates this incremental risk only on pairs where the full model is correct on both images and the compressed model is correct on the clean image. The four arms then cross two binary choices: which image selects the retained token positions, and which image supplies the visual features. We audit three open 8-9B vision-language models on four tasks and eight pre-specified shifts. We evaluate two training-free compressors---hard-pruning DivPrune and prune-and-merge ApET---at a matched 25% retained-position budget, producing 708,000 inference records. Reselecting positions on the shifted image, rather than reusing the clean-image positions, raises the failure rate in all six model-compressor settings. The increase is 3.04-9.60 percentage points for DivPrune and 2.05-4.20 percentage points for ApET, with every 95% interval above zero. Repeating the audit on a separately resampled second manifest reproduces all six increases. In a pooled monitor audit across the four benchmarks, support Jaccard instability fails the practical AUROC criterion of 0.65 in all six settings, whereas clean compressed-model uncertainty clears it. Thus, support turnover is a weak ranking signal under the audited benchmark mixture, while clean compressed-model uncertainty is substantially more informative. These results suggest that future visual-token compression evaluations should report conditional reliability alongside clean accuracy and efficiency.

</details>

Abstract: Adapting general-purpose vision-language models (VLMs) to medical visual question answering (VQA) is valuable for helping clinicians read images and make diagnoses, but annotated data are scarce and provide only short answers, without reasoning annotations. Supervised fine-tuning (SFT) fits these labels but overfits to them and amplifies their biases, eroding out-of-domain (OOD) generalization. Reinforcement learning (RL) better preserves generalization but never uses the gold answer as a training target: GRPO-style algorithms learn from the contrast between correct and incorrect answers within a group of sampled responses, so all-correct or all-wrong groups give no correctness signal. Existing SFT-RL hybrids either apply SFT and RL in separate stages or switch between them per question, or need reasoning traces from experts or stronger models, which medical VQA data lack. We propose GRASP, which combines RL and SFT in every update using only short answers: RL lets the model explore its own reasoning, SFT teaches the gold answer after that reasoning to supply the direct supervision RL lacks, and their balance adapts to how well the model answers each question. Tuning only about 1% of the parameters with LoRA, GRASP outperforms all compared training objectives both in domain and on OOD open-ended questions across Qwen3-VL-8B, InternVL3-8B and Gemma 3-4B. On an OOD benchmark spanning eight imaging modalities, GRASP-trained InternVL3-8B and Qwen3-VL-8B beat all 10 compared general and medical VLMs in open-ended accuracy, including models trained on up to 2,600 times more data or with over 250 times more trainable parameters. We release MedTerse, a dataset of 12.9K short-answer questions, and our code at https://github.com/sea-comet/GRASP-MedVQA.

</details>

Abstract: Contrastive vision-language models such as CLIP typically rely on independently parameterized transformer blocks across depth. We investigate the extent to which these blocks can instead be reused recurrently while preserving the transferable capabilities of CLIP. We study recursive transformer backbones in which a small set of $N$ transformer blocks is applied $M$ times per forward pass. By holding $N \cdot M$ fixed to the depth of a standard ViT-B, our models preserve computational depth while progressively increasing parameter sharing. We consider two recursive CLIP model families: rCLIP, where only the vision encoder is recursive, and r$^2$CLIP, where recurrence is also introduced in the text encoder. We study these models under both direct contrastive training and through knowledge distillation from a strong ViT-B/16 teacher pretrained on DFN-2B, using the substantially smaller CC3M and CC12M datasets. We evaluate the resulting models on zero-shot classification and cross-modal retrieval, and further study their adaptability through few-shot learning, visual grounding, and concept unlearning. Our results show that CLIP can tolerate substantial recurrent block reuse, although its effect depends on the encoder and downstream task. Recurrence also provides parameter efficiency by construction, since fewer distinct transformer blocks need to be stored and adapted. Our results establish recurrent block reuse as a practical design space for vision-language models and highlight its implications for knowledge transfer, downstream adaptation, and parameter efficiency.

</details>

Abstract: Most action-conditioned video world models remain limited to a single player, while existing multiplayer models support only small, fixed groups of agents. We introduce FlockWorld, an artificial-life environment built on Reynolds's Boids algorithm for studying population-scale multi-agent video world models. Specifically, FlockWorld renders synchronized egocentric crops, assigns distinct colors to preserve camera-agent identity across views, exposes rule-derived accelerations as actions, and lets the number of camera agents be configured independently of the size of the surrounding population. We further introduce FlockDiT, an action-conditioned latent diffusion transformer trained with flow matching on FlockWorld data to jointly generate synchronized egocentric videos for multiple agents. Our results suggest that joint generation benefits agent detection at density-matched settings and that diffusion forcing improves rollout fidelity. Together, FlockWorld and FlockDiT provide a controlled testbed for multi-agent video generation.

Abstract: Despite advances in automated liver lesion segmentation, a gap remains between image-derived predictions and clinically interpretable radiology reports. Bridging this gap requires quantitative measurements that are both reproducible and traceable. We present an evidence-grounded agentic AI framework that connects liver and lesion segmentation with deterministic quantification and structured reporting. TotalSegmentator-based nnU-Net models delineate the liver, Couinaud segments, and lesions, while computational tools extract volumes, dimensions, counts, and anatomical locations. A large language model (LLM) organizes these measurements into a predefined reporting schema, with numerical consistency checks against source measurements. Abstract: Vision-language anomaly detectors describe normal and abnormal appearance with text, and their gains are commonly credited to the semantic content of these descriptions. This attribution is plausible in the zero-shot setting, but few- and full-shot detectors interpose a trained visual adapter, and in that setting it has not been isolated. Changing a description changes both its meaning and its geometry, so accuracy comparisons cannot separate the two. We ask what a fixed pair of text embeddings, or anchors, contributes to such a detector: semantic content, effective scale, or joint anchor geometry. For a single anchor pair, the answer is effective scale alone. The score reads the anchors only through their difference, and a trained adapter can rotate to follow any replacement direction, leaving that direction, and with it the linguistic content, unidentifiable. We prove that replacement anchors with the same difference-vector length permit exactly the same probability maps and attainable losses. Across seven medical datasets, three vision backbones, both supervision regimes, and three adapter families, scale-matched random and orthogonal anchors track correct prompts; breaking the scale match opens a gap that restoring it closes without text. The same pattern holds in MVFA and when MadCLIP's learned prompts are replaced by a text-free learned scale. When one adapter serves many categories, the answer expands to their joint arrangement. Rotating all category axes together changes nothing, as predicted. For the tested CLIP prompts, these axes are nearly collinear, and meaning-free rotations that spread them apart improve performance on both industrial benchmarks. Even under frozen zero-shot transfer, a random anchor frame transfers as well as the prompt frame: coordinate consistency, not meaning. What these detectors read from their prompts is scale and arrangement, not semantic content.

</details>

Abstract: Recently, point-supervised temporal action localization has attracted increasing attention due to its low annotation cost (only one frame per action instance) and competitive detection performance. However, since point annotations are less complete than proposal annotations, existing methods mainly rely on multiple instance learning for snippet-level classification, which leads to a performance gap compared to fully supervised models. To bridge this gap, we propose P2P-Former, a novel point-to-proposal Transformer-based framework that effectively connects point-level supervision with proposal-level learning for temporal action localization. In this work, we identify two key challenges in transitioning from point-level supervision to full supervision: (1) how to generate reliable proposals as pseudo ground-truth, and (2) how to effectively integrate point-level cues into the fully supervised paradigm. To address the first challenge, we propose a Point-aware Proposal Generation (PPG) strategy that refines the confidence of proposals produced by a base locator using a point-aware importance estimation function, and then performs optimal matching between point annotations and candidate proposals to select reliable proposals as pseudo ground-truth. To tackle the second challenge, we introduce a Point-guided Semantic Enhancement (PSE) module that aggregates prototypes from point-annotated features and injects them into snippet features through Transformer-based interactions. Moreover, we design a Point-to-Proposal Constraint Loss (PPCL) that enforces each predicted proposal to include the annotated point, thereby encouraging more accurate localization. Extensive experiments on the THUMOS14 and ActivityNet-1.3 datasets demonstrate that our method outperforms several existing state-of-the-art approaches.

</details>

Abstract: Driving video captured through a windshield violates the world-space consistency assumed by 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS): the road scene exhibits parallax while cabin reflections remain attached to the camera, causing standard 3DGS to embed reflected content into the reconstructed scene. We introduce RIDE-Splat, a pipeline for reflection-free 3D reconstruction from posed driving video without clean images, reflection masks, or pretrained networks. RIDE-Splat assigns world-consistent transmission through the windshield to a 3DGS scene and the dominant camera-attached reflection to one shared, signed RGB image. It builds the clean reconstruction in two passes. First, it learns the 3D geometry and the signed reflection image, representing both added radiance and transmission attenuation. Then, it uses the reflection-removed training images to fit a clean 3DGS model. At inference, the reflection image is discarded, preserving the standard 3DGS rendering path. We also introduce the RIDE-Splat Dataset, the first 3D reconstruction benchmark for reflection removal from driving video -- with real driving scenes, physical windshield reflections in training views, and separately captured non-reflective evaluation images. Across the RIDE-Splat Dataset, XLD, and Wayve, RIDE-Splat outperforms 3DGS, DC-Gaussian, and RobustSplat on all nine aggregate metrics, improving PSNR by up to 3.59 dB and reducing LPIPS by up to 0.0876. We achieve the highest metrics in 38 out of 42 per-scene comparisons and removes windshield reflections that remain visible in the baselines while preserving the transmitted scene.

</details>

Abstract: Vision-language models are shifting from offline passive understanding toward online proactive interaction. However, prevailing methods largely rely on explicit requests while miss the implicit user intent (e.g., "where should I click next?" while a user navigate a website), meanwhile fail to update the state simultaneously as user requests might update continuously. These limitations make them less suited to intent-aware interaction in practical. To address these challenges, we introduce StreamVibe-Bench, a new benchmark serving as testbed for user intent interaction, with 4.1K evaluation samples and 85K training samples spanning explicit, implicit, and dynamic intents. Building on this benchmark, we develop StreamVibe-VL, a streaming proactive model that infers implicit user intents from continuous observations and dynamically tailors its interactions as these intents evolve. We further introduce StreamVibe-Harness, which maintains user intents as persistent states with a fleixble lifecycles via a hierarchical memory to track the latest user intent and environmental state. Our experiments reveal three key findings: (i) StreamVibe-VL preserves strong general video understanding while improving response timing and answer quality by 10.7% over other baselines; (ii) existing proactive models remain limited in understanding user intent and deciding when to respond, especially under implicit and dynamic intents; and (iii) StreamVibe-Harness further improves response consistency and adaptation over sustained interaction, resulting in a 12.3% gain in proactive interaction.
