Abstract: Automatic Speech Recognition (ASR) models such as OpenAIs Whisper have substantially improved speech recognition quality but remain expensive to fine-tune, especially when large amounts of transcribed audio are available. This makes it challenging for many practitioners to adapt these models to new languages or domains. In this work, we introduce an embedding-based subset selection approach for selecting smaller training sets for ASR fine-tuning. Using Whispers pretrained encoder, we map each audio sample into a fixed-dimensional embedding space and apply a greedy k-center algorithm to select diverse and representative subsets of the training data. We evaluate this approach on both Hebrew and Danish, showing that k-center selection often improves upon random sampling, particularly at smaller subset sizes. An analysis of lexical diversity reveals that k-center subsets capture a broader range of linguistic content than random subsets of the same size, which may help explain their observed effectiveness. Overall, our results demonstrate that embedding-based subset selection provides a practical strategy for ASR subset selection in the two evaluated languages, with the clearest recognition benefits observed at smaller sample budgets.

Abstract: Academic revision presents a governance problem when legitimate writing objectives conflict. Writing-process and social-science research show that revision is recursive and that wording carries epistemic and disciplinary commitments. Those literatures identify important constraints but do not settle which objective should prevail when concision, outlet form or stylistic variation conflicts with evidence and claim boundaries. This paper models revision as an admissibility problem under ordered constraints. Task-relative invariants, contextual constraints and mutable presentation choices define the admissible space; a claim-preservation contract links evidence and judgement to an authorised claim boundary. Four governance propositions specify preservation, precedence, context and sufficiency. The Academic Writing Filter implements the model by screening candidate revisions; it is not a universal definition or classifier of good academic writing. The contribution is a technology-independent mechanism for changing presentation without silently changing the research argument.

</details>

Abstract: Optical Character Recognition (OCR) is essential for digitizing historical archives, yet standard evaluation metrics such as Character Error Rate (CER) do not necessarily reflect whether meaningful information, such as names, dates, and identifiers, is preserved. In this work, we investigate entitylevel analysis of OCR errors as a complementary perspective for evaluating OCR quality in historical documents. Using a publicly available benchmark of 175 documents from the Berrutti Archive, augmented with 5,209 manually annotated entity mentions that will be released upon acceptance, we compare sequence-recognition OCR systems, OCR-specialized vision-language models, and general-purpose vision-language models. Beyond global transcription accuracy, we analyze entity preservation and recoverability under varying character-level edit tolerances. Our experiments show that OCR rankings depend on the evaluation criterion: while Qwen 3.6 achieves the highest entity coverage across all tested tolerances, several systems with lower exact-match coverage recover substantially when small errors are tolerated, suggesting that many of their errors correspond to localized character-level deviations rather than fundamentally incorrect entity transcriptions. Conversely, some systems remain unable to recover many entities even under relaxed matching. These findings show that OCR quality cannot be fully characterized by aggregate transcription metrics alone and highlight the importance of evaluating how OCR errors affect the preservation and recoverability of meaningful information.

Abstract: Energy-based models have shown promising properties for domain-specific tasks but remain challenging in modeling general-purpose scenarios. To bridge this gap, we design energy-steered models (ESMs) for foundational language modeling. ESMs integrate energy steering and token generation within a single architecture to leverage the energy-guided search to refine token generation. Specifically, ESMs first optimize a latent state over the energy landscape, then leverages the refined latent state for token generation. By performing such energy-guided pre-training on large-scale corpora, ESMs learn generally effective energy landscape where favorable tokens correspond to lower energy values. To explore the scaling behavior of ESMs, we pre-train ESMs on three large-scale corpora, then evaluate across 26 downstream tasks spanning a broad range of domains. Our results demonstrate that ESMs have promising scaling behaviors with increasing model size and training data, and outperform GPTs on most downstream tasks. These findings highlight ESMs as a promising architecture for large-scale pre-training and general purpose problem solving. We fully release the model weights and training infrastructure to enable the community to completely reproduce and extend our results.

Abstract: Transformer-based large language models (LLMs) impose substantial computation and memory costs as context sizes increase, motivating methods that compress a context into a shorter representation before inference. Existing soft-prompt compression methods typically use the same Transformer backbone as used in the target model, where producing the compressed representation leads to significant latency overheads. This restricts them to the offline setting where context is compressed once and then reused across many queries. We propose the Boa Compressor, a plug-and-play online context compressor built on a Mamba state-space model (SSM) that encodes arbitrary-length context in a single linear-time pass, producing a representation on which we condition a frozen target LLM. The SSM is trained by distillation, matching the next-token logit distribution from the LLM ingesting the full context (as teacher) to the same LLM ingesting the compressed representation (as the student). At equal compression ratios, Boa matches or surpasses the performance of existing state-of-the-art offline compression baselines, while being an order of magnitude faster than Transformer-based implementations, thereby reducing end-to-end latency rather than adding compression time overhead. We demonstrate that Boa also scales well to longer contexts and to compression ratios as aggressive as 16x. By enabling effective compression that is cheap enough to run on every query at inference time, Boa becomes a practical and general tool for efficient long-context inference.

</details>

Abstract: Diffusion large language models (dLLMs) enable flexible generation through iterative masked denoising, but incur substantial inference cost because token representations are repeatedly recomputed across denoising steps. Existing cache-based methods reduce this cost by reusing intermediate results across denoising steps and recomputing only a subset of positions at each step, chosen either on a fixed schedule or by heuristic signals of change. Our trajectory experiments instead point to decoding trajectory as the more relevant quantity. Cached generation remains accurate when it stays close to the full-denoising trajectory, with errors concentrated around unmask-order deviations and reduced as longer reference prefixes are recovered. This suggests a more direct principle: limiting representation error helps, but keeping generation on the full-denoising trajectory is a more direct route to accurate decoding. We therefore formulate efficient dLLM inference as trajectory-guided querying. Rather than broadly repairing representation error, we use full-denoising trajectories to supervise which unresolved positions should receive limited fresh computation. Based on this formulation, we introduce TRACEQUERY, a lightweight sparse decoding framework that predicts a small set of near-future trajectory-relevant positions, queries only those candidates, and lets the backbone select which one to commit from their fresh predictions. TRACEQUERY delivers the strongest quality-computation trade-off among cache-based approaches, sustaining high accuracy with substantially fewer fresh token computations, and generalizes across tasks, model variants, and families. Code: https://github.com/fish1928/tracequery.git

</details>

Abstract: Large Language Model (LLM) agents with tool use exhibit inconsistent behavior across independent runs from identical states, calling different tools, producing contradictory outputs, or pursuing divergent strategies. This variability compounds over time and undermines deployment reliability. Existing evaluation metrics primarily measure task correctness via completion-based scores and do not capture consistency in agent behavior across executions. Separately, agent behavior is analyzed either at the token level using probability distributions or at the single-response level using semantic clustering, but neither captures how inconsistency propagates across future turns of a trajectory. We introduce Recursive Semantic Divergence (RSD), an unsupervised trajectory-level consistency metric adapted from bisimulation metrics in reinforcement learning. RSD measures the expected cumulative semantic disagreement between two independent rollouts of the same agent from the same state, using natural language inference to detect logical inconsistency. It is defined recursively over future states and approximated by a neural network trained on independent rollouts. The learned metric estimates future divergence from the current state, providing a consistency score at each turn without requiring the full trajectory to complete. When used as a reward signal for fine-tuning, RSD reduces the variation in task progress from 132% to 91% on tau2-bench and from 90% to 44% on toolsandbox, while improving performance across benchmarks and model scales. Training with RSD as an additional reward signal matches supervised fine-tuning performance with substantially lower variance, without requiring ground-truth labels.

</details>

Abstract: A negotiation assistant can reach a good deal without accurately predicting the other person's response. We ask whether evaluations of social world models test that prediction directly. We audit 113 works published from 1999 to 2026. 9 primary evaluations measure a real person's response to an action selected by the system or experimenter. Only 2 also compare an explicit response forecast with that response. Their predictors use information available when the action is chosen, and the evaluated responses were excluded from fitting; both randomize the intervention. A separate full-text check of 25 language-model works finds no experiment meeting these conditions. The other evaluations test current-state inference, task success, or prediction from recorded actions. We propose a 1-step negotiation experiment that separates these questions. The findings describe the audited sample, not the prevalence of this evaluation gap across the field.

Abstract: Llama-3, Qwen3 and many other open LLMs pre-tokenize text with a regular expression whose word class admits only Unicode letters. Devanagari vowel signs are combining marks, so this regex splits Nepali words at every vowel sign, and models that use it spend 2.6 to 5.8 times as many tokens on Nepali as on the same English text. Merge-based vocabulary extension cannot join the split pieces: for Llama-3 and Qwen3 no number of new merges brings this premium below about 2.2, and ten released extensions already end within 4% of this pre-token floor. We give a small edit to the deployed regex that provably keeps every token id of text without combining marks or Devanagari characters. With 32,000 new merges it brings Nepali to 1.01 times English, about 54% fewer Nepali tokens than standard extension. In small-budget continued pretraining of Llama-3.2-1B and Qwen3-0.6B, the original tokenizer gave the lowest held-out Nepali bits per byte under every recipe we tested. Matching the repair's training steps to standard extension, with about 35% of the data repeated, reversed their order on Qwen but not on Llama. Whether the token savings give a favourable latency and quality trade-off in generation remains untested. We release the tokenizers and code.

Abstract: Scholarly Knowledge Graphs (SKGs) support literature search, scientific discovery, and agent-assisted research. Yet, most approaches to SKG construction pre-specify the knowledge unit to be represented, typically named scholarly entities or contribution-level statements, and retain only the publication content expressible through that unit. This choice produces two dominant yet distinct paradigms: concept-centric SKGs that preserve entities and local relations, and statement-centric SKGs that capture high-level discourse in sentences. In both cases, the graph reflects a chosen extraction target rather than how a paper constructs and communicates knowledge. We introduce SUDO, the Scholarly Upper Discourse Ontology, which defines how knowledge elements are organized and related to form a paper's research narrative, namely its scholarly discourse. Using linguistic principles that generalize across fields and tasks, SUDO enables each publication's scholarly discourse to be constructed from the bottom up as a coherent, source-grounded representation. Applied consistently across papers, the same structure connects their research narratives into a global scholarly discourse without discarding local context or provenance. We demonstrate SUDO's capabilities across a comprehensive suite of scholarly knowledge-modeling use cases and performance improvements on a downstream Large Language Model (LLM)-based scientific QA task.

Abstract: If your brain were connected directly to someone else's, would you still know which thoughts were yours? We build a functional version with two copies of one language model: each holds a private rule and code word, and one copy, the receiver, also attends to its partner's internal memory. The receiver took its partner's assignment as its own. Asked which rule it had been assigned, it named its partner's in 97% of answers when the partner's memory was weighted double (none without the link), and its open reports called the partner's code word its own, never the partner's. Ownership did not follow how well the receiver could tell what its partner had been assigned. At equal weight, the link made the receiver take its partner's rule in about half of its answers, and a steering vector matched on access gain in none; a text message introduced as the partner's was read correctly and never taken as the receiver's own. Writing the partner's name into its memory did not help, and cutting the link restored the receiver's own assigned rule. In this model, content laid over the receiver's own memory defaults to "mine", so systems that let models exchange internal states must test ownership as well as transfer, and a bridge between brains would need ground truth from outside the subject.

</details>

Abstract: Next-token prediction (NTP) rewards only the token that occurs next, penalizing every alternative. But natural language often offers many ways to express the same meaning. We introduce Conceptizer, a training framework that extends next-token prediction with a reward for meaning. Conceptizer uses a fixed, model-agnostic vocabulary of ~9K concepts, built from contextual synonyms, as units of meaning alongside tokens as units of text. It maps each next-token distribution to a concept distribution and shares the reward for the target meaning across its possible wordings. This provides a concept-level training signal without adding model parameters. Applied to 3 model families at 1B-8B parameters, Conceptizer achieves the lowest held-out NTP perplexity at concept-target positions and assigns the highest probability to contextual synonyms, against all 3 baselines. It also improves downstream performance on knowledge and sentiment tasks while remaining comparable on commonsense. These results show that language models can be directly optimized to predict meaning, not just the next token.

</details>

Abstract: Speaking-style captioning (SSC) aims to generate natural language descriptions of how speech is delivered, capturing paralinguistic attributes such as vocal timbre, prosody, and expressivity. Many downstream applications, including conversational AI agents, controllable speech generation, and large-scale audio annotation, require dimension-specific style captions that describe targeted aspects of speech (e.g., speaker traits, emotion, or delivery style), rather than a single undifferentiated description. However, existing work lacks a unified formulation for controllable speaking-style captioning. We address this gap by formulating SSC as an instruction-following audio-language task, where explicit instructions specify the speaking-style dimensions to be described. We introduce StyleInstructCaps, the first standardized benchmark for controllable speaking-style captioning in audio-language models. Across nine state-of-the-art audio-language models, we find that strong instruction-following ability does not necessarily imply grounded speaking-style understanding, and that SSC-specific instruction-diverse training substantially improves groundedness, controllability, and speaker consistency.

Abstract: Long-running assistants often interpret follow-up requests whose referents, constraints, and active goals were established many turns earlier. The central challenge is therefore not only context length, but retrieval intent: a current query such as ``use the previous configuration'' may omit the project, entity, and version terms needed to locate relevant evidence. Long-context models can recover those terms by replaying the interaction, but repeatedly pay the cost of processing history. Conventional retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) is cheaper, yet assumes an information-sufficient query; conversational rewriting either rereads the history or depends on an explicitly maintained textual memory. We introduce \emph{Parametric Session State} (\method{}), a reusable session-memory layer that remembers what to search for while keeping factual knowledge external.

Instruction-hierarchy benchmarks ask whether the higher-ranked source wins, but a source sets position, role and label at once. Second, we study 14 open-weight models (0.6B to 122B, thinking off and on) over 1,887,356 scored replies, checking main findings on held-out scenarios. Inside one message the first of two conflicting lines wins 72.42 to 94.66%, and across messages it keeps 22.95 to 62.65%. A policy sent first in the system (or developer) message loses most conflicts with later lines in every Qwen model but wins most on gpt-oss when it acts. In tool and file-write tasks it wins less often than the same text sent first as a labelled user message, in every model-mode. Third, we test whether pairs forecast larger conflicts; forecasts improve in every model-mode once they know where the task line is. Finally, by our keyword patterns at most 2.92% of decided pair replies flag or hedge a conflict.

</details>

Abstract: How can a language model know whether it has enough evidence to answer? Language models tend to answer even when the evidence they have is insufficient. Deciding whether to answer or gather more evidence requires a calibrated estimate of how likely an answer is to be correct with the available evidence. However, most calibration work treats the input as fixed, without asking how this estimate should change with the evidence. We introduce \emph{evidence-state calibration}, which requires a model's confidence before answering to match its probability of answering correctly and to change accordingly whenever the evidence changes this probability. We propose \ours{} (\textbf{Suffi}ciency \textbf{C}onfidence \textbf{E}stimation), which fine-tunes the confidence already present in a model's next-token distribution to match how often the model answers correctly from each evidence state. Across clinical diagnosis and multi-hop question answering with four models from two families, \ours{} reduces the Brier score by up to 60\% and increases AUROC by up to 0.16. Its confidence matches the empirical success rate and responds not only to the amount of evidence but also to which evidence is present, showing that models can learn to judge whether their current evidence is enough to answer.

</details>

Abstract: Content moderation compresses a graded, contested human judgment into a binary act (leave up / take down) over billions of items, at a scale where running a frontier LLM on every post is uneconomical. A new class of frozen "System One" models returns typed, calibrated decisions instead of text at ~USD 0.000025 per call and ~100 ms. We run the first independent audit of one such model on 142,658 human-labeled comments, and benchmark it against 14 frontier reasoning models (the GPT-5.x, Claude, Gemini 3, and Grok families) on a shared probe set. Two findings stand out. First, the cheap model is accuracy-competitive: its zero-shot classification AUROC (0.83) is not statistically separable (paired bootstrap, n=56; a failure to reject parity, not proof of equivalence) from 13 of the 14 frontier systems while costing 1-3 orders of magnitude less, and it is no worse calibrated than several of them. Second, "confidence" conflates two uncertainties that come apart: human disagreement (where annotators split on satire and coded language) and decision risk (where the automatic action is costly-wrong). Under an asymmetric moderation loss these call for opposite escalation signals, so no single confidence number serves both. Because the model prices extra questions at nearly zero, decomposing the judgment into parallel sub-questions and reading their spread recovers a human-disagreement signal that the model's native confidence misses -- but this remedy is specific: it helps the weakest, disagreement-blind models and only when the decomposition aligns with the sources of disagreement; a specialist toxicity model and the strongest LLMs do not need it, and the effect is distribution-sensitive. We will release the instrument, the cached runs, and the analysis.

</details>

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents work in scenarios with very different memory needs. A question about past conversations needs the exact facts it names, while a multi-step task needs a trace of the steps already taken together with details that the next step cannot name. Existing memories are each designed and tested for one kind of scenario. Retrieval memories such as A-Mem miss steps that the current query does not express, summaries drop exact strings, and pairing the two fixes the split of the budget in advance. We propose FlexA-Mem, a general memory that writes each unit of history once into an immutable store and renders a fresh memory for every call. The rendered memory is a compact timeline of records plus detail that a fixed rule fits into the remaining room without an LLM call. The detail is either relevant units as raw text or excerpts or the verbatim pieces that best match the query. With one store, FlexA-Mem serves both kinds of scenario. On AppWorld challenge tasks at 4k, it outperforms the second-best method by 11.5%, and on 147 unused tasks with predictions fixed in advance it outperforms A-Mem with a summary by 12.2%. On LongMemEval and LoCoMo, it matches a dedicated keyword retriever that writes no memory, while memories built on text written by an LLM fall far behind at tight budgets. Ablations show that the timeline carries AppWorld completion, whereas recovery mainly saves steps there and decides accuracy on exact facts. Code is available at https://github.com/WujiangXu/Flex-Amem

</details>

Abstract: To understand the emergence of grammatical behavior in language models, we track circuits for subject-verb number agreement in pythia-1b and ten seeds of pythia-410m across training. We find that subject-verb agreement is learned early, and is initially handled by a diffuse collection of neurons. However, after agreement behavior has stabilized, the mechanisms responsible for it continue to reorganize. The attention heads which mediate agreement often shift during training: in the case of pythia-1b, behavior is controlled by two heads early in training but by a third (different) head later. Furthermore, the circuit shrinks over training, with pythia-1b's final circuit being strikingly sparse. We take these results to be informative about how linguistic knowledge emerges and is represented in a language model.

Multi-agent debate offers a collaborative approach to this task by bringing together diverse perspectives to develop research ideas. Abstract: During post-training, large language models (LLMs) learn value preferences from corpora that may contain imbalanced proportions of opinions associated with different demographic groups. Amplifying these imbalances may further underrepresent some groups' opinions. However, how opinion mixtures shape value preferences across post-training paths remains unclear. To answer this question, we conduct controlled experiments using post-training corpora with different opinion mixtures and five post-training paths based on SFT, DPO, and GRPO. We find that post-training amplifies opinion imbalances differently across post-training paths. Once value preferences are amplified, whether further post-training can remove them depends on both the post-training method and the opinion mixture in the corpus. More concerningly, a model's stability under interventions depends on whether further post-training changes its value preference. Moreover, these value preferences are transferred to student models even when distillation uses corpora with no explicit opinion questions. These findings suggest that both the opinion mixture in post-training corpora and the choice of post-training method should be considered when developing, evaluating, and reusing LLMs. Our code and data are available at https://github.com/ku-nlp/llm-value-preferences.

Abstract: Linguistic productivity, as evidenced by the use of words in novel contexts, is one of the most striking facts about human language. However, how abstract the knowledge that enables such productivity is remains unclear. We compare people's and computational language models' linguistic productivity in contexts where successful generalization depends on form-based information. We focus on extending the use of a novel verb learned in the active voice to passive contexts. We find people extend active to passive uses in a way consistent with abstract generalizations concerning linguistic form, even when no plausible meaning can be assigned to the novel verb. In contrast, large language models succeeded only when a meaning like an existing verb's could be assigned to the novel verb. We conclude that computational models' syntactic knowledge remains tied to the distribution and meaning of specific lexical items in a way that human syntactic knowledge is not.

Abstract: Selecting a compact training subset is an important step in adapting medical language models from existing question-answering banks. We investigate perturbation-based data selection using two signals derived from Gaussian perturbations of the final-block representation: prediction-change frequency and non-flipping margin contraction. After a shared entropy prefilter, Representation-Sensitivity Prioritization (RSP) combines the signals using automatic root-mean-square scaling, while RSP-Drop ranks candidates by contraction alone. Both selectors use only questions and answer options during selection. We evaluate them with MedGemma-4B on five medical QA datasets at retained-set budgets of 64, 128, and 256 examples. At the primary budget of 128, RSP and RSP-Drop obtain dataset-averaged Macro-F1 scores of 47.55 and 48.28, respectively, compared with 45.11 for entropy-pool-matched random selection. Their relative performance varies across datasets and budgets. A selected-set case study identifies contrasting clean-margin profiles among the examples exchanged by the two rules, while a full-pool ablation shows that entropy prefiltering is not uniformly beneficial. These retrospective results characterize the empirical utility and limitations of perturbation-based selection under a fixed adaptation protocol. Evaluation uses one backbone and benchmarks with documented training-family overlap; it does not establish clinical effectiveness.

Abstract: Automated semantic complexity estimation remains underexplored in NLP, despite important applications in linguistics and interpretability. This paper defines semantic complexity as the code length required to uniquely specify a text's semantic meaning, and operationalises it via SCAPE-T, an attention-derived metric based on encoder-only transformers. Existing measures either model reader effort (readability, lexical difficulty), which correlates with surface features (word length, syntax), or quantify surface features directly (type-token ratio, word frequency). SCAPE-T targets semantic integration directly by combining three submetrics: (i) inverse-weighted self-focus (token-to-self), (ii) CLS focus (token-to-CLS), and (iii) inter-head redundancy (cosine similarity across heads), using layer-weighted aggregation; data-driven learning is confined to hyperparameter tuning for calibration. Development uses philosophical prose vs. MCTest stories; evaluation is on children's stories vs. technical Wikipedia articles, with pairwise accuracy on contrasts as the primary criterion. Baselines include readability, syntactic/lexical, and LLM-derived measures. SCAPE-T substantially outperforms all tested baselines under word-length control, with a pairwise accuracy of 78.5% (95% bootstrap CI: 76.1-80.4, N=10k pairwise comparisons; +12.7 percentage points over the strongest baseline); without that control, readability heuristics overtake, consistent with their reliance on surface features. On a short-terms abstractness dataset, SCAPE-T also leads. Ablations show each submetric contributes materially. Overall, the results show SCAPE-T isolates conceptual structure from lexical/syntactic confounds better than existing methods, and generalises across two held-out domains.

</details>

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are reported to be susceptible to reliability issues including hallucinations and reasoning errors. Existing quality detectors can predict unreliable outputs based on internal representations with decent accuracies. However, training these detectors often requires substantial domain-specific labeled data, which limits their applicability to new domains. We propose to address this challenge by transferring representational signatures across related domains. More specifically, we build a statistical framework that first extracts low-dimensional signatures for each domain, then uses cross-fitting to learn composition weights of domain-specific predictors. The weights adapt to each source domain's predictive relevance to the target domain. Our asymptotic analysis of a least-squares analogue characterizes the limiting prediction risk and aggregation weights in terms of domain discrepancies and representation bias. Experiments across multiple hallucination datasets and open-weight LLMs show that, with only 25 labeled questions in the new domain, adaptive transfer improves hallucination-detection AUROC by 6.8%. For reasoning-step prediction, it improves AUROC by 8.4%.

</details>

Abstract: French users may omit apostrophes and diacritics or make keyboard typos in everyday input. The effects of these common variations on language model performance and prediction reliability remain insufficiently understood. To address this gap, we construct a controlled French surface-variant benchmark covering seven task types, with 4,200 original-low-high triplets (12,600 input instances). Each high-intensity variant retains all low-intensity edits and adds further changes. We evaluate six models from two LLM families. The results show that accuracy decreases and Brier score and negative log-likelihood (NLL) increase with perturbation intensity. Larger models show smaller accuracy losses under high intensity, but increases in Brier score and NLL do not consistently decrease with scale. Keyboard typos cause the largest losses in accuracy and the quality of predicted probabilities at the predefined high-intensity settings. Finally, calibration temperatures fitted on original inputs transfer effectively to surface variants. Combining calibration with input restoration further improves Brier score and NLL over either method alone while preserving restored accuracy. Our benchmark provides a unified framework for jointly evaluating task accuracy and prediction reliability under common French surface variations.

</details>

Abstract: Combining language models is usually done by averaging weights, which works only when the models share a common starting point. We ask whether five frozen, independently trained models with different internal designs (four transformers from four organizations and one state-space model) can be related through their activations alone, with no weight merging. Using permutation tests, an independent replication probe set, depth sweeps, pooling ablations and confound checks, we find statistically robust correspondence between how the five models organize the same text. It is strongest at each model's final layer and weaker for the state-space model, whose weakest pairing is still 35-43 times chance. We use this to build a shared space with Generalized Procrustes Analysis, in which no model is a fixed reference. At the final layer, mean held-out retrieval accuracy over the twenty ordered model pairs is 0.91 to 0.93 on two probe sets (chance 0.03), beating pairwise linear maps (0.82 to 0.85), but the space is worse than those maps in the middle layers. It transfers to ordinary English of unfamiliar grammatical form and fails on mathematical and logical notation. We then test whether the space supports a router that picks among the five models on gradeable tasks. Across four task types we find no case where routing beats always using the strongest model, and we report this as a negative result. A correctness signal that looked strong turned out to be category membership in disguise. All experiments ran on one laptop without a GPU.

Abstract: Textual graph question answering (GraphQA) and graph-based retrieval-augmented generation (GraphRAG) have gained increasing attention as a way to ground large language models (LLMs) on structured knowledge. Despite extensive efforts, existing methods still face two key gaps. The first is an architectural gap: existing methods typically fine-tune an LLM-based generator, but do not also make the retriever itself a learnable LLM for graph evidence selection within one end-to-end retrieve-then-generate pipeline. The second is a retrieval-supervision gap: existing retrievers rarely use gold answer signals to supervise evidence selection. As a result, they may fail to recall evidence nodes required for reasoning, resulting in insufficient coverage of the gold answer set; meanwhile, naive attempts to boost recall often introduce excessive irrelevant information that compromises compactness and overwhelms the LLM. To address these gaps, we propose TaLenT, an LLM-based end-to-end retriever-generator framework for textual GraphQA. To bridge the architectural gap, TaLenT pairs a learnable LLM-based retriever for evidence selection with a fine-tuned LLM-based generator within one unified retrieve-then-generate pipeline. To bridge the retrieval-supervision gap, we leverage gold answer signals as retriever supervision. Specifically, we stratify graph nodes into gold answers, answer-related nodes, and irrelevant noise. We then adopt a weighted pseudo-label loss to prioritize gold answers and preserve answer-related nodes for coverage, while discarding irrelevant noise for compactness. Experimental results across three GraphQA datasets demonstrate that TaLenT consistently improves downstream performance.

</details>

Abstract: Rubric-based rewards enable effective reinforcement learning on non-verifiable tasks. However, because these rewards are typically defined as a weighted measure of rubric coverage, models are trained primarily to maximize a recall objective. As a result, RL-trained models produce responses that are more complete but also more verbose and contain more unnecessary or factually incorrect content, leading them to be less preferred than responses from the base model. To address this issue, we present the FORK reward, which jointly optimizes recall and a novel precision objective, which requires each unit of a model response to be both necessary and factually correct, as measured by rubric-driven necessity credits and a factual-correctness grader, respectively. Our experimental results show that FORK outperforms existing rubric-based RL methods and achieves Pareto-optimal performance that balances rubric coverage and overall response quality. Compared to the original recall-based reward, FORK substantially improves precision and reduces response length while achieving similar rubric coverage, which leads to significantly higher win rate compared to the base policy (e.g., from 10.6% to 68.0% on RubricHub-Medical and from 22.1% to 74.1% on HealthBench).

</details>

Abstract: Successful tool-use trajectories are not necessarily useful supervision. Even successful interactions may underrepresent multi-step dependencies or contain actions that are unjustified by the information visible to the agent at decision time. We identify two coupled requirements for effective trajectory supervision: compositional coverage, so that important observation-action dependencies are represented, and decision grounding, so that each supervised action is supported by the learner-visible interaction history. We introduce Trajectory2Gym, which constructs executable interactions around under-supported compositional structures and derives supervision only from grounded decisions in their realized executions. Structural-support selection allocates the resulting experience, while parent-normalized supervised fine-tuning preserves the semantic interaction as the unit of training weight. This reframes trajectory generation as the deliberate construction of learnable interaction experience, rather than the collection of successful trajectories. Across five Qwen3 scales, Trajectory2Gym improves Tau1, Tau2, and BFCL at every tested scale, increasing the equal-weight three-benchmark mean by 16.9-29.8 percentage points. The gains are strongest in stateful and multi-turn interaction, where later decisions depend on earlier observations and feedback. Our results suggest that scaling tool-use agents depends not only on collecting more successful trajectories, but on constructing interactions whose dependencies are represented and whose decisions are learnable from the agent's visible history.

Controlled experiments show that equal character budgets create a severe representation artifact for Chinese; byte-level encoding removes much of it, while static Chinese protection can subsequently make English the weakest language. On official evaluation, curriculum-dynamic checkpoints outperform a matched static Chinese-protective baseline while using fewer Chinese epochs; scaling capacity yields the strongest internal ARMOR-DP system. These results support a simple conclusion: multilingual weakness is a training-time state rather than a fixed property of a language. ARMOR-DP therefore separates diagnosis from intervention, allowing protection to follow the current bottleneck instead of a predetermined language label.

Abstract: Hallucination detection is increasingly important for evaluating and deploying large language models. However, existing methods are often compared across benchmarks that define and measure hallucination in substantially different ways, making reported scores difficult to interpret across settings. We study hallucination detection as a benchmark-conditioned measurement problem and organize existing benchmarks along three axes: provenance, which describes how model outputs and evidence conditions are obtained; typology, which captures the factuality and faithfulness failures represented by benchmark labels; and granularity, which specifies the unit at which hallucinations are annotated, predicted, and evaluated. Using this framework, we analyze how benchmark construction, annotation, and evaluation choices shape the underlying prediction task, and relate detector mechanisms and assumptions to the settings in which their performance is measured. We also examine span-level evaluation, where variation in annotation boundaries can substantially affect reported results, and propose Span Coverage F1 as a complementary containment-based metric. Our analysis clarifies what detector scores capture, highlights limitations of cross-benchmark comparisons, and motivates more explicit evaluation of generalization across benchmark conditions.

During a six-day first stage, each agent has 1,000 GPU-hours and $300 in API credit on a shared 64-GPU cluster, independently selecting data, implementing training procedures, and choosing checkpoints. All ten agents produce working assistants, with the strongest model, trained by GPT-6 Astra, closing a median of 76% of the base-to-Instruct gap across 12 capability benchmarks. We show that benchmark gains were modest, with no clear link to preference gains, while some agents encouraged longer responses and one model showed increased harmful compliance on StrongREJECT. RSI Arena provides an auditable setting for studying how human feedback can guide autonomous model development. It takes a step toward recursive self-improvement with human feedback (RSIHF), in which people help define what improvement means for the AI systems they use.

Abstract: Educational taxonomies organize educational content, such as exercise problems, into concepts, which are also called skills or knowledge components. These taxonomies define learning objectives in educational applications such as intelligent tutoring systems. When used for knowledge tracing, they improve predictive performance by approximately 3-5 AUC percentage points. These results demonstrate the potential of semantic embeddings for automatically constructing educational taxonomies that both recover structure present in human-developed taxonomies and provide useful alternative representations for downstream educational modeling.

Abstract: As machine translation systems approach human-level quality, traditional evaluation methodologies struggle to detect subtle translation errors. RATE provides a streamlined error taxonomy, expanded severity ratings, and multidimensional scoring balancing accuracy and fluency, facilitating deeper analysis of MT outputs. Abstract: Users of conversational assistants rarely state all requirements up front: they add constraints, amend them, withdraw them, digress, and return. Instruction-tuned LLMs fail here in two ways: active constraints stop being honored as turns accumulate (persistence failure), and withdrawn constraints keep being enacted (relapse). The only existing repair we know of is an external ledger that re-injects a compiled specification every turn. We propose StateWise, a self-improving approach that teaches a single LLM to maintain its own revisable constraint state, a typed state with tombstones for revoked constraints, so persistence and relapse are measured by executable checkers and ground-truth states are compiled for free. StateWise fine-tunes the LLM on two complementary skills, summarizing a dialogue into its constraint state and responding conditioned on that state, then self-trains on its own trajectories, keeping only turns that pass an exact round-trip check against the compiled state. This teaches 7-8B models to track the state almost perfectly but, surprisingly, not to obey it: on Llama-3.1-8B every supervised variant roughly doubles relapse. We therefore add a preference stage on self-generated relapse contrasts, where the rejected response enacts a revoked rule and the chosen one passes the round-trip check. On two backbones with held-out constraint families and unseen phrasings, StateWise cuts relapse by 8-22 points relative to supervised self-training, lifts conversation success by 8-19 points over the backbone, and beats an oracle compiled-ledger baseline on relapse with no inference-time scaffolding. We also characterize the recipe's cost on general instruction-following benchmarks and study weight interpolation as a mitigation.

</details>

Abstract: Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) lets large language models (LLMs) answer knowledge-intensive questions from retrieved evidence, but adapting a general-purpose RAG system to a specialized domain such as medicine is difficult when only an unlabeled corpus is available. Self-training approaches such as SimRAG address this by letting the LLM write its own question-answer pairs from domain passages and fine-tuning on the pairs that survive a round-trip consistency filter. We observe that these approaches treat every retained example identically: they never ask whether the student already knows the answer, already extracts it correctly from the retrieved context, or fails - and if so, whether the failure lies with the reader or with the retriever. To tackle this, we propose TriageRAG, a failure-aware self-training approach that probes the student on each self-generated question with and without retrieved context, triages it into one of four categories (known, solved, context-failure, retrieval-failure), and routes each category to the training signal that addresses it, including a parametric route that teaches the reader to answer when retrieval misses. Probing is cheap and repeated every round, so the training mixture follows the student, and the triage histogram doubles as a label-free diagnostic of where the domain gap lies. Experiments on 11 datasets spanning three domains with a Qwen3-4B backbone, and on CS-Bench with Qwen3-8B, demonstrate that TriageRAG outperforms a budget-matched SimRAG by 1.2-6.4 points and is the only method that recovers the closed-book level in every case where retrieval hurts the base model. A single ablation identifies the mechanism: round-trip filtering removes every training context that lacks the evidence, and a reader that never sees such contexts learns to over-trust noisy retrieval.

</details>

Abstract: Text-to-SQL systems achieve remarkable accuracy on academic benchmarks, yet performance drops dramatically in enterprise deployments. This gap stems from a fundamental assumption: existing approaches require complete schema documentation, but enterprise databases typically lack comprehensive documentation, featuring cryptic naming and implicit relationships. We present the first text-to-SQL framework that operates without schema documentation, instead learning database structure from historical query logs. Query logs encode implicit structural knowledge: which tables are queried together, how they join, and what columns map to business concepts. We construct a knowledge graph from parsed query patterns and introduce entity-driven GraphRAG retrieval that grounds user questions in domain terminology rather than relying solely on embedding similarity. Validated on both an industrial deployment with expert evaluation and the Beaver benchmark, our approach substantially outperforms similarity-based retrieval methods, the dominant industry approach, in both executability and answer quality. On Beaver, we achieve 95.7% execution validity compared to 60.8% for embedding-based retrieval, with expert data engineers preferring our system 70% to 10% on internal deployment. Our work demonstrates that text-to-SQL can operate effectively in schema-ambiguous enterprise environments, enabling natural language database interfaces "in the wild".

Abstract: Large language models increasingly act as partners in everyday communication. In this study, we refer to such epistemic question-asking as model curiosity, and introduce a theory-grounded taxonomy that distinguishes curiosity-oriented questions from generic model questioning. Across four conversation datasets, we find that curiosity-oriented questions remain rare (17-28% of all questions), but are associated with more exploratory and epistemically rich subsequent user responses, with varying patterns across datasets. These findings have implications for building LLMs that ask more selectively to support deeper exploration and human-AI collaboration.

Abstract: Tool-using large language models (LLMs) must decide whether external information warrants action. We evaluate five LLMs on 196 constructed false notices paired with legitimate counterparts and 204 archived attacks adapted to simulated tool use. When asked to act on the constructed notices, models issue harmful tool calls in 27%--99% of cases they have rated untrustworthy. Removing the preliminary rating step increases harmful execution by 15 percentage points on average, showing that credibility elicitation itself changes the behavior being evaluated. These reductions can also compromise legitimate task completion. Our findings motivate evaluating misinformation defenses through both harmful actions and legitimate task completion, while accounting for the behavioral effects of the evaluation protocol.

Abstract: Memory is increasingly used in long-horizon agents, yet the net value of building memory over direct retrieval from raw history remains largely unclear. Through a large-scale evaluation across multiple benchmarks and model backbones, we find many dedicated memory systems underperform naive RAG in many cases, indicating a negative net value. Taking RAG as a reference, we dissect the net value of memory into the gains and losses over direct retrieval, termed memory benefit and memory tax. These gains and losses arise because memory process changes how the raw information is organized and accessed. We view such changes as the double-edged deviations from the raw history. They are originally designed and introduced to benefit the task, for example by reorganizing facts or distilling preferences, but some may also remove or obscure evidence, giving rise to memory tax. Since memory benefit reflects the expected gains of its design, we then focus more on the overlooked memory tax, study how it arises from the system and evaluation side, and generalize the concept to more application scenarios of memory. We hope the notion of memory tax can provide a new lens and shift more attention from standalone performance to the net value of memory, informing more reliable memory designs and evaluations. We will open-source the code and results to the community. Project Page: https://hexuan21.github.io/MemoryTax/

On average, two post-trained copies of the same pretraining run have per-prompt non-refusal rates that correlate at 0.984. A different pretraining seed lowers the correlation to 0.811, and a different data recipe lowers it only slightly further, to 0.779. These results indicate that which jailbreaks break a checkpoint depends much more on its pretraining run than on its data, a pattern that holds in all 25 data recipes and at 150M and 300M parameters. In practice, the best of 91 attack templates found on one checkpoint stays the best after a new pretraining seed in only 27% of model pairs, against 67% after a new post-training seed. Each pretraining run also leaves a fingerprint: across 3,211 prompts, a model's per-prompt non-refusal rates identify its pretraining run among all 75 runs every time, and a random subset of 50 prompts still does so 98% of the time.

Abstract: We introduce the Literature and Linguistics Analysis Benchmark (LALA), comprising 236 open-ended questions written by experts in linguistics and literary studies, together with expert-provided ideal answers. By comparing models within families and at similar training scales using human and LLM judges, LALA identifies differences in linguistic competence associated with post-training, offering a framework for evaluating future models and guiding targeted improvements. We administered questions to 17 language models and evaluated their responses using both human expert assessment and two LLM evaluators, Sol and Qwen. Base models displayed little of the linguistic competence assessed by the benchmark, whereas their post-trained versions performed markedly better, suggesting that post-training plays a central role in making linguistic knowledge accessible and usable. Parameter count, by contrast, showed no statistically significant association with partial-credit performance, and competencies remained unevenly distributed across linguistic domains. We interpret this pattern as evidence that post-training adds linguistic competencies piecemeal, without producing a general linguistic intelligence capable of extending them beyond the learned policies that support them. Models performed best on logical relations between statements and selected discourse-level tasks, while even the strongest post-trained models continued to struggle with ambiguity, literary style, reference, and misleading assumptions. Abstract: Speculative decoding significantly accelerates autoregressive text generation in Large Language Models (LLMs), but its efficacy depends largely on two key factors: the distributional alignment between the draft and the target model, and the draft model's latency during inference. However, existing approaches typically utilize independently pretrained small draft models or seek to prune the target model to a manually chosen architecture, thereby failing to explicitly balance the trade-off between draft model alignment and token generation efficiency. To address this gap, we formulate draft model design as an optimization problem and propose T2D, a framework that constructs draft models directly from the target model using latency-aware structured pruning and knowledge distillation. In this manner, T2D obtains draft models that are jointly optimized for both measured inference latency and cross-entropy loss on the target model's responses, resulting in superlative speculative decoding performance. Through extensive experiments on multi-domain speculative decoding benchmarks and across several target model families of differing scale, we demonstrate how T2D prunes up to 92\% of the target model to obtain highly performant draft models compared to competitive baselines. Moreover, draft models obtained via T2D can also be utilized by other speculative decoding token-alignment and knowledge distillation methods in a plug-and-play fashion, leading to further gains. Our findings underscore the importance of jointly navigating the draft model architecture-alignment frontier to unlock the full potential of speculative decoding for LLMs.

</details>

Abstract: Long-context language models can increasingly process entire documents, making full-context prompting a practical alternative to passage retrieval. However, greater capacity determines how much information a model can receive, but not which information best supports a query. This distinction motivates rethinking retrieval: when the full document already fits, can selecting and organizing its evidence improve answering? The challenge is to retain supporting facts and relations while keeping the context focused on the question. To reconcile these goals, we introduce \ours, a training-free framework that uses semantic abstraction to guide selection while retaining original text for answering. \ours constructs a reusable semantic hierarchy from the full document in a single generation, linking node descriptions to source spans. Given a query, structural--semantic scoring and fine-grained lexical attribution nominate shared source nodes; their union with ancestor context preserves structural relations and complete local text. Across four frontier answer models, \ours improves average QA-F1 by 1.72 points over full-context prompting on three LongBench tasks, surpassing the evaluated baselines on the four-model average while reducing answer-context length by 58.55\%. Abstract: Long-sequence models must retain earlier information. Full attention keeps all past tokens but grows expensive, and recurrent-memory models compress history into fixed slots for linear-time processing. Limited capacity makes writes consequential: distractors can interfere with useful content, while skipped evidence cannot be recalled. Existing multi-slot memories write every token to the same number of slots, merging whether to store it with how many slots to update. We introduce Factorized Write Routing (FWR), which first keeps or skips each token and then chooses a fixed or token-adaptive number of slots. Queries still read all slots. With four slots per write, long-context accuracy favors always writing on dense Feature Recall (0.472 vs 0.337), but learned selective writing on sparse-fact Passkey (0.222 vs 0.048). The tested adaptive rule underperforms fixed allocation under the learned gate on both tasks. A diagnostic oracle given fact labels unavailable at deployment reaches 0.372 on Passkey, showing headroom in filtering distractors. The results suggest avoiding missed writes in information-dense contexts and filtering distractors when they dominate.

Abstract: In this paper, we introduce $Δτ$-Bench, an executable agent benchmark that models the structural evolution of large tool ecosystems. In $Δτ$-Bench, independently evolving services continually reshape how tasks can be accomplished: familiar tools may disappear or be replaced, while more efficient alternatives emerge amid overlapping functionality and noisy documentation. Against this moving capability frontier, we evaluate whether agents can proactively discover efficient tool-use paths and promptly revise their accumulated procedural knowledge, bringing together demands that existing benchmarks fail to examine. $Δτ$-Bench comprises 1,588 validated multi-turn tasks across 42 domains and six successive ecosystem states generated through capability-preserving transformations. Our evaluation shows that even the strongest evaluated model or harness achieves only 42.3% task success, while initial gains from existing skill-learning methods can not consistently translate into better adaptation as the ecosystem evolves. These findings motivate Steward, an ecosystem manager that jointly manages tools and procedural knowledge through incremental skill maintenance and dynamic provisioning during task execution. Experiments show that Steward sustains higher task success across ecosystem changes with lower skill-maintenance overhead, while improving both recovery from disrupted workflows and adoption of more efficient alternatives.

</details>

It consists of stance detection, fixed balancing (2 supporting + 2 opposing + 1 neutral), and a debiasing prompt, without introducing additional large language model calls. Abstract: Extracting salient information from long-horizon dialogue histories remains a significant challenge for Large Language Models (LLMs) due to the restricted context windows and efficient retrieval challenges. Current methods typically rely on history organization or local token-level optimization, suffering from information loss, retrieval redundancy, and myopic attention. Inspired by the view in cognitive science that dialogue constitutes sense-making and memory association, we propose CoLink, a framework that enhances LLMs' global context awareness in domain-specific multi-turn dialogues through information linking and compression. CoLink first clusters historical contexts into topic graphs to establish cross-semantic associations with external knowledge. Guided by the adaptive control of thought, it then retrieves and activates knowledge via synergizing semantic relevance and graph-structural topology. Crucially, to achieve effective compression of history and knowledge, we ground multi-turn dialogues in information theory and derive a principled optimization objective for extracting the core sense-making information. Coupled with a competitive slot mechanism, CoLink condenses the global context into dedicated memory and knowledge slots, efficiently distilling lengthy, redundant inputs into an extremely compact latent representation. Abstract: Large reasoning models are costly to serve because they are large and because they generate long chains of thought (CoT). Weight pruning and CoT compression address these two costs separately, but they interfere in that pruning adds redundant reasoning on problems the model still solves and incomplete reasoning on those it no longer solves, and on harder problems makes the model less compressible by subsequent length-preference optimization. We trace this to a blind spot on each side: pruning criteria do not distinguish necessary from redundant reasoning, and preference-based CoT compression receives no training signal on problems a pruned model always or never solves. We propose Contrastive Set-Difference (CSD), which identifies neurons enriched in redundant reasoning by contrasting their activations on long and short correct solutions of the same problem and prunes them preferentially, and we extend preference pairs to the problems a pruned model always or never solves. Pruning 20\% of DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B, the combination uses 8--22\% fewer thinking tokens on four math benchmarks than pruning with reasoning-aware calibration (RAC) followed by the same compression, with accuracy within 0.5 points.

Abstract: Understanding how individual token choices affect reasoning length is important for characterizing reasoning models. Building on prior findings that changing the first reasoning token (hereafter, the first token) can substantially alter reasoning length, we focus on this initial choice to investigate how its influence operates during subsequent generation. One possibility is that the first token changes the early text that follows, which in turn affects reasoning length. However, replacing only the first token of an existing reasoning prefix and resuming generation substantially changes the remaining reasoning length, even though all other prefix tokens are unchanged. To trace this persistent influence, we compare next-token distributions while varying only the first token and find that sensitivity is concentrated at a small set of generation positions. Blocking attention from these positions to the first token reduces the length gap between first-token conditions. Using next-token distributions from a different first-token condition at these positions also substantially changes subsequent reasoning length. Together, these findings suggest that attention to the first token at a few sensitive positions helps sustain its length effect, illustrating how a local token choice persistently influences a sequence-level outcome.

</details>

Abstract: In formal systems such as Lean 4, efficiently finding answers to mathematical problems and generating verifiable proofs is an important task in automated mathematical reasoning. In the traditional way where an upstream solver provides multiple candidate answers, the common workflow requires searching for proofs for each candidate separately, incurring redundant computational cost. To this end, we propose a joint proof framework that integrates candidate answer selection, answer supplementation, and formal proof generation into a unified reasoning process. At its core is a trained prover that selects given or proposes new answers and generates a proof for at least one answer in a single call. Joint proof can also reuse generated proofs to check for faithfulness errors. For problems with a unique answer, if two distinct answers both obtain verifiable proofs under the same formal statement, this confirms the statement does not faithfully preserve the original problem semantics. On MATH-500, AMC 2023, and AIME 2024/2025/2026, our framework achieves the same formal proof success rate as per-candidate proof generation with about one sixth of the token cost, and identifies at least 6.1% of the formalized statements as unfaithful without requiring additional judges. Abstract: The Voynich manuscript is a fifteenth-century book in a script that no one can read. One account holds that its text means nothing and that a scribe made it by copying his own words and changing them a little. The other holds that a real language lies hidden behind a cipher. We write each account as a program that fills the pages of the manuscript, fit every program with the same budget, and check it on statistics the fit never saw. The copying process of Timm and Schinner predicts these statistics far better than any cipher with one key. But ciphers that switch keys between the manuscript's two dialects, or that encode only the word classes of a real text, predict them equally well. Word statistics alone cannot decide. We look for a trace of language that needs no decipherment. In 28 texts in 16 languages the same word pairs keep sharing a line. The manuscript shows this pattern at half the strength of the weakest of these texts. A word code keeps the pattern above the manuscript unless its plaintext has a weak pattern or many words are miscopied. Solvers that decipher control texts find no readable text in the manuscript under a letter cipher, a word code or 28 codes with one letter, letter pair or syllable per word. Word codes of abbreviated Latin, the plaintext their fits prefer, show which word follows which more clearly than the manuscript unless two words in five are miscopied. One cipher remains possible, a nomenclator with a second key whose scribe miscopies more than half of the words, and our solvers cannot exclude it in 9 of 16 languages. Most of the structure a solver finds comes from how the end of one word shapes the start of the next, which reflects how tightly glyphs are bound to each other. Planted messages are found reliably only when they are long, each letter always becomes the same word and these words are adjacent, so other messages could hide.

</details>

Abstract: A long-lived assistant that brings up memories without being asked must decide at every turn whether anything in its store is worth an interruption. We show that this decision is a multiple-testing problem whose difficulty grows with the store size $N$. On streaming replays of LongMemEval and LoCoMo, a similarity threshold tuned to 5% false alarms at $N=8$ reaches 87-100% at $N=2048$, across embedding models and for explicit and implicit cues; LLM judges show the same growth from a lower base. We prove that a gate with false-alarm rate $α$ operates at a per-item false-positive rate of about $-\ln(1-α)/N$, which pins its hit rate to the far left of the single-comparison ROC curve and, under regular variation, makes it decay as a power of $N$ with an explicit constant. The resulting prediction matches the hit rate of a per-$N$ oracle threshold to within one percentage point. The analysis explains why lexical and semantic channels need thresholds growing like $\ln N$ and $\sqrt{\ln N}$, and why per-cue normalisation roughly doubles the hit rate at matched false alarms. A cue-swap conformal gate, which compares the current cue with the user's other cues on the same store, needs no labels and has a finite-sample false-alarm guarantee under exchangeability; on LongMemEval it holds false alarms near 5% at the hit rate of a label-tuned threshold. Three annotators (Fleiss' $κ=0.63$) find that about nine in ten false alarms are genuine interruptions. Code and data: https://github.com/RiverChenStillCompiling/price-of-scale

</details>

Abstract: Estimating the correctness of large language model (LLM) predictions without access to ground truth is critical for reliable deployment in high-stakes settings. Existing approaches, such as entropy and perplexity, operate solely on final output statistics, discarding the rich information encoded in intermediate reasoning steps. To address this limitation, we propose STEP (Soft Trajectory Estimation of Prediction correctness), a training-free framework that scores each reasoning step via lightweight forward passes to obtain a soft probability distribution qi over candidate answers, from which we derive a set of soft trajectory features capturing per-step confidence and its progression toward the final answer. Extensive experiments on various benchmarks demonstrate consistent and substantial improvements, particularly in AUROC, over output-based baselines and hard-indicator trajectory methods. Notably, Temporally-Weighted Consistency, which assigns higher importance to later reasoning steps where answer distributions have stabilized, contributes the largest gains, highlighting the importance of temporal structure in reasoning trajectories. These results further validate the advantage of preserving continuous probability signals over binary discretization.

Abstract: The largest open German text datasets are built from broad web crawls, which provide scale but limited coverage of specific domains. We introduce RefinedGAW, a processed and annotated resource derived from 26 semi-annual crawls of the German Academic Web, totaling 122 million documents and 314 billion tokens, with full crawl and capture history of every text. Documents labeled as academic research account for 84% of German RefinedGAW tokens across HTML and PDF. At 87.6 billion tokens, this exceeds the 64.9 billion tokens in the German subsets of FineWeb2 and FinePDFs, despite their almost five-times-larger combined size. RefinedGAW also contains substantial amounts of educational, math-heavy, and expert-oriented content with minimal commercial bias. Across HTML and PDF, 88% of German RefinedGAW documents have no near-duplicate candidate match in the three evaluated reference datasets, supporting complementary coverage. We release a queryable metadata index for fine-grained data selection and a rehydration tool for retrieving and processing source documents.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are neural networks trained on huge text collections to generate text by predicting what comes next. Engineers using ChatGPT and other generative artificial intelligence (AI) tools need a clear picture of what training changes and prompts add. The article starts with the Transformer, which connects words in context, and pre-training, where models learn to predict text. It then explains how fine-tuning adapts models to requests, with reinforcement learning from human feedback using human answer rankings. Next, retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) supplies documents, while chain-of-thought prompting encourages intermediate steps and agents use tools. FlashAttention lowers training and running costs by reducing data movement during attention; quantized low-rank adaptation (QLoRA) saves fine-tuning memory. Readers get a life cycle figure and tables comparing methods and their limits. A short checklist for judging LLM evaluation claims flags test material in training (data contamination) and model-based grading (LLM-as-a-judge). It also covers very long inputs (long context) and hallucination, meaning false or unsupported claims. Check factual accuracy separately from whether an answer sounds helpful.

Abstract: Tabular data is widely used in scientific applications, where complex layouts, including multi-level headers, merged cells, captions, and footnotes, make information extraction difficult. This is particularly important in pharmacokinetics (PK), where values must be correctly associated with the drug, species, dose, administration route, unit, and experimental condition. Parameter-efficient fine-tuning methods such as Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) reduce adaptation cost but do not explicitly incorporate relationships between table values and surrounding headers. We introduce HARP-LoRA, a hierarchy-conditioned rank projection method for low-rank adaptation in scientific table understanding. HARP-LoRA extends projected LoRA by constructing token-specific intrinsic representations from the row- and column-header hierarchies of each data token. These representations condition the low-rank down-projection, incorporating hierarchical context while keeping the pretrained model and random projection tensors frozen. We evaluate HARP-LoRA on 950 manually annotated PK tables from 587 scientific articles and on WikiTableQuestions (WikiTQ) using Llama~3.2 3B, Llama~3.1 8B, and Gemma~3 27B. With Llama~3.1 8B, HARP-LoRA achieves a PK F1-score of 57.64\% and WikiTQ accuracy of 53.15\%, improving over LoRA by 1.42 and 6.56 points, respectively. With Gemma~3 27B, it achieves the best PK F1-score of 70.63\% and WikiTQ accuracy of 56.87\%. These results use only 172K, 197K, and 547K trainable parameters, far fewer than LoRA and TableLoRA. Overall, conditioning lightweight adapters on table-header hierarchies improves extraction from scientific tables and reasoning over flatter general-domain tables while maintaining parameter efficiency. Code is available at: https://github.com/hosseinsholehrasa/HARP-LoRA

Abstract: A chunky gold-tone chain necklace bought online for 2.43 USD was photographed four ways: worn with formal attire against a wood-panelled wall, worn at a party, worn with casual clothing in a recycling yard, and laid flat with no person present. Among the four models that answered image prompts, the same necklace was priced 1.9 to 3.3 times higher in the formal photograph than in the flat-lay (all p < 0.01). Against the yard photograph, the ratio ranged from 1.23 (Grok 4.5, not significant) to 3.57 (Kimi K3), with Claude Fable 5 at 3.32. A text-only version of the task, in which the setting is described in words, produced formal-to-yard ratios of 2.2 to 3.9 in all six models, so the effect does not require pixels. Flat-lay estimates themselves were 7.8 to 18.7 times the receipt price, so part of any headline "outfit multiplier" computed against the receipt is baseline overestimate, not a context effect. A single instruction to ignore clothing and setting significantly reduced the effect in one model (ratio of ratios 0.77, 95% CI 0.65 to 0.91) and not detectably in the others; telling the model the photo came from a company website or a personal account changed nothing significant. The study isolates a mechanism with one object and one wearer at about 10 sessions per model and condition; we outline the replication needed next, with multiple objects and a human benchmark. Abstract: Entity matching is the task of deciding whether two records describe the same real-world entity. Current entity matching methods rely on pre-trained language models (PLMs) which require task specific fine-tuning or generative large language models (LLMs) which do not require task-specific fine-tuning but are slow and expensive at matching large record sets. Decision models are a new class of LLM-based models that return a probability for each predefined answer option rather than generate text. This paper investigates decision models as an alternative to generative LLMs and fine-tuned PLMs for entity matching. We evaluate six decision models in a zero-shot setting across eight benchmarks and compare their performance with that of the generative LLM GPT-6 Luna and the PLM-based matcher Ditto. Our experiments show that, without task-specific training data, the four strongest decision models achieve mean F1 scores close to that of GPT-6 Luna, with the best model slightly surpassing it. This model also outperforms Ditto on six of the eight benchmarks. All decision models are faster than GPT-6 Luna, and most are less expensive. For three of the four strongest decision models, the predicted match probabilities are well calibrated without requiring any task-specific recalibration. A model cascade that routes the least confident record pairs to an ensemble of generative LLMs improves the mean F1 scores of the four strongest decision models by 1.9-4.0 percentage points. Using the best decision model and its best-performing prompt for each benchmark, the cascade surpasses the highest published F1 scores from literature on four of the eight benchmarks.

</details>

Abstract: Latency-sensitive spoken agents must know not only which tool to call, but also when they have heard enough to act. Waiting for the turn to complete delays execution even when the request is already actionable. However, neither existing spoken agents nor spoken dialogue corpora define the earliest point at which a request becomes actionable. Through controlled task generation, speech synthesis and alignment, and gated annotation, we construct 26,694 dialogues totaling 786 hours across 48 domains, with disjoint training and test argument values. We additionally create an audio rendering of $τ^2$-bench for external evaluation. We train an omni-modal language model on this corpus, enabling causal, chunk-level decisions and partial-transcript generation for downstream call completion. The model achieves tool-state macro-F1 scores of 0.48 and 0.47 on held-out dialogues and the external benchmark, respectively. For correct calls, the estimated mean lead over a turn-end cascade, using the same formatter, is 1.8-2.0 seconds in-domain and 1.1 seconds on $τ^2$-bench. These results demonstrate a data-driven path toward spoken agents that act while listening. We will open-source the pipeline and the training scripts.

Abstract: An agent that truly knows and acts on behalf of its user has been a long-standing promise of AI assistants. Computer-use agents bring this promise within reach by capably executing fully specified tasks across apps. However, real-world user requests are often underspecified. To act correctly for the particular user, an agent must infer some missing information from the user's activity history and ask for other information that only the user can provide, without making unsupported assumptions or burdening the user with unnecessary questions. We present iPrefBench, a benchmark for evaluating the infer and ask capabilities of personal mobile-use agents in an executable iOS environment with an interactive simulated user. Grounded in a coherent persona with 3,051 past activity events, iPrefBench comprises 130 underspecified user tasks across 26 apps. Each omitted user-dependent requirement is, by construction, either history-inferable or ask-only. iPrefBench evaluates personalized mobile agents on both whether agents complete tasks correctly for the user (i.e., personalized task completion) and whether they seek user input when necessary (i.e., user-agent interaction). Evaluation of 7 frontier agents reveals a substantial gap between execution and personalization. Agents achieve high action completion scores (up to 91.6%) but low full task success (up to 47.7%) because personalization lags behind. They especially struggle with ask-only information, failing to request enough necessary information. Our findings highlight a challenge for personalized agents in knowing what to infer and when to ask. We hope that iPrefBench can serve as a valuable testbed for building agents that know their user and work with them.
