Abstract: Knowing when they are reliable is essential for language models to support human-AI collaboration. Yet interaction feedback is not always complete or correct, and may mislead models' understanding of their own capabilities. We introduce LADDER to study how language models assess themselves under unreliable feedback. Our findings show that models can accurately report supplied performance information while still struggling to form trustworthy self-assessments from interaction experience. Apparently accurate assessments can obscure capability differences and do not necessarily predict future task performance. These findings suggest that trustworthy self-assessment should be evaluated against observed task performance and its predictive value. Our study provides empirical evidence of the limits of model self-knowledge and a basis for evaluating capability awareness in reliable human-AI collaboration.

Abstract: In high-stakes domains such as finance, critical information evolves through multi-party interaction. Recovering these changes and their sources is fundamental to reliable speech understanding. Existing speech benchmarks focus on transcription, local understanding, and turn-level state prediction, leaving joint decision evolution across speakers insufficiently evaluated. We introduce DecisionTraceBench, a speech state-tracking benchmark for Decision-Critical Information State Tracking (DCIST). It jointly evaluates decision event sequences, speaker attribution, scope, and temporal evidence, using trajectory-level F1 to measure complete event-chain recovery. Our dataset comprises Chinese financial dialogues involving two to four speakers, with 100.39 hours of synthetic audio and 8.61 hours of human-performed recordings. On a fixed cohort of human-performed recordings, five cascaded systems and two direct-audio models achieve DCIST scores of 5.73% to 21.35%. Ablations with speaker-labeled reference scripts and no temporal localization requirements yield a maximum score of only 38.72%. Audio perturbation experiments further show that overall transcription error rates do not adequately reflect complete trajectory recovery. These findings reveal limitations in jointly understanding information provenance and state evolution, providing a benchmark for evaluating decision-process understanding and decision traceability in high-stakes speech applications.

Abstract: Knowledge editing aims to update outdated or incorrect knowledge in large language models without full retraining. Existing parameter-editing methods remain limited for long-sequence knowledge: single-point editors provide insufficient coverage for extended targets, while fixed chunk-based editors distribute editing effort uniformly across rigid predefined windows, failing to prioritize difficult, information-dense tokens. In this work, we show that token entropy is closely associated with both editing difficulty and semantic importance: high-entropy regions are harder to edit and often contain key factual content. Motivated by this observation, we propose DynEdit, a dynamic framework for long-sequence knowledge editing. DynEdit uses token-level entropy to guide non-uniform sliding, increasing the coverage of difficult high-entropy regions, and further applies entropy-guided token-level optimization to strengthen updates on information-dense tokens within each editing window. Abstract: User memory helps personalized systems build on their understanding of users over time. Experiments across two builders and two task readers show that BehaviorMem leads the evaluated agentic memories on remember and infer, and reveal different method rankings across the two capabilities and competitive statistical baselines. Abstract: Language models now answer questions alongside other claims: a note in the system prompt, a message from another agent, their own earlier answer. We ask what it takes to talk a model out of an answer it gets right on its own, and separate three factors that earlier studies vary together: where the advice sits in the prompt, who is said to have written it, and what the model has already written. On Qwen3.5-27B and four-option MMLU-Pro items, with the rest of the advice held fixed in each comparison, we find three things. First, position matters far more than the API channel, in opposite directions for a bare claim and an argument: a bare "the answer is X" does most harm just before the model answers, while the same claim with a false rationale does most harm before the question, where it raises error on items the model otherwise almost never misses from 1% to 67%; moving the claim between the system message and the user message at the same position changes error by a few points. Second, attributing the note to an authority rather than to another participant, and phrasing it assertively, each raise error by 10-27 points, while the two authorities we tested differ by under two. Third, replaying a correct worked answer before the advice protects it whether the text is shown as the model's own or as someone else's, and the same replay keeps wrong answers from being corrected by a bare correct note. The position result replicates under an independently written prompt. Code, items and all result tables are released at https://github.com/coolkillercat/safety.

</details>

Abstract: Much of the information needed for data analysis is buried in text columns: an analyst may infer customer frustration from complaint text and use it to explain resolution time. We call workloads in which processing text columns is central to subsequent data analysis TADA (\emph{Text Analysis in Data Analytics}). In an agentic setting, the user's query dynamically specifies this processing, and answering it interleaves LLM-based semantic steps with exact SQL or Python operations. This mixed execution raises two design questions: which work should be neural and which symbolic, and which semantic outputs later steps can reuse. We address them for agent-planned TADA workflows with two complementary, query-driven semantic atoms. Each atom takes a sequence of text items and a natural-language query specifying the semantic task: \textbf{Tagging} returns one string per input item, while \textbf{Categorization} returns $M$ category objects over $N$ input items. Tagging results are materialized as a row-aligned column and Categorization results as a category table; SQL or Python then performs exact computation over this exposed state. We realize this interface in TADA-Agent, whose atoms run inside generated Python programs and internally handle inputs beyond a reliable context window. We evaluate TADA-Agent on TABench, a benchmark of 40 complete analysis requests over spreadsheet tables spanning workflow composition, input--output structure, and literature-grounded analytical scenarios. Against a semantic-operator baseline and relative to a held-out TADA reference run, TADA-Agent delivers comparable row-level semantic quality, has higher complete-artifact preservation estimates, and uses over an order of magnitude fewer semantic-model requests under the evaluated native execution paths.

</details>

Abstract: Rubric-based evaluation asks which level of a rubric a response meets. It is usually done by prompting a large language model (LLM) as a judge, which needs a generative call per judgment and reads every level description, so its cost grows with the rubric. We present RubriX, a rubric judge whose architecture is inspired by contrastive language models (CLM): a LoRA-adapted encoder embeds the input and each level description, and a softmax over scaled cosine similarities gives a probability for every level without text generation. Trained on public rubric data and proprietary synthetic data, RubriX reaches 75.4% exact accuracy on held-out rubrics, against 57.1% for TypeSafe's Jev and 49.8% for a Qwen3-8B judge. On eight human-labelled benchmark sets it is within 6 percentage points of Jev's accuracy on most of them, while Jev keeps a larger lead on two and ranks responses best on average; against the results reported in the G-Eval paper, RubriX outperforms G-Eval with GPT-3.5 on every SummEval dimension and both QAGS subsets. With level embeddings cached, RubriX's latency stays at about 28 ms per judgment as level descriptions grow from 10 to 3,000 tokens, while Jev's (approximate network time subtracted) rises from 44 ms to 211 ms and the Qwen3-8B judge's from 32 ms to 516 ms, making RubriX 7.7x faster than Jev and 18.8x faster than the Qwen3-8B judge at 3,000 tokens per level.

Abstract: A numerical claim generated by a financial analyst agent can match a number in a company filing but refer to the wrong entity, concept, period, unit or scope. A check based on reference answers can mark such an answer as wrong but does not explain why it fails. In this paper, we present NumTrace, a new benchmark that audits the provenance of each numerical claim made by financial analyst agents: its source and financial semantics. NumTrace currently has 540 questions over 2,163 SEC EDGAR documents. To the best of our knowledge, it is the first numerical benchmark about who holds a company's shares (Form 13F) and what its executives are owed (Item 402(i)). On a frozen snapshot of these documents, we build the reference answers according to accounting rules. NumTrace then gives each claim three separate verdicts: value, binding and support. By retaining separate value and binding verdicts, NumTrace detects right number, wrong account (RNWA) errors: a claim has an RNWA error when it passes the value check but fails the binding check. The RNWA rate quantifies the gap between value-only and joint value-and-binding pass rates. Finally, we audit three agents, Claude Opus 5.5, GPT-6 Astra and DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash. RNWA errors make up 67.5-81.8% of each agent's failing claims. In particular, on 13F co-filing group questions, about 91% of claims pass the value check, but fewer than half pass the binding check.

Abstract: Controlling personality in large language models is important for building conversational agents that can be customized to different users and interaction settings. However, existing personality control methods often face a tradeoff between strong trait expression and natural dialogue generation. We introduce FATE-5, Faithful Alignment of Trait Expression for the Big Five, a two-stage framework that separates personality encoding from naturalness alignment. FATE-5 first learns continuous soft prompt representations of Big Five profiles using dialogue supervision and an auxiliary survey objective. It then improves dialogue naturalness through preference alignment based on both dialogue quality and personality agreement. Across three language model backbones, FATE-5 maintains strong trait expression while achieving substantially higher dialogue naturalness than other trait-strong methods. On held-out questionnaires, it achieves monotonicity correlations of 0.97-0.99 and recovery RMSE of 8.9-12.4, substantially improving over the evaluated baselines. These results demonstrate effective continuous personality control while maintaining natural conversational behavior.

Abstract: Outcome RL raises pass@1 but not pass@k at large k (Yue et al., 2025). But every pass@1 is scored under a token budget. Every recipe also trains under one. On physics and math problems at fixed token budgets, we compare the pass@1 of 20 released RL models (1.5B to 32B) with that of the thinking models they started from. We ask whether each pass@1 gain is changed behaviour or new knowledge. We divide each change in pass@1 into three parts. Finishing measures answering before the budget runs out. Reliability measures being right once finished. Reach measures problems RL solves that its base answered wrongly in all eight tries, minus problems the base solves that RL answered wrongly in all eight. We find four things. (1) Where the base often hits the token budget before answering, most of the change, gain or loss, is finishing (82% at 4k and 8k). Given more tokens, the base closes most of the finishing gap itself. (2) Where the base has enough tokens, the gain is reliability: the RL model is graded right more often, even on problems both finish. (3) By our definition, RL rarely solves what its base cannot. Reach adds at most 2.1 percentage points on any of our four physics and math sets. We checked 144 physics problems that only the RL model solved. Of these, 3.5% hold up when resampled or read. In a random sample, nine of ten new RL steps a human confirmed also appear in the base's samples. (4) In a small-scale RL run on an 8B model, the gain on new problems is finishing, with reliability flat. Supplying each problem's physical setup was not shown to improve on plain RL.

</details>

Abstract: Pretrained transformers spend a large share of their inference compute inside self-attention, yet the per-head query-key interaction is structurally low-rank by construction. We ask a diagnostic question: across layers and architectures, which heads actually use the full rank budget on real data, and which do not? We propose SVD-Attention, a post-hoc method that decomposes the per-head interaction matrix $M_h$ and scores every singular mode by both its spectral magnitude and its activation variance across tokens on a small calibration set. The score keeps the modes the head can amplify and that real data actually activates, and discards the rest; the resulting factorization is exact for the retained modes and needs no finetuning. Across seven pretrained models and five task types, the diagnostic finds a task-dependent pattern: redundancy sits in the early to middle layers for sentiment and image classification and for generation, and in the late layers for dense prediction and extractive question answering. It is also predictive: truncating the layers it flags costs at most $1.75$ F1, while truncating the same number of layers elsewhere in the same model costs up to $50$ F1. SVD-Attention is an analysis tool rather than a compression method: acting on it reduces inference FLOPs by $0.8$-$1.5\%$ on its own and by $8$-$11\%$ when composed with weight pruning, with $0$-$4\%$ change in task quality.

</details>

Abstract: GLP-1 medications are increasingly used for weight management, and many people share their experiences online. These narratives show why people take the drugs and what they experience, but they remain unstructured and disconnected from formal pharmacovigilance. We therefore present AskGLP1, a system that transforms online patient narratives into a knowledge graph (KG) and answers questions about medication experiences. Through an LLM-assisted pipeline grounded in the MedDRA terminology, AskGLP1 screens nearly 8.5 million Reddit posts for medication experiences and distills 163,135 of them, with 2,506 WebMD reviews, into a KG of 385,790 drug-side-effect and drug-purpose relations. We find that GLP-1 experiences are source- and drug-specific. Online narratives share the most common gastrointestinal symptoms with FAERS, but they foreground everyday burdens such as fatigue and food aversion, whereas FAERS reports more often include injection-site reactions and selected serious diagnoses such as pancreatitis. We further find that posts report less nausea and vomiting for tirzepatide than for semaglutide, more fatigue for Wegovy than Ozempic and for Zepbound than Mounjaro. Evaluations show that the agent answers with verifiable evidence and declines requests for medical advice. Our findings reveal gaps between what FAERS captures and what people report online, and they call for patient-centered approaches to pharmacovigilance. Abstract: End-to-end full-duplex spoken dialogue models enable natural, real-time spoken interactions with interruptions, backchannels, and overlapping speech. Prior evaluations have assessed diverse interaction capabilities and identified some degradation patterns across conversational turns. However, how model behavior evolves over extended sessions remains insufficiently understood. In this work, we address this gap by systematically characterizing long-session degradation and developing efficient methods to mitigate it. We introduce a closed-loop evaluation framework for long-session stability and apply it to several publicly available full-duplex speech models, revealing degradation over time in responsiveness, spoken content quality, and voice consistency. To mitigate this degradation, we propose a self-distillation-based training framework for lightweight LoRA adaptation that improves long-session stability with a small amount of additional training. We further introduce a two-resolution context (TRC) architecture that preserves recent interactions in full detail while compressing older interaction history into key-value representations. We evaluate our methods on three models with distinct architectures, assessing long-session stability, information retention, and computational efficiency. Across all three models, we show that LoRA adaptation mitigates performance degradation over ten-minute sessions, while adding TRC extends these gains beyond the training session length and reduces KV-cache growth by more than 90%.

Abstract: Simultaneous speech translation (SimulST) aims to translate ongoing speech with low latency while preserving translation quality. Recent studies have adopted large language models (LLMs) for SimulST and formulated it as a multi-turn dialogue task for efficient generation, achieving state-of-the-art performance. However, existing dialogue-style SimulST approaches typically rely on specialized training schemes to acquire simultaneous translation behavior, either using synthesized read/write trajectories, which may provide suboptimal training signals, or jointly optimizing translation quality and latency, whose competing objectives may cause optimization instability. In this paper, we introduce SimPOTrans, a reinforcement learning (RL) framework that enables offline speech translation models to self-adapt to dialogue-style SimulST. Through a simple two-stage RL post-training pipeline, SimPOTrans rewards an offline model for exhibiting translation behaviors beneficial to simultaneous scenarios when given partial speech inputs, thereby enabling offline-to-simultaneous self-adaptation without synthesized read/write trajectories or explicit latency optimization. At inference time, SimPOTrans requires no external decision policy and enables flexible quality--latency control by simply adjusting the chunk size of speech input. Experiments on the ACL 60/60 and MCIF benchmarks across three language directions (En$\rightarrow$De, En$\rightarrow$Zh, and En$\rightarrow$Ja) demonstrate that SimPOTrans outperforms several strong baselines, achieving a superior quality--latency trade-off.

</details>

Abstract: Transportation agencies maintain extensive crash databases to identify safety priorities, evaluate countermeasures, and guide resource allocation. Making these data accessible to practitioners requires flexible ways to translate analytical questions into reliable results. Existing dashboards support predefined analyses, while additional requests often depend on specialists who understand database queries, coded fields, and agency conventions. This study presents CrashDB-QA, a fully local, schema-grounded agentic workflow that enables natural-language analysis of crash databases. Specialized components interpret questions, generate and validate read-only SQL, execute queries, and explain the results. A shared knowledge base grounds query construction in the agency's data dictionary and safety-analysis conventions, while recorded intermediate outputs support inspection and auditing. An optional extension, CrashDB-QA+, checks the alignment among the question, query, and execution result and supplies evidence for iterative repair. We evaluate the workflow on a frozen snapshot of 4.4 million Wisconsin crash records using a new benchmark of 300 questions across ten analytical categories and nine local open-weight models. Domain grounding substantially improves query accuracy, and a code-specialized 7B model provides the best accuracy-latency balance among the tested models. Compositional analyses remain more difficult than routine retrieval, particularly comparisons between groups or periods. Verification improves performance when the Judge uses authoritative field definitions and the Refiner receives execution evidence, whereas additional checking alone offers no consistent benefit. These findings demonstrate the potential of locally deployed, domain-grounded workflows to broaden access to crash analysis and identify the reasoning and verification capabilities needed for reliable use.

</details>

Abstract: Agent Skills allow LLM agents to combine reusable capabilities, but checking each Skill in isolation cannot rule out policy violations when they are composed. We term such failures Conflict of Skills (CoS): artifacts or assessment signals produced for one purpose may be reused downstream without preserving the restrictions or authorization needed for their new use. Using controlled executions of publicly available Skills and matched single-Skill controls, we identify four recurring mechanisms behind CoS. Building on these findings, we introduce the Conflict of Skills Attack (CoSA), which adds locally admitted Skills to an existing pool to supply missing business operations (capability supplementation) or reshape artifact exchange, decision reuse, and target/action bindings (relationship reshaping). CoSA leaves existing Skills and runtime permissions unchanged. To evaluate this threat under autonomous Skill selection, we introduce CoSA-Bench, built from real-world Skills and controlled services, with 288 tasks across recruiting, finance, IT, and business, covering data leakage, unauthorized state modification, and unsafe execution. Across five LLMs and three agent harnesses, additions capped at 5% of each original pool raise the policy-violation rate from 35.02% to 58.45%, an increase of 23.43 percentage points. Trace analysis links compliant-to-violating transitions to cross-Skill dependencies involving the additions. Finally, we develop a lightweight harness-level guard that checks information flow and authorization before consequential actions. On OpenClaw recruiting tasks across five models, the guard reduces violations from 65.28% to 6.67%, while policy-compliant task completion on matched benign controls changes from 66.67% to 65.00%.

</details>

We propose QERA, a Question-driven Evidence Reasoning Architecture that asks before it answers. QERA first turns the request into a requirement checklist and typed information needs, filtered by code for faithfulness to the user's words; it then answers from the whole conversation, asks the same questions again with quotes verified by code, and applies a guarded local repair. It needs no training or rubrics and makes about four LLM calls per task. On all 405 tasks with four frontier LLMs, QERA attains the highest solving rate on every LLM (21.0% on average, against 18.2% for the strongest of three inference-time baselines and 14.6% for direct answering) and leads every baseline significantly when pooled across LLMs, while using fewer tokens than PSCI and 4-5% of the tokens of Ctx2Skill. Abstract: Large language models operationalize an intuitive, view of meaning as use. We argue that it faces a rule-following challenge. Using Kripke's interpretation of Wittgenstein, we argue that finite histories of use do not determine a unique rule for future extension. This reframes the debate about OOD generalization but also about NLP evaluation; benchmarks that measure performance on fixed use cases or local robustness, we argue, do not by themselves establish semantic competence. To evaluate whether models preserve rules and concepts under principled domain extension, we propose to evaluate two capacities, central to this ability: abstraction and recursion; and we offer an experimental paradigm for testing both.

Abstract: Multilingual transformer models are often assumed to generalise across languages, but their effectiveness for coreference resolution in low-resource settings remains unverified. We investigate this gap in Bengali, a language with over 250 million speakers but limited annotated coreference resources. We evaluate transformer-based pre-trained encoders under multiple settings, including zero-shot, fine-tuning, data augmentation, and cross-lingual transfer. Our results challenge several common assumptions about model generalisation and data efficiency in low-resource NLP. We demonstrate that a small amount of high-quality annotated data significantly outperforms larger mixed-quality datasets (p < 0.01). We find that data augmentation, particularly back-translation, does not improve performance and can degrade results in coreference resolution. Cross-lingual transfer is also inconsistent: transfer from a distant language like English reduces performance, and transfer from the related language Hindi provides no consistent benefit across models. Several widely used multilingual models exhibit collapsed embedding spaces, rendering them unsuitable for similarity-based clustering tasks. These findings highlight the importance of annotation quality over data quantity and provide empirical guidance for developing coreference resolution systems in low-resource languages.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) often produce plausible but incorrect answers, motivating reinforcement learning methods that reward correct answers, penalize incorrect answers, and allow abstention. However, fixed outcome-based rewards do not distinguish incorrect responses with different confidence levels or account for differences in answering reliability across queries. Consequently, such rewards may inadequately penalize overconfident errors and fail to discourage unnecessary abstention. We propose CalibraRL, a reinforcement learning framework that incorporates response confidence and query-level answering evidence into reward design. For non-abstaining responses, CalibraRL combines correctness-based utility with a Brier-based penalty that encourages agreement between confidence and correctness. For abstentions, it estimates conditional answering reliability using a Beta posterior derived from the correctness of non-abstaining responses within each sampled group. The abstention reward increases with the posterior probability that the model's accuracy on non-abstaining responses is below a predefined threshold. CalibraRL reuses training rollouts without requiring additional annotations indicating whether the model knows the answer to each question. Experiments on three QA datasets demonstrate that CalibraRL improves answer precision and reduces hallucinations compared with strong baselines. Further analysis shows that CalibraRL reduces mean expected calibration error (ECE) by 30.8\% across two backbone models and three datasets, highlighting its potential to improve the reliability of retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) in web search.

</details>

Abstract: The value of language models for any community depends on whether a model actually understands the culture of the people it intends to serve. We use the Igbo language as a case study. Existing benchmarks test translated Western ideas, lacking depth. To address this, we introduce CAB-Igbo, a benchmark developed from indigenous cultural sources and verified by native speakers across six domains. We evaluated diverse models under varying conditions, incorporating regional personas. Performance was assessed using multiple-choice accuracy and human evaluations across Epistemic Fidelity, Representational Richness, Linguistic Quality, and Pragmatic Proficiency. Results reveal a stark dissociation between language fluency and cultural knowledge. Through statistical testing, we show that for most models, the severe lack of Epistemic Fidelity and Representational Richness is already present in English and does not significantly worsen when switching to Igbo. Instead, native language prompting mainly degrades Linguistic Quality and Pragmatic Proficiency. Gemini 2.0 Flash is the only model whose cultural knowledge scores improve under Igbo prompting. We find that current models struggle to demonstrate the knowledge needed for true alignment with Igbo, and multiple-choice formats allow pattern-matching to appear as understanding. Furthermore, automated judges agree with humans on linguistic quality but fail on epistemic fidelity. Ultimately, cultural evaluation must be built from community knowledge, establishing human evaluation as the gold standard to prevent misrepresenting low-resource communities globally.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used for codebook-based content analysis in computational social science, where codebooks translate theoretical concepts into labeling schemes. While prior work reports strong predictive performance, it remains unclear whether these systems produce reliable and robust text annotations. We conduct a large-scale empirical failure-mode analysis of LLM-based coding pipelines across four datasets and five models by systematically evaluating their stability, robustness, and conceptual validity under controlled perturbations, domain shifts, and optimization procedures. We uncover multiple failure modes that are largely invisible under standard metrics, showing that high aggregate scores frequently coexist with severe label-level disparities, instability across runs, domain sensitivity, and shifts in label interpretation. Importantly, these failure modes persist under widely used optimization strategies and remain largely invisible under standard metrics such as accuracy and micro-F1. Our results demonstrate that current LLM-based coding pipelines cannot be treated as stable measurement tools without targeted validation beyond aggregate performance.

Abstract: Given that the Chinese are one of the oldest ethnic groups to immigrate to the US, their cuisine have become central to the US dining scene and an interesting avenue to study how Chinese immigrants have culturally adapted into American life. In that regard, previous research has focused on analyzing the English names that Chinese restaurants adopt, e.g., finding the use of familiar and typical words. To investigate how Chinese immigrants have culturally integrated in the US depending on social, geographic and demographic contexts, we investigate in this work the under-explored relationship between the Chinese and English names of American Chinese restaurants. Specifically, we conduct a study on 3,172 Chinese restaurants (10% of the total on Google Maps) sampled across the US. We present 8-dimension name frames to capture the underlying meanings behind names and create the NameFrames dataset containing 884 annotated parallel Chinese/English names. We also built an NLP pipeline to analyze the semantic discrepancies between names with both frame-based and embedding-based distance metrics. Our frame analysis shows that English names more often emphasize Location and Specialty, while Chinese names more often emphasize Positivity and Ambiance. Furthermore, we observe that for Chinese restaurants located in urban areas, those in counties with a higher concentration of Chinese population, or those that are relatively new, the discrepancies between the English and Chinese names are noticeably larger. Finally, interviews with 10 Chinese restaurant owners reveal additional interesting insights regarding naming conventions.

Abstract: Diffusion language models (DLMs) generate text through iterative denoising, enabling bidirectional context modeling and parallel token refinement. Recent studies have applied reinforcement learning (RL) to DLMs and improved their reasoning performance. However, the iterative denoising process makes credit assignment more challenging, as final outcome rewards provide limited information about how different tokens contribute to the final output. In this paper, we propose Denoising Token Credit Assignment (DTCA), a framework for token-level credit assignment in reinforcement learning for diffusion language models. DTCA estimates token contributions by measuring the improvement in prediction confidence of remaining masked positions after token commitment and groups these signals according to part-of-speech (POS) categories. These contribution weights are used to select informative denoising timesteps and assign token-level learning signals during RL training. DTCA further calibrates these learning signals according to token-level entropy changes induced by policy updates. Experiments on LLaDA-8B-Instruct show that DTCA consistently improves mathematical reasoning performance across different generation budgets, with gains of up to 9.0 points on MATH500 and 5.3 points on GSM8K over the base model.

</details>

Abstract: The number of paper submissions to major AI conferences has increased dramatically in recent years, substantially increasing the peer-review workload of researchers. At the same time, large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used to support peer review, both by individual reviewers and by conference organizations. While LLM capabilities have improved considerably, their evaluations may be influenced by linguistic characteristics that are unrelated to scientific content. In this work, we introduce a controlled experimental framework for studying such effects by systematically perturbing the linguistic style of research papers. Specifically, we rewrite papers to exhibit (i) linguistic features associated with native or non-native English writing, and (ii) high- or low-confidence linguistic features. To construct these interventions, we derive fine-grained rewriting instructions grounded in the linguistic literature and apply them using LLMs. Crucially, our interventions are designed to preserve the underlying scientific content. We then evaluate the resulting papers using different LLM reviewers. Our experiments expose a critical vulnerability in LLM-based peer review: reviewers systematically reward confident rhetoric independent of scientific content, raising serious concerns about the robustness of their assessments.

</details>

Abstract: Idioms have long been a challenge for machine translation, and while large language models (LLMs) have proven increasingly adept at handling them, whether quality estimation (QE) systems have kept pace and can reliably score idiomatic renderings of these expressions above faulty literal ones remains unclear. We introduce the first parallel dataset pairing idiom-bearing English sentences with both literal and idiomatic translations across nine target languages, augmented into multi-sentence segments and a graded literality continuum. Using this dataset, we benchmark eleven conventional QE metrics from the COMET and MetricX families and ten LLMs from the Qwen-3, Gemma-4 and OLMo-3 families used as GEMBA-style judges. We find that most systems, especially older metrics and smaller models, display a marked literality bias, rating literal translations of idioms above idiomatic ones. Higher parameter counts and newer training sets appear to reduce this bias to an extent; nevertheless, of all the benchmarked systems, only MetricX-24-Hybrid (XL, XXL) and Gemma-4-31B consistently rate idiomatic translations of idioms above literal ones, and only Gemma-4-31B reliably ranks intermediate degrees of idiomaticity and literality. Our findings caution against deploying standard QE metrics in domains rich in figurative language, where higher scores might be rewarding undesirable literalism.

</details>

Abstract: Lean verifies that a formal proof establishes its stated proposition under declared assumptions, but does not check whether that proposition faithfully expresses the mathematical claim in a manuscript. Assessing this correspondence requires interpreting the main theorem through its project dependencies, which may be scattered throughout a large development. We introduce MechAlign, a framework for project-level semantic alignment review that combines formal dependency extraction with LLM-based assessment. Its extraction tool, LeanTrace, constructs a statement dependency graph from elaborated declarations, retaining definition bodies and structure constraints while excluding proof bodies and stopping at an explicit library boundary. The reviewer uses this context to investigate alignment and report discrepancies with localized evidence. We conduct a preliminary review of 201 main results from 173 OpenAI Math projects, using supplied informal descriptions and available paper context. A hyperbolic-group case illustrates how dependency inspection localizes a missing dimensional constraint relative to the manuscript, consistent with the upstream declaration of formalization scope. We also construct LeanReviewBench, comprising 501 fixed claim-statement-context triples from OpenAI Math, Fermat's Last Theorem, and Kakeya, with description provenance recorded. The resource supports controlled comparisons of more project-level semantic alignment evaluations.

Abstract: A fundamental challenge for explainable and accountable language models is revealing how external information influences their answers. This paper examines whether models are self-attributive: that is, whether they indicate when -- and only when -- their responses to questions are influenced by information provided alongside the question. Furthermore, we explore how self-attribution can be improved through counterfactual training, which explicitly teaches models to distinguish between answers that do and do not depend on supplied information.
  We found that most of the eleven frontier models rarely attribute influential information in their answers, and the rest attribute non-influential information. Training acknowledgment into reasoning does not fix this: the acknowledgment does not reach the final answer.
  We introduce CREDIT, which directly supervises final-answer self-attribution with counterfactual labels, without the need for human annotation. CREDIT substantially improves attribution in four open models, including two distilled reasoning models, and outperforms reasoning-level fine-tuning and prompting baselines. Among answers that agree with a user's suggestion, CREDIT distinguishes those changed by the suggestion from those the model would have given anyway. Gains transfer partly to unseen information types and prompts containing sources that support conflicting answers.

</details>

Abstract: Constrained decoding guarantees that Large Language Models (LLMs) produce outputs conforming to predefined formats, but is often assumed to inherently degrade reasoning. We examine this assumption through a taxonomy of three constraint types: \emph{format constraints}, which enforce output structure; \emph{validity constraints}, which mask invalid answer; and \emph{reasoning constraints}, which restrict reasoning trajectories. We theoretically show that appropriate constraints can preserve or improve performance by eliminating invalid paths. Across four model families and eleven open-source models, causal ablations on diverse benchmarks using state-of-the-art constrained decoding strategies reveal that apparent performance degradation largely stems from non-equivalent prompting and output formats rather than from decoder-level masking. Meanwhile, validity constraints improve reliability and extractability by eliminating invalid answers, while reasoning constraints can improve reasoning and, consequently, accuracy when they enforce beneficial trajectories. These findings show that the impact of constrained decoding depends on the type of constraint and the output space it removes. Our code is available.

</details>

The results are conditional on article pages that could still be retrieved, and the correspondence subsets do not isolate the effect of input differences. Reconstructed records should therefore be assessed field by field and not treated as completely interchangeable with native ones.

Abstract: Operator manuals of agricultural implements are delivered in many languages, and the standards require that competent persons review every translation, which makes this review the bottleneck. This paper presents a proof of concept that compares two training workflows on the same LLM, EuroLLM-1.7B-Instruct, trained on operator manuals of one manufacturer. In approach A, a back-translator translates each language version back into English, and an English reader compares it with the English version. In approach B, a checker reads the English version and the translation together, where the checker reports differences. The test sets contain errors planted in numbers, units, opposite words, obligations, negations, and conditions in 24 languages, and they include unmodified text and meaning-preserving edits for alerts and false alarms. The trained back-translator concealed many planted errors, most of all weakened obligations, while translation-quality scores rose. In contrast, the separately trained checker detected most planted errors, although it was weak for units and for number changes unlike those in training. A check of alerts on real Finnish translations showed that most alerts were false, caused by language-specific wording, so the precision on real text remains a limitation. Within this setting, a trained checker might support, not replace, the review by competent persons, and firmer conclusions need more data.

Abstract: Ambiguity in prescriptive text such as laws, contracts, rules, policies, or recommendations can be both desirable (e.g., if it enables a law to adapt to changing context, such as new technologies) and undesirable (e.g., if it leads to arbitrary discretion or conflict over the law's interpretation). To help navigate these trade-offs, it would be valuable to have methods for quantifying ambiguity that are predictive of such outcomes, but existing measures capture how text is perceived in isolation rather than how it is interpreted in context. In this paper, we propose quantifying ambiguity as the degree of disagreement among a set of stakeholders ("raters") on how alternative prescriptive texts ("policies") apply in each of a set of relevant scenarios ("cases"). Using two policy domains, we present preliminary analysis suggesting that, even when using simulated raters, this approach (i) correlates with real human disagreement, and (ii) is robust to spurious text features. We then apply the measure to existing deliberation datasets to describe the association between policy ambiguity and support for those policies.

Abstract: Both models and minds not only encode features that describe an entity, but can specifically represent which features of that entity are relevant for a given context. This core capability underlies in-context learning in language models and a variety of cognitive phenomena in minds, yet it is unclear how intelligent systems identify and promote relevant features. In this work, we propose Amortized Direction Accentuation (ADA), an algorithmic hypothesis that provides an explanation for how relevance is determined in the context of few-shot concept induction --- the ability to infer a latent concept from a small number of positive examples. Under ADA, concept induction proceeds by forming a summary representation of the examples in a feature space that is shaped by amortized knowledge about which features tend to be useful for finding commonalities between items. We investigate and validate ADA in language models using a few-shot concept induction task. This work provides a novel perspective on the mechanisms supporting contextualization in language models and a promising hypothesis for a core aspect of cognition.

Abstract: Looped Transformers offer a parameter-efficient approach to language modeling by repeatedly applying a shared backbone. Vanilla looped Transformers use each backbone output directly as the input to the next iteration. These outputs may contain information useful several iterations later, but retaining it across the loop depends entirely on the single hidden state. We introduce StateLoop, which maintains one additional persistent state per token to retain information across iterations. At each iteration, a learned gate controls how much of the previous state is retained and how much information from the backbone output is incorporated. The updated state is projected back and added to the backbone output for subsequent iterations and final prediction. This design reduces Pile perplexity and improves average accuracy across eight zero-shot tasks over Vanilla Looped. Its perplexity advantage over Vanilla Looped grows with trained loop depth, consistent with persistent state becoming increasingly useful as recurrent computation deepens.

</details>

Abstract: Looped Transformers increase computation per token by repeatedly applying a shared backbone, offering a way to improve language modeling and downstream task performance at a fixed backbone parameter count. However, each loop depends on the preceding loop, so increasing recurrent depth also increases decoding latency. Existing approaches include Parallel Loop Transformers, which reduce latency by overlapping loop computation across token positions, but yield rapidly diminishing marginal accuracy gains at larger depths. To address this challenge, we introduce LoopFold, which folds computation at multiple recurrent depths into parallel batches for the same token prefix. A lightweight predictor estimates inputs to later loops so they can start before earlier loops finish. A corrector then refines the outputs of these later loops using newly available results from earlier loops. All components are trained jointly using only the final next-token prediction loss. In experiments with 1.4B models, deeper LoopFold configurations achieve progressively higher average zero-shot accuracy across eight tasks, rising from 53.41% to 56.38%. Vanilla looped models and PLT show limited gains over the same depth range. Compared with a nine-loop vanilla model, LoopFold uses five sequential stages and additional parallel computation to reduce decoding latency by 46%.

</details>

Abstract: Language models are increasingly considered for clinical decision support, where their answers should be grounded in clinical evidence and remain robust to irrelevant information. We find that blocking attention to a known distractor restores the answer, whereas head masking can suppress correct answers. To remove a distractor's effect without knowing which sentence it is, we propose EXCISE, an inference-time method that blocks the sentence most influencing the answer, but only if the model's hidden states mark it as inconsistent with the case. Abstract: Modeling the latent, multi-step reasoning processes of learners is a core challenge for adaptive tutoring systems, which often rely on simple right/wrong answers rather than understanding how students think. Instantiated here for grammatical reasoning, this study introduces a diagnostic framework that makes students' step-by-step problem-solving visible by analyzing their action sequences in an interactive learning game. The framework computes five interpretable strategy dimensions (e.g., systematic error correction, random guessing), estimates coordination demand from task structure and behavioral patterns, and classifies reasoning into four distinct states. Analyzing 36,234 interactions from 234 students working on 42 morphosyntactic exercises, we found that reasoning strategies vary primarily by context rather than stable traits (intraclass correlation = 0.14), and students show high persistence in unproductive states; specifically, 74.6% of failures driven by high coordination demand (rather than simple knowledge gaps) are followed by another failure without intervention. Our early warning model predicts validation success with strong accuracy (AUC = 0.826 using strategy patterns alone; 0.902 with additional sequential context) while avoiding circular reliance on current error counts. These findings demonstrate that making latent reasoning processes visible enables earlier, more targeted scaffolding that responds to how students are thinking rather than simply whether they succeed. While demonstrated in language learning, the framework provides a generalizable, interpretable architecture for diagnosing complex, structured reasoning in any domain.

Abstract: Skills provide agents with reusable experience to guide future decisions without modifying the underlying model. However, coding trajectories are often entangled with task- and repository-specific contexts, making it challenging to distill procedural skills that generalize across diverse coding environments. To address this challenge, we present Hierarchical Skill Evolution (HSE), which progressively distills procedural knowledge from coding trajectories through hierarchical evidence aggregation. HSE first extracts task-level evidence from individual trajectories, then aggregates recurring evidence within the same repository to form repository-specific skill candidates, and finally consolidates consistent patterns across repositories into transferable skills across repositories. The evolution is driven by an external writer-evaluator loop that generates and assesses skills while keeping the underlying coding model frozen. The loop externally updates the writer and evaluator policies to improve subsequent skill generation and assessment. This hierarchy progressively filters task-specific details while preserving procedures that generalize across diverse coding environments. Successful trajectories yield reusable guidance for future tasks, while failed trajectories reveal recurring failure modes that guide avoidance and recovery. We evaluate HSE on SWE-bench Verified and DeepSWE, using GLM-5.2 across Pi and Claude Code harnesses and GPT-5.5 and Opus 4.8 with Pi. Across both benchmarks, 10 skills evolved solely from SWE-Gym improve coding performance across all evaluated settings, demonstrating transfer across benchmarks, coding models, and agent harnesses. We further evolve the skill library from target-benchmark trajectories without verifier feedback, relying solely on the learned evaluator for candidate assessment and achieving additional gains through benchmark-tailored skills.

</details>

Abstract: An offline language-based support tool is presented to assist pilot training and tactical decision-making in air combat operations. Built entirely with open-source components, it performs text preprocessing, embedding generation, semantic search, and language generation locally, ensuring full offline functionality. Average ratings ranged from 4.00 to 4.40 on a five-point scale, with Mistral NeMo 12B obtaining the highest score, and the pilots rated the response time of the tool as fully adequate compared to manual search. The relevance of the retrieved content was identified as the main point for improvement. Potential applications are discussed in onboard mission systems, flight simulators, and training environments.

Abstract: JEV decision models pair a language model backbone with a lightweight scoring head to select among natural-language options. Direct selection compares candidate answers within one request, whereas entailment reranking evaluates each candidate in a separate natural language inference (NLI) request over fixed relation options. We examine whether the readout and adaptation choices used for direct selection remain effective for cross-request reranking. We combine a mean-span readout with selective backbone adaptation. Mean-span pooling replaces the option-ending pointer with an average of the contextualized states inside each NLI option, without adding head parameters. Selective adaptation retains low-rank updates in attention and gated DeltaNet projections while removing feed-forward adapters. On MC11, which averages accuracy over eleven reranking configurations from seven sources, selective adaptation improves NeoHorse-1-4B accuracy over the all-module baseline while cutting trainable parameters by more than half, and mean-span pooling yields further consistent gains at the same parameter count.

Abstract: As frontier language models grow increasingly capable and autonomous, independent third-party evaluations are critical for characterizing their systemic safety risks. While closed-source releases are routinely accompanied by comprehensive system cards, open-weight models receive less scrutiny regarding frontier risks. To address this gap, we present an independent safety evaluation of DeepSeek V4.1 Flash-1017, the latest version of V4.1 Flash that departs from V4 with a new architecture, scaled-up training, and strong agentic capabilities. Drawing on the EU General-Purpose AI Code of Practice, we evaluate the model across 34 benchmarks, including 5 in-house suites, spanning evaluation integrity, loss of control, and harmful manipulation. Conforming to the AEF-1 standard, we compare V4.1 Flash-1017 against V4.1 Flash, earlier DeepSeek V4 models, and leading closed-source systems (GPT-6 Astra, GPT-5.6 Sol, and Gemini 3.8 Flash). Overall, V4.1 Flash-1017 shows substantial safety improvements over the V4 generation and approaches or matches leading closed-source baselines on several dimensions, while still exhibiting notable behaviors that warrant continued attention. Building on our findings, we highlight three key observations and recommendations. First, because safety can shift and even regress across minor updates, developers should integrate safety evaluation into incremental releases. Second, as final-turn outputs and end-state checks alone cannot reliably detect loss-of-control behaviors, deployments require process-level observability across both reasoning traces and action logs. Third, because public benchmarks elicit strong evaluation awareness and face data contamination risks, developing private benchmarks is essential for trustworthy frontier evaluation.

</details>

Abstract: For large language models (LLMs) and multimodal large language models (MLLMs), model-reported self-confidence is often miscalibrated, yet it may still be useful for efficient evaluation. For direct auditing, confidence-stratified sampling is used to recover population accuracy from partially verified outputs without requiring calibrated confidence. Finite-sample uncertainty is characterized, and the analysis shows how highly uneven confidence strata affect audit efficiency and label allocation. For judge-assisted evaluation, the judge is modeled through sensitivity and false-positive rate, yielding an error-corrected estimator with calibration uncertainty. The two routes are then compared through human-label and total-cost efficiency boundaries.
  Experiments on MMLU-Pro and MMMU-Pro show that miscalibrated self-confidence can still support statistically valid stratified auditing. Under highly imbalanced confidence distributions, proportional allocation substantially reduces estimation error relative to equal-per-stratum allocation. For judge-assisted evaluation, only a small subset of target--verifier pairs satisfies the idealized human-label efficiency condition, and automated inference costs can further limit potential savings. These findings clarify the statistical and economic conditions under which confidence-guided auditing and automated verification are useful.

Abstract: Existing jailbreak attackers fall into two classes: static planners fix their single- or multi-turn adversarial plans in advance, remaining simple but losing sight of the victim's replies, wasting turns and limiting transferability; interactive generators read each victim reply to generate the next-turn attack but require in-loop evaluators for success signals or hints, incurring substantial costs and inflated performance. Bridging this gap, we propose SEMA-Auto, a sighted and efficient multi-turn attacker with autonomy, interacting with the victim model and terminating the session by itself upon success. To stabilize its behaviors and boost its capabilities, SEMA-Auto is trained in two stages. Synthetic bootstrap tuning installs the notion of attack targets and conforming decisions by fine-tuning the base model with bootstrapped trajectories. Decision-supervised reinforcement learning then optimizes two objectives jointly yet separately, reinforcing the attack with higher jailbreak reward while supervising the decision at each turn against an absolute target. Our method achieves state-of-the-art (SOTA) attack success rates (ASR) across multiple datasets, victims, and judges, outperforming all static and interactive baselines, within almost the fewest turns, demonstrating both effectiveness and efficiency. For example, SEMA-Auto performs a $76.0\%$ ASR@1 within an average of $3.0$ turns against DeepSeek-V4-Flash on AdvBench, while the best static baseline is $54.8\%$ with fixed $7$ turns and the best interactive baseline is $43.8\%$ in $3.7$ turns. Against safety-hardened frontier models, GPT-oss-120B, GPT-5.4, Claude-Haiku-4.5, and GPT-6-Astra, our counter-trained attacker achieves an average $59.6\%$ ASR@1 on HarmBench, $13.8\%$ over prior SOTA. Abstract: Deep research, or any use of large language models (LLMs) to read a large document collection and produce insights, requires synthesizing information from different sources. In this work, we find that LLMs produce long-form research reports that read as confident syntheses but which fail to accurately reflect the underlying documents. We call this failure smoothing. In collapsing diverse and potentially conflicting sources into a single narrative, the model discards the evidential "jaggedness" of its corpus and asserts conclusions more decisive than the citations can support. We introduce a claim level scoring pipeline, SmoothScore, to quantify this effect. Our pipeline extracts key conclusive claims from model generated reports, then finds evidence relevant to each claim and clusters that evidence along high level aspects. Finally, each claim is scored with respect to the evidence to quantify the degree of smoothing done by the agent. Across 7 systems and 4 datasets, we find that agents consistently produce stronger claims than warranted.

</details>

Abstract: Diffusion large language models (dLLMs) can generate multiple tokens in parallel, but often require many decoding rounds to complete a response. Efficient generation depends not only on which tokens are easy to predict, but also on how committing to them helps predict the remaining positions. A highly predictable token may provide little useful context, while revealing a less predictable token can help several other positions become predictable sooner. We propose dSpark, a new schedule distillation-based framework for learning when to commit tokens for faster diffusion language generation. To achieve this, we construct unmasking schedules that prioritize tokens whose reveal makes other positions easier to predict, reducing uncertainty earlier in decoding. We then distill these schedules into the model to enable faster parallel sampling, with a KL-divergence regularizer to help preserve generation quality. We extensively evaluate our approach across mathematical reasoning and code generation tasks. On GSM8K, our method achieves remarkable 10.12$\times$ and 5.12$\times$ speedups over the various baselines on LLaDA-8B-Instruct and Dream-7B-Instruct, respectively, while maintaining superior task performance. Our code is available at https://github.com/VILA-Lab/dSpark.

</details>


### [388] [Understanding Jev-Style System One Models: Does Structure Reduce Selection Bias?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35826)
*Mingyang Song,Mao Zheng,Zhiyuan Ma,Xinpeng Zhou*

Main category: cs.CL

Abstract: Selection bias in large language models (LLMs) can persist despite instructions to ignore presentation order or prompts that elicit reasoning. Recent efforts to reproduce Jev-style System One behavior have produced a growing family of models that predict probabilities directly over supplied candidates. Yet systematic comparisons of selection bias across Jev versions, Jev reproductions, and generative LLMs remain limited in multi-option tasks. This leaves a central question: does Jev-style decision structure reduce selection bias? We address this question with a simple test that shuffles options while keeping question and candidate content fixed. Across two Jev versions, twelve Jev reproductions, and six generative LLMs, we trace individual answers through eight presentations and compare them with fixed-order repetition. On our primary collection of 1,700 questions from two benchmarks, Jev's accuracy varies by less than two percentage points, yet its cumulative failure set expands by 27--29\% relative to the original order, with first failures continuing into the final presentation. This loss of initial successes is substantially larger than under fixed-order repetition, even for highly repeatable Jev reproductions. Nor does Jev show a general advantage over the LLM baselines in preserving correct choices. Aggregation offers limited protection: voting yields modest gains for Jev, while even unanimous answers are frequently wrong. These results expose a gap between repeatable decisions and decisions that remain correct across equivalent presentations, showing why stable accuracy alone cannot establish resistance to selection bias.

</details>


### [389] [Preserve to Expand: Source-Aware Adaptation and Model Merging for Multilingual LLM-Based ASR](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35846)
*Yuchen Zhang,Haralambos Mouratidis,Ravi Shekhar*

Main category: cs.CL

Abstract: Expanding multilingual ASR systems to new languages is challenging when adaptation must preserve previously supported languages without replaying source-language training data. We study this problem in connector-based Speech LLMs, where a trainable connector maps speech representations into a frozen large language model. We evaluate target-update construction and merging strategies for adapting the connector while preserving source-language performance. In single-target expansion, target-only fine-tuning and standard TIES merging learn new languages but cause severe source-language forgetting, while SSU-Wanda+TIES gives the best source-preserving trade-off. In multi-target expansion, update merging provides stable source retention, whereas recursive expansion can improve target adaptation under favorable orders but is highly order-sensitive. These results show that multilingual ASR expansion requires balancing target acquisition, source retention, and interactions among target-language updates.
